  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

No MPAA trigger status, income tax band or annual allowance is explicitly recorded in the fact find or any other supporting document in the pack. The transaction history [P3][P6] shows regular pension charges and fund sales but contains no tax status information. No separate tax assessment, HMRC document or MPAA notification is present in the pack.
    The report's sole tax-status assertion relevant to this requirement is that the MPAA has not been triggered and the client does not have a lower annual allowance. The case file contains no document that either corroborates or contradicts this assertion: the fact find records domicile and residency as United Kingdom but is silent on MPAA status, tax band and annual allowance. The income tax band and annual allowance figure are absent from both the report and the file, but those omissions are extraction gaps noted in expectedButAbsent and are not findings against the report on their own. The 35-year tenure is unmatched in any passage but is contextual rather than financially material. The Yvette income discrepancy (£28,000 report vs £16,800 cashflow) is the most significant finding as it could affect affordability calculations and cashflow modelling reliability. The guard about different bases does not explain this gap — both figures purport to be Yvette's annual employment income.

    Two material discrepancies require attention. First, the report contains an internal inconsistency on expenditure: the 'Your current situation' narrative states ~£2,493/month essential expenditure, but the cashflow modelling section of the same report states ~£2,206/month, a difference of £287/month (~11.5%). The Fact Find [P1] records £2,206.67 as the calculated total monthly household expenditure, corroborating the cashflow figure and contradicting the narrative figure. No explanation is given for why the narrative figure is higher. This is a material internal inconsistency because the cashflow projections — which underpin the advice — are built on £2,206, not £2,493, and no reconciliation is provided. Second, the cashflow [P2] shows 'Money In' of £38,800 in 2025 (age 55/59). This equals £22,000 (DB pension) + £16,800 (Yvette 3-day pro-rata from [P9]), yet in 2025 Yvette is described as still working full-time at £28,000 p.a. The reduction to 3 days is stated as an intention 'from next year'. If the cashflow already uses the reduced salary in 2025, it understates household income for the current year, which affects the cashflow baseline. The Yvette reduced income calc document [P9] supports the £16,800 figure used in the cashflow but the report's own narrative places the reduction in the future. The £2,200 target retirement income is corroborated by [P18] and [P24]. The £1,700 net/month DB pension and £22,000 gross are corroborated by [P11] and [P2]. The £890 net driving income is corroborated by [P11]/[P19], and the cessation is recorded in the report. Anthony's gross annual earnings being recorded as £14,000 in [P11] reflects the annualised driving income before cessation; this is consistent with the part-time role described. The assets total of £669,092 arithmetic is internally consistent but the balance sheet omits separate disclosure of the GIA and ISA assets; this is a disclosure gap but not an arithmetic error. The £0.67 difference between £2,206 (report) and £2,206.67 (fact find) is within rounding tolerance and not a finding. The gross/net guard is respected throughout — no cross-basis comparisons have been made.

  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   Main residence at 27 Reigate Road, Urmston, Manchester, mortgage-free, valued at £400,000. Total liquid assets £190,000 comprising: £100,000 inheritance from late father's estate (due this month), £40,000 Chartered savings account, £20,000 Cash ISA (Mr Barnes), £20,000 Yvette's Cash ISA with Moneybox, £10,000 emergency fund. The Assets & Liabilities table shows Cash & Savings £190,000 and Personal Pension (Aviva Platform) £79,092, NET Asset Value £669,092. No liabilities (£0). The cashflow modelling opening values for the Aviva ISA are £20,000, and the GIA opening value is £52,326.
    File:     Fact find [P18] records assets for Anthony Barnes totalling £590,000: house mortgage-free £400,000, incoming funds from sale of Dad's property £100,000, Chartered Savings Account £40,000, MoneyBox Cash ISA 4.5% £20,000, Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA £20,000, Cash Emergency Fund £10,000. No liabilities recorded. Fact find [P11] confirms emergency fund £10,000, total monthly expenditure £2,206.67. Cashflow modelling [P6] shows opening values: Chartered Savings Account £40,000, Aviva ISA £20,000, Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA £20,000, Aviva GIA £52,326, Cash Emergency Fund £10,000, Aviva Platform Personal Pension £82,692. Total savings & investments opening value £142,326. The fact find [P18] records the Cash ISA in the name of Anthony Barnes only ('MoneyBox Cash ISA 4.5%'), not separately as Mr Barnes's Cash ISA, and the Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA is also recorded under Anthony Barnes as owner. The suitability report's Assets & Liabilities table shows Cash & Savings as a single £190,000 line and does not itemise the GIA or the ISA separately in the balance sheet, though these appear in existing arrangements sections.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report's Assets & Liabilities table shows Cash & Savings at £190,000 but the cashflow modelling opening values [P6] show total savings & investments of £142,326 — a difference of £47,674. This is partly explained by the Aviva GIA (£52,326) being treated as an investment product rather than cash, but the report's Assets & Liabilities table does not include the GIA at all under either cash or investments (only Main Residence, Cash & Savings, and Personal Pension Aviva Platform £79,092 are listed). The GIA value of approximately £57,120 (per the report's own existing arrangements section) or £52,326 (per the cashflow model opening value) is absent from the Assets & Liabilities balance sheet.
      - The Aviva GIA is stated in the suitability report's existing arrangements as £57,120 (used as the basis for the 3% initial charge calculation: £57,120 at 3% is part of the £4,794 total) but the cashflow modelling opening value for the Aviva GIA is £52,326 [P6] — a difference of £4,794. This difference equals the initial advice fee of £4,794, suggesting the cashflow model may have deducted the fee from the GIA opening value, but this is not explicitly stated and creates a factual discrepancy between the two figures as presented.
      - The suitability report's Assets & Liabilities table includes only three asset lines (Main Residence £400,000, Cash & Savings £190,000, Personal Pension Aviva Platform £79,092) totalling £669,092. The GIA (£57,120 per existing arrangements), Stocks & Shares ISA (£20,000 per existing arrangements and cashflow model), and the new products are not shown in the balance sheet, making the net asset value of £669,092 an incomplete picture of total assets.
      - The narrative states the £100,000 inheritance was 'due this month', implying it had not yet been received at time of the fact-find (August 2025), yet the £190,000 liquid assets figure in both the narrative and the Assets & Liabilities table includes it at full value. The fact find [P18] also records it at £100,000 as of 06/08/2025 as 'incoming funds from sale of Dad's property'. There is no clarification in the report as to whether it had been received by the report date (16/12/2025).
      - The fact find [P18] records the MoneyBox Cash ISA under Anthony Barnes as owner, not Yvette, though described as 'Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA'. The suitability report attributes the £20,000 Moneybox Cash ISA to C2 (Yvette). This ownership ambiguity is present in both documents but the report treats it as Yvette's asset while the fact find records it under Anthony Barnes.
      - The cashflow modelling [P6] shows the Aviva Platform Personal Pension opening value as £82,692, whereas the suitability report's existing arrangements and Assets & Liabilities table state £79,092 (and the canonical model records £79,092.32). The difference of approximately £3,600 corresponds to the recommended new pension contribution of £3,600, suggesting the cashflow model may reflect the post-contribution value, but this is not stated explicitly and the two figures are inconsistent as presented.
    Several discrepancies exist between the assets stated in the suitability report and the supporting evidence. The most material are: (1) The GIA (valued at £57,120 in the report's own existing arrangements section) is entirely absent from the Assets & Liabilities balance sheet, making the net asset value of £669,092 materially incomplete. The GIA should increase the net asset figure by approximately £57,120. This is an omission from the balance sheet that is internal to the report itself — no external document is needed to establish it. (2) The Aviva GIA value differs between the existing arrangements section (£57,120) and the cashflow model opening value (£52,326) [P6], a difference of £4,794 which matches the initial advice fee. The report does not state that the cashflow model starts post-fee, so this is an unexplained inconsistency between two sections of the report/case file. (3) The Aviva Platform Personal Pension opening value in the cashflow model (£82,692) differs from the existing arrangement value stated throughout the report (£79,092). The £3,600 difference matches the recommended pension contribution; again, no explanation is given. These differences are material because the assets and cashflow modelling figures underpin the capacity-for-loss and affordability conclusions. The inheritance timing ambiguity (noted in the extraction ambiguities) is present in both the report and the fact find and does not represent a contradiction between them, but the report's treatment of it as a received asset in the balance sheet without clarification is a disclosure shortfall. The Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA ownership is recorded under Anthony Barnes in the fact find [P18] and attributed to Yvette in the report; both describe it as the wife's ISA so this is a recording convention difference rather than a material contradiction. The incomplete balance sheet (missing GIA and S&S ISA) is the primary concern: the report's own figures do not reconcile across its sections.

  [G1.6] State pension entitlement is consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Both clients (Anthony Barnes and Yvette Barnes) expect to receive the full State Pension of £11,973 per annum gross from age 67, not yet in payment. This is asserted once in the suitability report covering both clients.
    File:     Cashflow models [P2], [P7], [P9] each record 'State Pension of £11,973 (inflated by 2.5%) from age 67' — corroborating both the amount and the state pension age. Cashflow model [P5] records Yvette's STATE PENSION AGE as 67. Cashflow model [P6] records Yvette's STATE PENSION AGE as 67. The fact-find [P15] records the State Pension Entitlement section as entirely blank (State Pension Retirement Age, Basic State Pension, Additional State Pension, Pension Credit, Spouses Pension, BR19 Projection and Notes all blank). No formal state pension forecast (e.g. government gateway statement or BR19) is present in the pack for either client.
    Discrepancies:
      - The fact-find [P15] records no state pension details whatsoever for Anthony Barnes — all fields are blank — meaning the £11,973 figure has no supporting fact-find entry for the primary client.
      - No BR19 or government gateway state pension forecast is present in the pack for either client, so the assertion that each client will receive the FULL state pension of £11,973 cannot be verified against primary evidence.
      - The figure-match log notes '67,' (the second instance of statePensionAge, for the second client) was not matched in the passages, though this appears to be a parsing artefact given multiple passages confirm age 67 for state pension.
    The suitability report asserts that both clients will receive the full State Pension of £11,973 per annum from age 67. The cashflow models ([P2], [P7], [P9]) corroborate the amount (£11,973) and the age (67), and [P5] and [P6] confirm Yvette's state pension age as 67 in the modelling tool. These are category [F] documents (cashflow files) rather than primary evidence of entitlement; they reflect what was input into the model, which itself derives from the report's assertion. The report cannot corroborate itself via the cashflow inputs it generated. The fact-find [P15] is on file but records every state pension field as blank for Anthony Barnes — it does not support the £11,973 figure or full entitlement claim. No BR19 projection or government gateway forecast is present for either client. The claim that both clients will receive the FULL state pension (i.e. maximum entitlement, not a partial amount) is a specific assertion that requires a forecast or National Insurance record check to substantiate. Without either a completed fact-find entry or a primary state pension forecast document, this assertion is unsupported by evidence. The state pension age of 67 is consistent across all cashflow scenarios and is not in dispute. The only discrepancy of substance is the absence of primary evidence to support the full-entitlement claim for either client. The second '67,' not matching in the figure-check log appears to be a parsing artefact; age 67 is confirmed in multiple passages and is not a finding. Severity is moderate: the state pension figure drives retirement income planning but the cashflow modelling is at least internally consistent; the concern is the absence of primary corroboration for full entitlement rather than a contradiction.

  [G1.7] Existing products (pensions, investments and protection plans) are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report records four existing arrangements: (1) Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd, SIPP, policy AV2075926-001, current/transfer value £79,092.32 as at 23/11/2025, holdings Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity (35.02%), Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (64.95%), Cash (0.03%), no ongoing contributions; (2) Unspecified provider, Cash ISA, £20,000; (3) Chartered, cash savings account, £40,000; (4) Moneybox, Cash ISA (Yvette's), £20,000. Total existing arrangement values sum to £159,092.32.
    File:     [P12] (Aviva client report) confirms policy AV2075926-001, product 'Pension Portfolio Pre-Retirement', closing value £79,092.32 as at 23/11/2025, with holdings VANGUARD LIFESTRATEGY 60% EQUITY - ACC at 35.02% (market value £27,694.85), VANGUARD LIFESTRATEGY 80% EQUITY - ACC at 64.95% (market value £51,371.06), and CASH at 0.03% (£26.41). [P1] also shows no money purchase or final salary schemes other than this one Aviva pension. No provider document is present in the pack for the Cash ISA (Unspecified), the Chartered savings account, or the Moneybox Cash ISA.
    Discrepancies:
      - The fact find [P1] records a value of £88,590.88 for AV2075926-001, whereas the suitability report states £79,092.32 as at 23/11/2025. The fact-find entry does not carry an explicit as-at date so the basis of the difference cannot be confirmed as a date gap alone.
      - The fact find [P1] shows the Policy Start Date as 09/03/2026, which is a future date relative to the report date of 16/12/2025. This appears anomalous — either a data entry error in the fact find or a planned transfer completion date recorded in the wrong field.
      - No provider document or statement is present in the pack for the Cash ISA held with an unspecified provider (£20,000), the Chartered savings account (£40,000), or the Moneybox Cash ISA (£20,000). These three arrangements have no corroborating evidence beyond the suitability report itself.
      - The report's switch charges assessment implies the new plan annual charge is £490 (£427 existing + £63 increase), but the annual recurring charges table for the new Aviva Platform Personal Pension states £512.69. This £22.69 difference is an internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction and relevant to whether the existing plan value used as a base is consistent throughout.
      - The derived figures note that the £63 at 0.08% implies a fund value of £78,750.00, and £427 at 0.54% implies £79,074.07, neither of which exactly equals the stated transfer value of £79,092.32, suggesting small rounding inconsistencies in the charge calculations based on the existing plan value.
    The primary existing arrangement — Aviva SIPP AV2075926-001 — is well corroborated. [P12] confirms the closing value of £79,092.32 as at 23/11/2025, matching the report precisely, and confirms all three holdings with matching allocation percentages. The fact find [P1] records a value of £88,590.88, but this is consistent with a different valuation date (the fact find predates the Aviva client report valuation date); the guards note that values move between dates. However, the fact find does not carry an explicit as-at date for this value, so the date-gap explanation cannot be fully confirmed — it is a possible explanation, not a certainty. The anomalous Policy Start Date of 09/03/2026 in the fact find is a data quality concern but does not affect the value reconciliation on the existing plan.
    
    The three remaining arrangements (Cash ISA £20,000 unspecified, Chartered savings £40,000, Moneybox Cash ISA £20,000) have no supporting provider documents in the pack. The requirement states minimum corroborating categories of 1, and the absence of any category A, B, C or E document for these three arrangements means the report's assertions about them are unsupported. These are relatively contextual savings/ISA figures rather than the pension transfer value that drives the recommendation, but they are included in the initial advice fee calculation base (£159,812 total including ISA £20,000, GIA £57,120 and pension £79,092) and so are material to the charges disclosed.
    
    The internal charge arithmetic inconsistency (£490 implied vs £512.69 stated for the new plan annual monetary charge) is a separate data inconsistency noted in the extraction. It does not affect the existing arrangement values but does indicate the existing plan value is being used inconsistently as a base across different sections of the report.
    
    The holdings allocations (35.02%, 64.95%, 0.03%) are confirmed exactly by [P12]. The policy number AV2075926-001 is confirmed by both [P12] and [P1]. The transfer value equals the current value, consistent with [P10] which states 'The transfer value of this plan is the same as the current value.'

  [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations unverified
    Report:   The report contains four identified internal inconsistencies: (1) Monthly expenditure is stated as £2,493 in 'Your current situation' but £2,206 in the cashflow modelling inputs — a £287/month unexplained difference. (2) The existing pension fund risk level is stated as 6 throughout, but the alternative solutions section states one fund is risk profile 5 and the other risk profile 7. (3) The switch charges assessment table implies the new pension annual charge is £490 (£427 existing + £63 impact), but the annual recurring charges table for the new Aviva Platform Personal Pension states £512.69 — a £22.69 discrepancy. (4) The initial advice fee is stated as £4,794 in 'Our charges' but £4,794.37 in the cashflow modelling inputs.
    File:     This requirement compares the report against itself. No external supporting documents were searched or required. The derived figures confirm: the £427+£63=£490 implied new plan charge versus the £512.69 stated in the charges table is a genuine internal arithmetic inconsistency. The derived figures also show that £427 at 0.54% implies a fund value of £79,074 (consistent with the Aviva Life & Pensions existing value of £79,092.32 within rounding), while £512.69 at 0.62% implies a fund value of £82,692 — a different base, suggesting the new plan charge of £512.69 is applied to a higher fund value (post-contribution or post-transfer) than the existing £79,092.32. The 0.08% differential applied to £79,074 yields £63, consistent with the switch table, but 0.62% applied to £82,692 yields £512.69, consistent with the charges table. The two monetary figures are therefore on different fund value bases.
    Discrepancies:
      - Monthly expenditure: £2,493/month (Your current situation section) vs £2,206/month (cashflow modelling inputs) — £287/month unexplained difference.
      - New pension annual monetary charge: £490 implied (£427 + £63 from switch charges assessment table) vs £512.69 stated (annual recurring charges table for Aviva Platform Personal Pension).
      - Initial advice fee: £4,794 (Our charges section) vs £4,794.37 (cashflow modelling inputs section) — £0.37 rounding difference.
      - Existing pension risk level: stated as 6 throughout (existing arrangements table and main recommendations) but alternative solutions section states one fund is risk profile 5 and the other risk profile 7.
    Four internal inconsistencies are identified within the report itself.
    
    1. EXPENDITURE MISMATCH (High): The report states monthly expenditure as £2,493 in the 'Your current situation' narrative and £2,206 in the cashflow modelling inputs. The £287/month difference (approximately £3,444/year) is material and unexplained. No basis difference is offered — both purport to represent the client's current monthly outgoings. This affects whether the cashflow model accurately reflects the client's financial position and is a genuine internal contradiction on a figure that drives the recommendation analysis.
    
    2. NEW PENSION CHARGE MONETARY DISCREPANCY (High): The switch charges assessment table shows existing charge £427, impact £63, implying new plan = £490. The annual recurring charges table states the new Aviva Platform Personal Pension total charge as £512.69. The derived figures show that £427 at 0.54% and £63 at 0.08% are both computed on a base of approximately £79,074 (the existing fund value), while £512.69 at 0.62% implies a base of approximately £82,692 — which is £3,618 higher, consistent with the £3,600 new contribution being added. The report does not explain this basis difference. However, the 'Like for Like' comparison using £63 outperformance requirement is therefore applying the new plan's higher charge rate (0.62%) to the existing lower fund value (£79,074), which understates the new plan's actual cost (£512.69 vs £490). This is a material disclosure concern: the switch assessment underestimates the new plan's cost by £22.69/year because it does not account for the larger fund value the new plan will hold.
    
    3. INITIAL ADVICE FEE ROUNDING (Low): £4,794 vs £4,794.37 is a £0.37 rounding difference. This is trivial and falls within normal rounding tolerance.
    
    4. RISK LEVEL PRESENTATION (Moderate, but outside scope of this check): The risk level discrepancy (6 overall vs 5 and 7 for individual funds) relates to investment suitability and risk profiling, which falls under CHK-003/CHK-009 and should not be assessed here.
    
    The two substantive findings for this check are: (a) the unexplained £287/month expenditure discrepancy between narrative and cashflow model, which is material to the financial planning basis; and (b) the £22.69 monetary charge discrepancy arising from different fund value bases being used in the switch assessment versus the charges table, which affects the accuracy of the switch cost disclosure. The rounding difference on the advice fee is noted but is not material.

  [G1.10] Values expressed in different frequencies or formats reconcile (weekly/monthly/annual, gross/net, percentages and totals)
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report states: (1) Anthony's defined benefit pension from Aon of £22,000 gross per annum, net approximately £1,700 per month; (2) Yvette works full-time earning £28,000 gross per annum, with a net of approximately £890 per month cited for a part-time role she has since stopped; (3) both clients expect the full State Pension of £11,973 per annum from age 67; (4) monthly essential expenditure of approximately £2,493 per month plus £800 discretionary; (5) cashflow modelling inputs current expenditure as approximately £2,206 per month; (6) initial advice fee stated as £4,794 in the charges section and £4,794.37 in the cashflow modelling inputs section; (7) existing pension annual charge: £427 at 0.54%; new pension switch assessment implies new charge of £490 (£427 + £63); new pension annual recurring charges table states £512.69 at 0.62%.
    File:     The fact find [P1] records Anthony's private pension from Aon Final Salary at £1,833.00 gross monthly (£21,996 annualised) and net £1,700 per month. The fact find [P4] records Yvette's basic employment net monthly income as £890. The fact find [P1/P2] records total calculated monthly household expenditure as £2,206.67 and total net monthly income as £2,590. The fact find [P9] records holidays at £5,000 annually and entertainment at £800 monthly. The cashflow file [P12] confirms £22,000 annual income in the 'In' column for years when only the DB pension is in payment. The Yvette reduced income calculation file [P11] shows gross income of £16,800 per annum (£1,400/month) for Yvette's 3-day week arrangement, with take-home of £15,615.60 per annum (£1,301.30/month) — not £890/month. The fact find [P6] records no state pension values. The extraction ambiguity document notes the monthly expenditure discrepancy (£2,493 vs £2,206) and the monetary charge inconsistency (£490 implied vs £512.69 stated).
    Discrepancies:
      - Monthly essential expenditure: report states £2,493 per month in the 'Your current situation' narrative but £2,206 per month in the cashflow modelling inputs — a £287/month difference with no explanation in the report.
      - New pension annual monetary charge: switch charges assessment table implies £490 (£427 existing + £63 increase) but the annual recurring charges table for the Aviva Platform Personal Pension states £512.69 — a £22.69 discrepancy on the same new plan.
      - Initial advice fee: report states £4,794 in the charges section but £4,794.37 in the cashflow modelling inputs section — a £0.37 rounding difference.
      - State pension of £11,973 per annum: asserted in the report but not corroborated by any state pension figure in the fact find [P6], which records all state pension fields as blank.
      - Yvette's income for the part-time role: report states £890 net per month; the Yvette reduced income calculation file [P11] shows take-home of £1,301.30 per month for a 3-day week on £16,800 gross — materially different from £890 net per month, though these may relate to different employment episodes (full-time vs 3-day week).
      - Aon pension gross annual equivalent: report states £22,000 gross per annum; fact find [P1] records £1,833 gross monthly = £21,996 annually — within rounding tolerance.
      - Cashflow modelling 'In' figure for years 2025–2029 is £38,800 per annum [P12], which is consistent with £22,000 DB pension + £16,800 Yvette gross (3-day week), but the report narrative states £28,000 gross for Yvette full-time — the cashflow uses a different earnings figure without explicit reconciliation in the report.
    Three material issues arise. First, the monthly expenditure figure used in cashflow modelling (£2,206) differs from the figure stated in the 'Your current situation' narrative (£2,493) by £287 per month. The fact find [P2] corroborates £2,206.67 as the calculated total monthly household expenditure, suggesting the cashflow figure is correct and the £2,493 figure in the narrative may include an additional component (possibly the £5,000 annual holidays = ~£417/month brings it closer but not to £2,493; or it may include discretionary spending differently). The report does not explain the difference. This is an internal inconsistency the report relies on in different sections, meeting the 'High' severity threshold. Second, the new pension annual monetary charge is stated inconsistently: the switch charges assessment implies £490 (£427 + £63) but the annual recurring charges table states £512.69. The derived figures confirm £512.69 at 0.62% implies a fund value of ~£82,692, while £427 + £63 = £490 at 0.62% implies a fund value of ~£79,032 — the difference reflects a higher assumed fund value in the charges table (likely because the £3,600 new contribution has been added to the £79,092 transfer value). The report does not explain why two different fund values underlie these two monetary charge presentations in the same document. This is a data inconsistency bearing on costs disclosure. Third, the £11,973 state pension figure is used in projections but no state pension record or entitlement evidence appears in the fact find [P6], where all state pension fields are blank. The £890 net/month figure for Yvette's part-time role: the report states she earned approximately £890 net per month in a role she has since stopped. The reduced income calculations file [P11] shows £1,301.30/month take-home for a 3-day week on £16,800 gross — this appears to relate to a different scenario (reduced hours going forward) rather than the stopped role, so a direct contradiction cannot be confirmed, but the figures are not reconciled in the file. The cashflow 'In' figure of £38,800 (£22,000 + £16,800) also implies the cashflow model used £16,800 for Yvette rather than £28,000, consistent with a pro-rata arrangement, but the report narrative does not make this explicit. The £4,794 vs £4,794.37 difference is a trivial rounding difference explained by the rounding guard and does not constitute a finding. The Aon pension £22,000 vs £21,996 annualised is also within rounding tolerance.

  [G1.11] Facts used in calculations, cashflow modelling, projections and assumptions match the facts stated in the report narrative
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   Cashflow was modelled. Assumptions stated in the report: general expenditure inflation 2%, savings growth 1.25%, state pension inflation 2.5%, property growth 4.7%, lending rate 4%, Aviva Platform investments growth 4.5%, surplus income saved 0%. Critical yield uses retirement age 60, low/mid/high growth rates 0.00%/2.94%/5.88%, ongoing adviser charge 1% p.a. Modelled current expenditure approximately £2,206/month. Baseline scenario inputs include DB pension £22,000 gross p.a., Yvette employment income £28,000 gross p.a. (3 days/week), state pensions £11,973 p.a. each from age 67, Aviva Platform Pension £79,092 plus £3,600 gross contribution, Aviva GIA £57,120 (minus initial fee £4,794.37), Aviva ISA £20,000. The 'Your current situation' narrative separately states essential monthly expenditure of approximately £2,493 plus £800 discretionary. The charges section states the initial advice fee as £4,794. The switch charges table shows existing plan annual charge £427 and impact +£63, implying new plan £490; the annual recurring charges table states new plan total £512.69. The existing arrangements table and main body describe the existing pension risk level as 6; the alternative solutions section states one fund is risk profile 5 and the other risk profile 7.
    File:     Cashflow files (F category) confirm: general inflation 2%, property 4.7%, state pension 2.5%, lending 4%, surplus income saved 0%, market crash scenario applies -30% return override in 2028, expenditure reduces to 80% on death of either party. Cashflow - Market Crash [P6] confirms 2028 return override. Pension switch report [P18] confirms critical yield retirement age 60, mid growth 2.94%, high growth 5.88%, ongoing adviser charge 1%. Savings growth rate 1.25% and Aviva Platform investments growth rate 4.5% are not corroborated in the retrieved cashflow assumption passages (cashflow files show only the inflation assumptions listed, not investment return rates for savings or the Aviva Platform). The cashflow files do not explicitly state a monthly expenditure input figure of £2,206. The switch charges table [P18] shows existing plan £427, impact £63; the annual recurring charges table (report internal) states £512.69 for the new plan. No cashflow file passage records the DB pension income, Yvette's employment income, or state pension figures as inputs.
    Discrepancies:
      - Monthly expenditure in the cashflow modelling section (£2,206/month) differs from the essential expenditure stated in the 'Your current situation' narrative (£2,493/month essential + £800 discretionary); these are never reconciled in the report.
      - Initial advice fee stated as £4,794 in the 'Our charges' section but as £4,794.37 in the cashflow modelling inputs section — a £0.37 rounding discrepancy between two sections of the report.
      - Switch charges assessment table implies new plan annual charge of £490 (£427 existing + £63 impact), but the annual recurring charges table for the Aviva Platform Personal Pension states £512.69 — a £22.69 inconsistency in the same report.
      - Existing pension risk level stated as 6 in the existing arrangements table and main recommendations section, but the alternative solutions section states the two current funds carry risk profiles of 5 and 7 respectively — a contradiction between sections of the report.
      - Savings growth rate 1.25% is stated in the report's cashflow assumptions section but is not corroborated in any retrieved cashflow file passage.
      - Aviva Platform investments growth rate 4.5% is stated in the report's cashflow assumptions section but is not corroborated in any retrieved cashflow file passage.
      - The cashflow modelling inputs figure of £2,206/month expenditure is not matched in any retrieved cashflow file passage.
    Several internal inconsistencies are present in the report itself and do not require external evidence to establish.
    
    1. EXPENDITURE FIGURE (£2,206 vs £2,493+£800): The report's own 'Your current situation' section gives essential expenditure of ~£2,493/month plus £800 discretionary (~£3,293 total), yet the cashflow modelling section inputs ~£2,206/month. The difference of £287/month (or ~£1,087/month against total expenditure) is unreconciled and unexplained. This is material because the cashflow outcome (no shortfall) depends on the expenditure assumption. The extraction explicitly flags this as an internal inconsistency. This is the most significant finding.
    
    2. NEW PLAN ANNUAL CHARGE (£490 implied vs £512.69 stated): The switch charges assessment table states existing plan £427 and impact +£63, mathematically implying new plan = £490. The annual recurring charges table in the same report states £512.69 for the new Aviva Platform Personal Pension. The derived figures section confirms both computations. The £22.69 gap is not explained. Both figures are on a comparable basis (annual recurring charge for the same plan at inception). This is an internal arithmetic inconsistency on a charge figure that is relied upon in the switch comparison.
    
    3. INITIAL ADVICE FEE (£4,794 vs £4,794.37): A trivial rounding difference of £0.37 between the charges section and the cashflow inputs. This is Low severity and practically immaterial.
    
    4. RISK LEVEL (6 vs 5 and 7): The main body describes the existing pension funds as risk level 6 throughout; the alternative solutions section states one fund is risk profile 5 and the other risk profile 7. These are not strictly irreconcilable (6 could be a blended average), but the report does not explain the relationship, and the alternative solutions section directly contradicts the single '6' used elsewhere. This check covers factual consistency; the implication for whether switching is appropriate is CHK-009 territory and is excluded here. The inconsistency itself as a factual matter is a discrepancy.
    
    5. ASSUMPTIONS NOT CORROBORATED IN CASHFLOW FILES: Savings growth rate 1.25% and Aviva Platform investments growth rate 4.5% are asserted in the report but the retrieved cashflow file passages do not include investment return rates (only inflation assumptions are visible in the retrieved passages). The cashflow files that were retrieved (market crash, Tony/Yvette predeceases) do not show these growth rates. This is an absence of corroboration rather than a contradiction. However, given the cashflow files do confirm other overlapping assumptions (inflation rates, surplus saved %, 2028 return override), the absence of investment return rates in the passages is likely a retrieval gap rather than the files being silent — but on what is available, these two rates are unconfirmed.
    
    6. DB PENSION, EMPLOYMENT INCOME, STATE PENSION INPUTS: These key income figures fed into the cashflow (£22,000, £28,000, £11,973 each) are not directly confirmed by any retrieved cashflow file passage. The cashflow files retrieved do not show an income schedule. This is a retrieval gap; the files are present but the income input pages were not retrieved.
    
    The expenditure inconsistency (item 1) and the charge arithmetic inconsistency (item 2) are the material findings. The expenditure discrepancy affects whether the cashflow correctly represents the clients' spending and therefore whether the no-shortfall conclusion is valid. The charge inconsistency affects the switch comparison. Both are internal to the report and need no external document to establish.

      [G1.1] No Issue
        - Report states Anthony Barnes is aged 55; fact find [P4] records his age as 56 (DOB 06/01/1970). If the report date is 16/12/2025, Anthony would be 55 years old (turns 56 on 06/01/2026), so the fact find age of 56 may reflect a later or prospective age, but the fact find itself states 56 while the report states 55.
        - Fact find [P4] records a Policy Start Date for the Aviva pension as 09/03/2026, which is a future date relative to the fact find completion date of 06/08/2025 and even relative to the report date of 16/12/2025 — this is anomalous but not a personal detail discrepancy per se.
        - No date of birth is present in the canonical model (extraction noted as absent); comparison of exact DOB between report and fact find cannot be performed, though the fact find records DOB 06/01/1970.

CHK-002 — Objectives, Needs and Time-Horizon Alignment
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G2.1, G2.2, G2.3, G2.4, G2.5, G2.6, G2.7, G2.8). The report states the inheritance amount relevant to the GIA is treated as the full £60k property money for investment; the fact find notes [P15] record the client receives circa £100k but will use £40k on kitchen/bathroom, leaving £60k to invest — this is consistent in outcome but the report does not acknowledge the £40k deduction or the fact that only £60k of the £100k inheritance is invested. The fact find [P3] records the client wants to be 'fully retired by 60'; the report does not state a target retirement age explicitly as a numbered objective, though the cashflow modelling implicitly uses age 60. The fact find records the cash ISA transfer as highly conditional ('not dead set on doing this'); the report presents it as a client-stated objective without qualification, potentially overstating client commitment to this goal.

  [G2.1] Stated goals and their priority order
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report asserts seven client-stated objectives: (1) ensure investments match agreed Cautious Balanced risk level (Risk Level 4); (2) invest inheritance tax-efficiently using a GIA, and consider transferring Cash ISA to a Stocks & Shares ISA; (3) provide flexibility and growth to support a desired retirement income of approximately £2,200 net per month; (4) simplify administration by keeping pension, ISA and GIA on one Aviva Platform with ongoing professional oversight; (5) take advantage of the pension annual allowance with a contribution of £3,600 gross (£2,880 net) in 2025/26; (6) review pension arrangements not assessed for six to seven years; (7) do not move Chartered savings account (£40,000) at this time. All seven are marked isClientStated=true. No priority ranking or detailed breakdown is extracted.
    File:     The fact find [P3] records: review of Aviva pension to improve upon it; client wants to be fully retired by 60; minimum income in retirement of £2,200 net per month; investment advice on £60k of property money coming in this month and £40k cash to avoid paying tax on interest; already used full ISA allowance this year; potentially interested in transferring cash ISA to S&S ISA but 'would have to be really worth his while — not dead set on doing this'. The adviser notes [P15] record: about to receive circa £100k but will use £40k on kitchen/bathroom, so only investing £60k; possibly move ISAs — not likely; only ATR 4; need to assess earnings for pension contribution. Notes [P16] record: retired, gets DB income, works part-time, retirement defo by 60. The cashflow and research files [P6, P8, P9, P10] model the plan against a retirement event and income target consistent with the report. No priority ranking appears in any file document.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the inheritance amount relevant to the GIA is treated as the full £60k property money for investment; the fact find notes [P15] record the client receives circa £100k but will use £40k on kitchen/bathroom, leaving £60k to invest — this is consistent in outcome but the report does not acknowledge the £40k deduction or the fact that only £60k of the £100k inheritance is invested.
      - The fact find [P3] records the client wants to be 'fully retired by 60'; the report does not state a target retirement age explicitly as a numbered objective, though the cashflow modelling implicitly uses age 60.
      - The fact find records the cash ISA transfer as highly conditional ('not dead set on doing this'); the report presents it as a client-stated objective without qualification, potentially overstating client commitment to this goal.
      - No priority ranking of objectives appears in either the report or any file document.
      - The report refers to the Chartered savings account (£40,000) not being moved at this time; the fact find does not separately record this as a stated client objective — it appears to be an adviser note rather than a recorded client preference.
      - The report states the pension has 'not been assessed for six to seven years'; the file does not independently corroborate this specific timeframe (no prior review date appears in the evidence).
    The core objectives — £2,200 net monthly retirement income, risk level 4 alignment, tax-efficient investment of the £60k inheritance via GIA, possible ISA transfer, pension review, and pension contribution — are all traceable to evidenced sources in the fact find [P3] and adviser notes [P15, P16]. The substance of each objective is corroborated. The discrepancies noted are of varying materiality. The conditionality of the ISA transfer is understated in the report relative to the fact find: [P3] records it would 'have to be really worth his while — not dead set on doing this', whereas the report presents it as a straightforward client-stated objective. This is a minor disclosure shortfall rather than a fabricated objective. The retirement age of 60 is evidenced [P3, P16] but not explicitly named as an objective in the report, though it is embedded in the cashflow modelling. The six-to-seven-year pension review timeframe is asserted in the report but not independently corroborated by any file document, though no document contradicts it either — this is an absence of corroborating evidence rather than a contradiction. The absence of priority ranking is consistent across report and file, so is not a discrepancy between them; however, where objectives could be read as competing (e.g. GIA investment vs ISA transfer uncertainty), the absence of priority order is a minor gap. No vulnerability markers appear in the evidence that would materially affect this assessment. Overall the objectives are substantially evidenced, with two minor concerns: the ISA transfer conditionality is softened in the report, and the pension review timeframe lacks independent file corroboration.

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) a long-term time horizon for the objective; (2) an intended retirement age of 60 for the client (Anthony Barnes, DOB 06/01/1970, so age 60 in 2030); (3) life expectancy basis citing ONS figures of approximately 84 for a 55-year-old male and 87 for a 59-year-old female; (4) the pension switch comparison uses retirement age 60 as the maturity point. The report is silent on an explicit targetDate, targetAge or termYears field (confirmed absent from extraction as NotPresentInDocument).
    File:     [P23] Fact find: Target Age recorded as 60; time horizon recorded as 'Medium Term'; objective narrative references income in retirement of £2,200 net per month. [P6] Pension switch report: retirement age used for the comparison is 60, consistent with the report. [P2]/[P4] Pension switch projections: 'Projected value at age 60' used throughout — corroborates the 60 retirement age. [P8]/[P10]/[P12]/[P14] Cashflow models: Anthony's 'Retire' event is set at age 67 in 2037, not age 60; his state pension age is 67. [P17] Cashflow — Tony Predeceases scenario: retire event at age 67 in 2037 for Anthony, with 'retire in 2030' appearing to reference a different event on the timeline (possibly Yvette's retirement at age 64). [P18] Cashflow — Current Situation: Yvette's 'Retire' event is age 67 in 2033. [P19] Cashflow — Yvette Predeceases: asset table shows ages 60/64 in 2030 as a year row but no retirement event at 60 for Anthony. Life expectancy: ONS figure for male stated as ~84 — corroborated by '84' in [P17] (ages run to 103/104). ONS figure for female stated as ~87 — nearest in evidence is 85 (Yvette ages in [P17]), not 87; the cashflow models run Yvette to age 107/108, not 87. The fact find records 'Medium Term' as the time horizon, while the report asserts 'Long Term'.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report asserts a retirement age of 60 for Anthony, but all cashflow models (P8, P10, P12, P14, P17) record Anthony's 'Retire' event at age 67 in 2037 — a 7-year gap that exceeds the 2-year tolerance.
      - The fact find (P23) records the time horizon as 'Medium Term', but the canonical model asserts 'LongTerm'. These are different horizon labels on the same case.
      - The report cites an ONS life expectancy of approximately 87 for a 59-year-old female. The figure 87 is not matched anywhere in the evidence (nearest is 85 in [P17]); cashflow models run Yvette to age 107/108, which is a planning horizon not an ONS life expectancy figure. The 87 figure is unsubstantiated by any retrieved passage.
      - The pension switch report (P2, P4, P6, P7) uses retirement age 60 as the maturity date, consistent with the suitability report's stated retirement age of 60. However, the cashflow planning tools use age 67 for Anthony's retirement, creating two different planning horizons in use simultaneously within the same advice file without reconciliation or explanation.
      - The fact find (P23) notes the objective time horizon as 'Medium Term', which is inconsistent with both 'Long Term' (report) and the pension switch analysis projecting to age 60 (5 years from age 55).
    There are two material issues after applying the guards.
    
    1. Retirement age 60 vs 67: The suitability report and pension switch report consistently use age 60 as Anthony's retirement / pension maturity age. All cashflow models in the file, however, record his 'Retire' event at age 67 in 2037. The guard notes that a selected retirement age on a pension plan is a contractual default and that the switch analysis routinely uses its own figure. Both guards are relevant: the pension switch figure of 60 could be a contractual maturity point rather than Anthony's actual intended retirement date, and the cashflow models' age 67 could reflect a different planning scenario (e.g. state pension age). However, the suitability report itself asserts retirement at 60 as the client's intended retirement age (extracted from /parties/clients[]/employments[]/intendedRetirementAge = 60), and this is corroborated by the fact find target age of 60 (P23) and the pension switch comparison (P6). The cashflow tools' use of age 67 is therefore a material inconsistency within the file: the financial plan models retirement at 67 while the recommendation is structured around retirement at 60. This is unexplained and unreconciled in any retrieved document, and the spread (7 years) exceeds the 2-year tolerance.
    
    2. Time horizon label: The fact find records 'Medium Term' and the report asserts 'LongTerm'. At age 55 with a target retirement of 60, a 5-year horizon would conventionally be medium-term, not long-term. This further suggests the 'LongTerm' label in the report may be aligned to a longer planning horizon (perhaps to age 67 or beyond), but if so the report does not explain this and the pension recommendation is built on a 5-year horizon to age 60.
    
    3. ONS life expectancy figure of 87 for a 59-year-old female: This figure appears only in the suitability report and is not corroborated by any evidence passage. The cashflow models run to age 107/108 for Yvette, which is a planning assumption, not an ONS statistic. The figure 87 is unsubstantiated. This is a weaker concern (the overall life expectancy narrative does not drive the investment horizon) but it is an unsupported assertion.
    
    4. The vulnerability overlay (/vulnerability/assessment is NotPresentInDocument) means no formal vulnerability assessment was extracted. The report confirms a Power of Attorney is in place (P22), which is a potential indicator of vulnerability consideration, but no further vulnerability data in the file materially affects this specific horizon/objective check.
    
    The dominant finding is the retirement age inconsistency between the recommendation (60) and the cashflow plan (67), which is unreconciled and material to assessing whether the strategy delivers the stated objective.

  [G2.3] Access requirements — emergency, planned and ad-hoc
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) a £10,000 emergency fund retained for instant access, confirmed as sufficient ('Amount put aside for Emergency Fund £10,000 / Amount of Emergency Fund Required £10,000 / Emergency Fund Shortfall £0'); (2) a planned spend of circa £40,000 from inheritance proceeds on home renovations; (3) a planned investment of the remaining £60,000 of the inheritance into GIA/ISA/pension; (4) the client is comfortable with 10-15 working days disinvestment timeframe because significant liquid cash is available for instant access; (5) the client has confirmed sufficient liquid capital immediately or on short notice to meet unexpected emergencies.
    File:     The fact-find [P18] records: Emergency Fund Amount put aside = £10,000, Amount of Emergency Fund Required = £10,000, Emergency Fund Shortfall = £0. This directly corroborates the £10,000 emergency fund assertion. The fact-find also records Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure = £2,206.67, Total Net Monthly Income = £2,590.00, Revised Total Disposable Monthly Income Available = £383.33. No fact-find passage explicitly records the £40,000 renovation plan or the £60,000 investment plan as a client-stated intention. The cashflow model files [P3–P16] show Anthony's 'Retire' event at age 67 in 2037 across all scenarios and model liquid assets projecting forward without any discrete withdrawal event in the near term for renovation. The cashflow inputs section [P21] references 'Aviva Investment Portfolio (GIA) - £57,120' and 'Aviva ISA Portfolio - £20,000 (from Cash ISA transfer)' and 'Aviva Platform Pension - £79,092 plus £3,600 gross contribution', consistent with investing the inheritance, but no cashflow or fact-find document independently records the £40,000 renovation earmark or the £60,000 investment allocation as a client-stated access requirement separate from the suitability report itself.
    Discrepancies:
      - The £40,000 planned renovation spend and the £60,000 planned investment allocation from inheritance proceeds are stated in the suitability report but are not corroborated by any fact-find entry, client questionnaire or cashflow model note — only the report itself records these as client intentions.
      - The cashflow modelling inputs [P21] do not include a near-term £40,000 withdrawal event for renovations, suggesting either the renovation has already occurred by the cashflow date, or it was not modelled, but neither is explained in any supporting document.
      - The report claims the client 'confirmed' sufficient liquid capital and comfort with the 10-15 working day timeline; no fact-find field or attendance note independently records this confirmation separate from the suitability report's own narrative.
    The emergency fund claim is the best-evidenced element: the fact-find [P18] independently records £10,000 put aside, £10,000 required, and £0 shortfall, which directly matches the report's assertion. This is corroborated by a category-B source and raises no concern. The planned renovation (£40,000) and planned investment allocation (£60,000) from the inheritance are stated exclusively within the suitability report (category I passages [P19]). No fact-find field, client questionnaire entry, or cashflow scenario independently records these as separately articulated client access requirements. The cashflow inputs [P21] show the GIA at £57,120 and ISA at £20,000, which is consistent with having invested the £60,000 net of fees, but this is internal consistency not independent corroboration. The absence of a £40,000 near-term withdrawal event in any cashflow scenario [P3–P16] is a mild inconsistency — if £40,000 was truly earmarked from cash for renovation, it might be expected to appear as a modelled outflow, though it is possible the renovation was expected to be funded from the uninvested cash holding rather than from modelled investments, and the omission may reflect a timing assumption. The client's confirmation of comfort with the 10-15 working day timeline and of sufficient liquidity is similarly self-contained within the report with no independent fact-find field recording it. These are not contradictions — the file does not say the opposite — but the access requirements beyond the emergency fund lack independent evidential support from category B or F sources. This is a missing-evidence concern at low-to-moderate severity: the emergency fund is properly evidenced, the broader access framework (renovation earmark, liquidity confirmation) rests solely on report assertion. No vulnerability indicators appear in the retrieved passages that would aggravate or alter this finding.

  [G2.4] Foreseeable life changes
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts seven foreseeable life changes: (1) Yvette intends to reduce working hours to 3 days per week from next year; (2) Yvette plans to fully retire when Mr Barnes turns 60; (3) both expect full State Pension of £11,973 p.a. from age 67; (4) inheritance of £100,000 from late father's estate due this month, ~£40,000 earmarked for renovations and £60,000 to invest; (5) from 6 April 2027 most unused DC pension funds will be included in the taxable estate for IHT; (6) pension minimum access age rising from 55 to 57 on 6 April 2028; (7) an income shortfall period is anticipated when Yvette retires until her State Pension begins at age 67.
    File:     The cashflow files record Yvette's retirement event at age 67 in 2033 (not at the point when Mr Barnes turns 60, which would be 2030). The cashflow files show Anthony's retirement event at age 67 in 2037, and both clients' State Pension age as 67. The cashflow files confirm the State Pension inflated at 2.5% p.a. The adviser notes confirm the ~£100,000 inheritance with ~£40,000 earmarked for kitchen/bathroom renovations and only the £60,000 to be invested. The adviser notes also record uncertainty about Mr Barnes's continued part-time employment and whether he will definitely stop at 60. No cashflow scenario models Yvette retiring when Anthony turns 60 (2030); instead all scenarios set her retirement at age 67 (2033). The shortfall period assertion (Yvette retires, then awaits State Pension at 67) is inconsistent with the cashflow modelling, which shows Yvette retiring at 67 simultaneously with her State Pension commencing, leaving no gap. The legislative changes (IHT from 2027, MPAA age rise to 57 from 2028) are not client-specific foreseeable changes per the guard.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Yvette plans to fully retire when Mr Barnes turns 60 (i.e. 2030 when Anthony is 60 and Yvette is ~63/64); cashflow models show Yvette's retirement event at age 67 in 2033, not at Anthony's age 60.
      - Report asserts a shortfall period when Yvette retires until her State Pension begins at 67; but the cashflow model sets Yvette's retirement at age 67 (2033), meaning her State Pension would begin at the same time, leaving no shortfall gap to model — the two are internally inconsistent.
      - Report states Yvette intends to reduce hours to 3 days per week from next year; adviser notes record uncertainty ('Defo 60?') about when Mr Barnes will stop, but no cashflow scenario models a step-down in Yvette's income at the intermediate stage before full retirement — no corroboration in cashflow of the part-time phase.
      - State Pension amount of £11,973 p.a. is stated in the report; the cashflow files confirm a State Pension for Yvette at age 67 but do not explicitly state the £11,973 figure for Anthony in the retrieved passages (though the cashflow narrative for Yvette references it).
    The most material discrepancy is the conflict between the report's assertion that Yvette plans to fully retire when Mr Barnes turns 60 (2030, when Yvette would be approximately 63–64) and the cashflow modelling, which consistently sets Yvette's retirement event at her own age 67 in 2033 across all four scenarios (Current Situation, Market Crash, Tony Predeceases in 2040, Yvette Predeceases in 2040, Higher Inflation). If the report's stated goal is that Yvette retires at Anthony's age 60, the cashflow model does not reflect this. This is a direct contradiction between the suitability report's stated foreseeable life change and the supporting evidence. The secondary discrepancy follows from this: the report's assertion of an income shortfall period when Yvette retires until her State Pension begins at 67 is only coherent if Yvette retires before 67 (as stated — at Anthony's age 60). If the cashflow model instead retires her at 67 simultaneously with her State Pension, no shortfall gap would exist, and the cashflow does not model one. These two discrepancies are linked: if Yvette retires at 63–64, a 3–4 year gap to State Pension at 67 would create a shortfall — consistent with the report's narrative — but the cashflow evidence does not model this scenario. The adviser notes confirm Mr Barnes's retirement intent at 60 is uncertain ('Defo 60?' and 'When will he stop? Defo 60?'), which is consistent with the report asserting an intention rather than a certainty, but also show that the cashflow modelling did not implement it. The reduction in Yvette's hours to 3 days per week from next year is stated in the report but is not reflected in any retrieved cashflow scenario as a step-down income event — no evidence of client authorship or quantification of the income reduction. The inheritance figure and allocation (£100k, £40k renovations, £60k to invest) is well corroborated by the adviser notes [P10]. The State Pension figure of £11,973 is referenced in the cashflow files (P2 confirms State Pension of £11,973 for Yvette from age 67). The legislative changes (IHT 2027, MPAA age 57 from 2028) are universal changes, not client-specific, so per the guard they are not client-specific foreseeable changes and their accuracy is not assessed here. The vulnerability path was noted as absent from extraction; no vulnerability indicators appear in the retrieved passages, so no overlay applies here.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report states the following objectives for Anthony Barnes: (1) ensure investments match agreed Cautious Balanced risk level; (2) invest inheritance tax-efficiently using a GIA and consider transferring Cash ISA to a Stocks & Shares ISA; (3) make a pension contribution of £3,600 gross (£2,880 net) in the 2025/26 tax year to take advantage of the annual allowance; (4) review existing Aviva Platform pension for suitability; (5) retire and maintain lifestyle. The cashflow modelling shows a 'Retire' event for Anthony at age 67 in 2037. The report states the client wants to be invested in a Cautious Balanced (risk level 4) strategy. No explicit priority ranking of objectives is stated. The report records that Anthony recently retired from his factory role and is now working part-time (then stopped), with a DB pension of £22,000 gross p.a. The report does not flag any conflict between objectives.
    File:     The policy research/fact-find (category G, passage P18) records client objective 3 as: 'Wants to be fully retired by age 60, so wants a plan for that.' Anthony is currently age 55. This means the client's stated goal is full retirement at age 60 (i.e., in approximately 5 years, around 2030). However, the cashflow models (P14, P15) record the 'Retire' event for Anthony at age 67 in 2037, not age 60. The cashflow model labelled 'Current Situation' (P11) also shows the plan timeline with a 'retire in 2030' annotation on the chart (P5), which aligns with age 60. The cashflow model assumptions sections (P14, P15) record Anthony's 'Retire' event as age 67 in 2037. The suitability report's cashflow section (P6) states the analysis is sufficient to meet expenditure needs through to age 100 with no shortfall. The fact-find (P18) also records a cash reserve objective of £100k+ and records the pension review, GIA investment and ATR objectives. No explicit conflict resolution between the age-60 retirement goal and the age-67 cashflow modelling retirement event is recorded in the evidence.
    Discrepancies:
      - The policy research document (P18) records the client's stated retirement goal as 'fully retired by age 60', but the cashflow modelling assumptions (P14, P15) record Anthony's retirement event at age 67 in 2037 — a 7-year discrepancy.
      - The cashflow chart (P5) appears to annotate 'retire in 2030' (age 60) as a plan event for one scenario, but the same document's assumptions section (P15) records the retirement event at age 67 in 2037, creating an internal inconsistency within the cashflow files themselves.
      - The suitability report does not mention the client's stated goal of retiring at age 60, nor does it explain why the cashflow modelling uses age 67 as the retirement age instead.
      - No priority ranking of objectives is stated in the report, and the competing uses of capital (GIA investment, ISA transfer, pension contribution, renovation spend of ~£40,000) are not explicitly prioritised or reconciled against the retirement-at-60 planning horizon.
      - The report states Anthony 'recently retired' but also models a retirement event at age 67 — it is unclear whether 'retired' in the report narrative refers to the factory role only, with continued modelling of a formal financial retirement at 67, or whether the age-67 event is an error relative to the client's stated age-60 goal.
    The central concern under G2.5 is whether stated goals conflict with each other or with the planning assumptions used to justify the strategy. Here, a material conflict is visible between: (a) the client's stated objective of being 'fully retired by age 60' recorded in the policy research fact-find (P18), and (b) the retirement event used in the cashflow modelling, which is set at age 67 in 2037 (P14, P15). This is not a minor labelling difference: a 12-year investment horizon (to age 67) supports a materially different risk/return, liquidity and drawdown strategy than a 5-year horizon (to age 60). The cashflow chart in P5 does annotate 'retire in 2030' (age 60/64) as an event label on the timeline, which aligns with the fact-find objective, but the assumptions section of the same cashflow documents records the retirement event as age 67. This internal inconsistency in the cashflow files means it is unclear which horizon was actually used in the modelling. The suitability report does not refer to the age-60 retirement goal at all — it records that Anthony 'recently retired' from his factory role, but does not connect this to a planning horizon of age 60, nor does it explain why age 67 is used in the cashflow model. The failure to surface and resolve this conflict in the report means the reader cannot assess whether the recommended strategy (which involves locking funds into a pension accessible only from minimum pension age, investing in growth assets in a GIA, and adopting a Cautious Balanced risk profile) is genuinely calibrated to a 5-year horizon to age 60 or a 12-year horizon to age 67. The two horizons would produce different conclusions on suitability. The guard on NarrativeAlignment requires structural testing: these two objectives do not merely sit alongside each other — the age-60 retirement goal creates a specific access/liquidity requirement within 5 years that would affect whether the recommended strategy is appropriate, particularly for the pension (where access is age-55 minimum, so accessible now, but the planning horizon matters for risk), the GIA (growth assets over what term?) and the ISA. The absence of explicit priority ranking is a lower-order concern given these objectives do not directly compete for the same capital. The more serious issue is the unresolved conflict between the stated retirement age and the modelled retirement age, and the absence of any discussion of this in the report. Regarding vulnerability: the report notes Anthony is aged 55, recently retired from 35 years of shift work, and his wife is planning to reduce hours. No formal vulnerability assessment is on file (noted as absent in the extraction). There is no evidence in the pack of characteristics of vulnerability that would materially affect this specific check beyond what is already captured.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states six objectives: OBJ1 (ensure investments match Cautious Balanced risk level), OBJ2 (invest inheritance tax-efficiently using GIA/ISA), OBJ3 (build up pension fund for good standard of living in retirement), OBJ4 (make lump sum pension contribution using inheritance), OBJ5 (retain sufficient liquidity/emergency fund), OBJ6 (estate planning/IHT efficiency). The recommendations are: NP1 (Aviva SIPP, linked to OBJ1/OBJ4/OBJ6), NP2 (ISA, linked to OBJ1/OBJ6), NP3 (GIA, linked to OBJ5/OBJ3), NP4 and NP5 (both linked to OBJ2/OBJ3/OBJ4). The client is aged 55, with a selected retirement age of 60, and all objectives are described as LongTerm. The report confirms a £10,000 emergency fund and notes the client retains sufficient liquid capital. Yvette (spouse, age 59) is noted as intending to reduce hours to 3 days per week from next year as a foreseeable change. The cashflow models scenarios including state pension at 67. The pension can be accessed from age 55 (rising to 57 in 2028). Cashflow modelling uses a retirement age of 67 for Yvette.
    File:     Cashflow documents (P1, P2, P4, P6) confirm Yvette is aged 59 and modelled to 'Retire' at age 67 in 2033, with state pension at 67. The pension value in cashflow is shown as £82,692 (versus £79,092.32 in the report). The cashflow fees are 0.63% ongoing (platform/fund) plus 1% financial planning fee. Multiple scenario cashflows (market crash, Tony predeceases, Yvette predeceases) are present. The asset statement [P6] shows Anthony's age as 55 and Yvette's as 59 in 2025 with pensions accessible. P15 (pension switch report) projects to age 60 with low/mid/high growth. P10 and P22 confirm the emergency fund, liquidity confirmation, and the inheritance-driven objectives. P13 and P17 confirm the risk-realignment rationale and fund switching rationale. No separate needs analysis document (category B or C) is present in the retrieved passages — objectives appear only in the suitability report itself (category I).
    Discrepancies:
      - The report selects retirement age 60 for the SIPP projections (and the pension switch report projects to age 60), but the cashflow modelling models Yvette retiring at 67 (P1) and the narrative context implies retirement income planning to 67 when state pension commences — the two time horizons (60 for pension projection, 67 for cashflow/income planning) are not reconciled in the report.
      - The pension fund value used in the cashflow model is £82,692 (P2, P4, P6) versus £79,092.32 stated in the report as the initial investment value — a difference of approximately £3,600, likely reflecting the gross contribution, but this is not explicitly stated.
      - Cashflow fees are modelled at 0.63% platform/fund (P2, P4) versus 0.62% stated in the annual recurring charges table in the report — a minor discrepancy of 0.01%.
      - Objectives are stated only in the suitability report (category I passages); no independent needs analysis document (category B, C or F) corroborates the objective statements or confirms client authorship.
      - The report states all objectives have a LongTerm time horizon, but OBJ5 (retain emergency fund/liquidity) is inherently an immediate/ongoing access requirement, not a long-term investment objective — classifying it as LongTerm is inconsistent with its nature.
      - The linked objective for NP3 (GIA) includes OBJ5 (liquidity/emergency fund) and OBJ3 (build pension fund), but the GIA is an invested product that does not directly serve either an emergency liquidity need or a pension-building objective — the linkage is weak.
      - Yvette's planned reduction to 3 days per week is noted as a foreseeable change, but its impact on household income and expenditure is addressed only partially in the cashflow — the cashflow inputs state Yvette's employment income as £28,000 pro-rata for 3 days (P21), but the timing and quantum of reduction are not confirmed in an independent document.
    The core objectives and their linkage to recommendations are internally consistent within the report: the risk-realignment objective (OBJ1) links naturally to the fund switch within the SIPP, ISA and GIA; the inheritance deployment objectives (OBJ2/OBJ4) link to the GIA, ISA and pension contribution; the retirement income objective (OBJ3) links to the pension; the liquidity objective (OBJ5) is noted and an emergency fund confirmed. The cashflow modelling covers multiple stress scenarios and is supported by the file (P1, P2, P4, P6).
    
    However, three concerns arise. First, and most materially, the pension projections are run to age 60 (the selected retirement age for the SIPP), while the cashflow income planning is based on retirement at 67 (when state pension commences, P1). The report does not explain whether age 60 is the intended benefit crystallisation date or merely the projection terminus, nor does it reconcile the two horizons. The client is 55; the NMPA rises to 57 in 2028. A recommendation to project to age 60 without explaining that this is a planning age rather than an access date, and without reconciling it with the cashflow horizon of 67, is a disclosure shortfall that affects the appropriateness assessment.
    
    Second, the objectives are stated entirely within the suitability report (category I). No independent needs analysis, fact-find, or client-authorised document in the file corroborates them. The objectives read as specific rather than generic boilerplate, which is favourable, but the absence of any category B/C/F document recording client-stated goals means the claim that these reflect the client's own expressed priorities is unsupported by external evidence.
    
    Third, the linkage of NP3 (GIA) to OBJ5 (emergency fund/liquidity) is weak: a GIA is an invested product subject to market risk and is not an appropriate emergency fund vehicle. OBJ5 is also classified as LongTerm, which is inconsistent with its nature as an immediate liquidity requirement. The report does separately confirm a £10,000 cash emergency fund, but the objective linkage creates a confusing picture.
    
    The 0.01% cashflow fee discrepancy (0.63% vs 0.62%) and the £3,600 pension value difference (likely the gross contribution) are minor and explicable, not material concerns here. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability indicators are identified in the evidence that would materially affect this assessment — the client recently retired and his wife is reducing hours, both of which are addressed in the cashflow.
