  [G2.7] Goals, assumptions or planning requirements stated in the report that are not supported by the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts seven client-stated objectives: (1) ensure investments match Cautious Balanced risk level (Risk Level 4); (2) invest inheritance tax-efficiently using a GIA and consider transferring Cash ISA to Stocks & Shares ISA; (3) provide flexibility and growth to support desired retirement income of approximately £2,200 net per month; (4) simplify administration by consolidating pension, ISA and GIA on one Aviva Platform; (5) take advantage of pension annual allowance with £3,600 gross contribution in 2025/26; (6) review pension arrangements not assessed for six to seven years; (7) do not move Chartered savings account (£40,000) at this time. Planning assumptions include: 2% general expenditure inflation, 1.25% savings growth, 2.5% state pension inflation, 4.7% property growth, 4% lending rate, 4.5% Aviva Platform investments growth, 0% surplus income saved, critical yield growth rates of 0%/2.94%/5.88%, retirement age 60, 1% ongoing adviser charge in projections, modelled expenditure £2,206/month, DB pension income £22,000 gross p.a. All objectives are marked isClientStated=true.
    File:     [P3] Fact Find records under 'Retirement Goals & Objectives Notes': the client wants to review his Aviva pension; wants to be fully retired by 60; minimum income in retirement is £2,200 net per month; looking at investment advice on £60k of Dad's property money and £40k cash to avoid paying tax on interest; already used full ISA allowance; potentially interested in transferring Cash ISA to S&S ISA but 'not dead set on doing this'. [P9] Fact Find goals table records regular income target of £2,200/month, target age 60, time horizon 'Medium Term'. [P7] Adviser notes: about to receive circa £100k but will use £40k on kitchen/bathroom; has another £40k in high interest savings; only look at investing £60k; possibly move ISAs — 'not likely though'; only ATR 4; expected growth 4.5%; both used ISA allowance; need to assess earnings for pension contribution. [P8] Adviser notes: 'Retired, gets DB income, but works part-time. When will he stop? Defo 60?' [P5] Fact Find records total monthly household expenditure of £2,206.67 and emergency fund of £10,000 (amount put aside equals amount required, shortfall £0). [P10] Fact Find records holidays £5,000 annually and entertainment £800/month for Anthony Barnes. [P11] Risk profile report records no additional notes on investment target or agreed risk level discussion. The ATRQ was not completed at the meeting ([P1]: 'Client did not have time to complete an ATRQ today, so have sent him an email version to complete himself on Defaqto').
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the objective to invest the inheritance tax-efficiently using a GIA is client-stated; the file notes (P7) indicate the adviser directed attention to the £60k figure ('only look at investing the £60k') and the GIA wrapper is not mentioned as a client-stated preference — the tax-efficiency motive is consistent with the file but the specific GIA vehicle is not recorded as client-initiated in the fact find.
      - The report states the Cash ISA transfer objective as a client objective; P3 and P7 indicate the client is 'not dead set' on doing this and it was 'not likely though' — the file presents this as tentative rather than a firm objective.
      - The report does not explicitly record the client's wish to spend £40k of the inheritance on a kitchen/bathroom (noted in P7 as 'will use £40k on kitchen/bathroom'), which is a foreseeable use of capital that affects the investment sum available, yet is not acknowledged in the objectives or assumptions.
      - The report asserts objective (4) — simplifying administration by consolidating on one Aviva Platform — as client-stated; the fact find goals notes in P3 and P9 do not record consolidation/simplification as an expressed client preference. This objective appears to have been adviser-framed rather than client-articulated.
      - The risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced) is stated as the agreed level in the report; P11 records no additional notes on how the agreed risk level was reached, and P1 confirms the ATRQ was not completed at the meeting — the client completed it independently on Defaqto, so there is no in-meeting corroboration of the agreed risk level discussion captured in the risk profile report.
      - The report's retirement age assumption of 60 is used in the critical yield calculation; P8 records the adviser questioning 'Defo 60?' — suggesting some uncertainty about this figure at the time of the meeting, though P3 and P9 both record target age 60.
      - The report uses modelled expenditure of £2,206/month, consistent with the fact find total (P5: £2,206.67); however the report's 'Your current situation' section states £2,493 essential plus £800 discretionary — the higher figure is not reflected in the cashflow modelling, and no explanation is given for the discrepancy (noted in extraction ambiguities but not addressed in the report narrative).
    The core retirement objectives — target age 60, minimum income of £2,200 net per month, review of Aviva pension — are well-evidenced in the fact find (P3, P9) and adviser notes (P7, P8). The inheritance investment goal and tax-efficiency motive are supported by P3 and P7. These key objectives have clear client authorship in the file. However, several issues arise. First, the Cash ISA transfer is presented in the report as a client objective when the file records it as highly tentative ('not dead set', 'not likely though' — P3, P7); this overstates client intent. Second, the platform consolidation objective (objective 4) is not recorded anywhere in the fact find or adviser notes as a client-expressed preference; it reads as an adviser-framed efficiency benefit, not a stated client goal, and calling it isClientStated=true is unsupported. Third, the planned £40k spend on kitchen/bathroom (P7) is a foreseeable capital deployment that affects the net investable sum and should be reflected in assumptions or objectives but is absent from the report. Fourth, while the retirement age of 60 is recorded in the fact find, the adviser's own note (P8 'Defo 60?') signals it was not firmly established at the time of the meeting — the report treats it as settled without acknowledging this uncertainty. Fifth, the expenditure discrepancy (£2,206 modelled vs £2,493 essential + £800 discretionary stated elsewhere in the report) is an internal inconsistency already identified in the extraction; it means the cashflow modelling understates expenditure relative to the report's own narrative, which is relevant to whether the retirement income objective can be met. The risk level agreement process has no supporting notes in the risk profile document (P11) and the ATRQ was completed by the client independently without adviser oversight documented in the file. The guards apply as follows: the retirement age and income target are evidenced in the fact find and are not merely inferred; the consolidation objective, however, is not evidenced even as an inferred client preference. The Cash ISA transfer is evidenced but as tentative, not as a firm objective. The kitchen/bathroom spend (foreseeable life change) is in the file but absent from the report. Overall, several objectives are adequately evidenced, but two are either overstated (consolidation as client-stated) or inadequately qualified (Cash ISA transfer), and one foreseeable material capital use is unaddressed.

  [G2.8] Target retirement income stated in the report is consistent with the evidence and with the figures the plan is modelled on
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states a target income of approximately £2,200 net per month (monthly frequency), including the defined benefit pension. The cashflow modelling uses current expenditure of approximately £2,206 per month. The DB pension income modelled is £22,000 gross per annum (£1,833/month gross, £1,700/month net per the fact-find). The plan is modelled to retirement age 60. The critical yield analysis uses retirement age 60. The cashflow assumptions include general expenditure inflation of 2%, savings growth 1.25%, state pension inflation 2.5%, property growth 4.7%, Aviva Platform investments growth 4.5%, lending rate 4%, surplus income saved 0%.
    File:     The fact-find [P1][P5] records the client's minimum income in retirement as £2,200 net per month, with a target retirement age of 60, income frequency monthly. The notes [P3] query 'Defo 60?' in relation to retirement, suggesting adviser uncertainty about whether 60 is confirmed. The fact-find [P15] shows DB pension income of £1,833/month gross (£1,700/month net), which at £22,000 gross per annum is consistent with the modelled DB pension income figure. The fact-find [P4] records total net monthly income of £2,590 and monthly expenditure of £2,206.67, consistent with the £2,206 modelled expenditure. The pension switch report [P9][P13] uses retirement age 60 in projections. The DB income figure of £22,000 gross p.a. (£1,700 net/month) means that at retirement the DB pension alone provides £1,700/month net, leaving a shortfall of approximately £500/month net against the £2,200 target, to be met by the recommended arrangements. No cashflow output document confirms the £2,200 target income is deliverable at age 60 from the modelled figures; the cashflow documents [P19][P21][P22] are present but contain only charts and narrative without explicit confirmation of the target income being met.
    Discrepancies:
      - The adviser notes [P3] record 'Defo 60?' suggesting uncertainty about whether the retirement age of 60 is fully confirmed by the client, while the report treats age 60 as a settled assumption throughout.
      - The DB pension income modelled in the report is stated as £22,000 gross per annum; this figure is not directly corroborated in the fact-find which records only £1,833/month gross (= £21,996/year, effectively consistent but not exactly £22,000 — immaterial rounding difference).
      - The cashflow modelling inputs use £2,206/month as current expenditure, while the 'Your current situation' section of the report states £2,493/month essential plus £800 discretionary — a material difference flagged as an internal inconsistency in the extraction; both figures appear in the report and neither is resolved as the operative figure for suitability purposes.
      - The target income of £2,200 net per month includes the DB pension, but the cashflow output documents in the file (P19, P21, P22) do not contain a tabular or explicit confirmation that the recommended strategy delivers this combined income at age 60 — the cashflow modelling section confirms the inputs but the output adequacy is not verified in the evidence.
      - The adviser notes [P3] flag uncertainty about when the client will stop part-time work ('When will he stop?'), which could affect the income bridge needed before full retirement at 60, but the report does not address this foreseeable income change.
    The core target income figure of £2,200 net per month is well evidenced in the fact-find [P1][P5] and is consistently stated in the report. The retirement age of 60 is recorded in the fact-find [P1] and used throughout the pension switch projections [P9][P13][P14]. The DB pension income of £1,700 net/month (£1,833 gross/month) from the fact-find [P15] is consistent with the £22,000 gross p.a. figure the report models. The £2,206 modelled expenditure matches the fact-find figure [P4] to within rounding. These core elements are consistent and corroborated.
    
    However, there are two concerns that prevent a clean pass. First, the adviser notes [P3] record explicit uncertainty — 'Defo 60?' — about whether retirement at 60 is confirmed. The report treats 60 as a settled objective and builds all projections on it, but the note suggests it was not settled at advice time. This is material because the pension switch projections at age 60 [P9][P13] are the basis for the switch recommendation, and if the actual retirement age differs, the critical yield and projected values change. This is a process risk concern at moderate severity.
    
    Second, the adviser notes also query when the client will stop part-time work [P3], which affects the income available before full retirement, but the report does not disclose this as a foreseeable income change or model any bridge. The fact-find [P15] records current income of £2,590/month net (DB £1,700 + employment £890), and the income change section records 'No' expected changes — yet the notes confirm part-time work is expected to end before or at 60. This is not modelled or disclosed in the report, creating a gap between the stated objective and the plan's assumptions.
    
    The guard on today's-money vs modelled figures does not apply here because the target is stated in today's money and the cashflow assumptions include 2% expenditure inflation — these are not directly comparable figures and the report does not purport to compare them directly. The guard on DB inclusion is relevant: the report explicitly states the £2,200 includes the DB pension, so this is not a finding about basis mismatch.
    
    The expenditure discrepancy (£2,206 vs £2,493+£800) is flagged in the extraction as an internal inconsistency but is not a G2.8-specific finding; CHK-001 owns it. However, the use of the lower figure (£2,206) in modelling without explanation is noted as a data quality point relevant to whether the cashflow accurately represents the client's position at retirement.
    
    Overall: the target income figure is evidenced and internally consistent, but the retirement age assumption is queried in the file and the income change before full retirement is unaddressed. These are moderate concerns, not high, because the retirement age is at least recorded in the fact-find even if the adviser's own notes cast doubt on its firmness, and the DB income alone is substantial relative to the target.

CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 9 raised a concern (G3.2, G3.3, G3.4, G3.5, G3.7, G3.8, G3.9, G3.10, G3.11). The canonical model records the CFL rating as 'Unspecified'; the report text (P11) states the rating is 'Medium-High' — these are inconsistent with each other, and neither is corroborated by a document outside the report. The CFL questionnaire section in the risk profile report [P1] records 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning no adviser rationale, discussion notes or CFL outcome label is documented in the supporting assessment tool — yet the report asserts a named Medium-High rating derived from a specific analysis. The report asserts CFL is separately assessed from ATR, but the risk profile report's CFL section [P1] is blank beyond the raw questionnaire responses [P2]; no documented derivation of a CFL conclusion separate from ATR is evidenced in the file. 1 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts a CFL rating of 'Medium-High' (the canonical model records the rating field as 'Unspecified', but the report text in [P11] states 'your capacity for loss is Medium-High'). The assessment basis is stated as: (1) a good level of guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions meaning only a small shortfall period when Yvette retires until her State Pension begins; (2) significant funds to cover any shortfall providing resilience to investment volatility; (3) cashflow analysis used to assess whether sufficient funds will be available for retirement. The report asserts CFL is separately assessed from ATR.
    File:     The risk profile report [P13] confirms a formal ATR questionnaire was completed on 09/08/2025 and an agreed risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) was reached. [P1] shows a Capacity for Loss questionnaire section exists in the risk profile report, but records 'No additional notes were recorded' in every sub-section (attitude to risk, investment target, agreed risk level, and the CFL questionnaire itself). [P2] shows CFL questionnaire responses were captured for Mr Tony Barnes on 09/08/2025. [P19] confirms both the investment target section and agreed risk level section also record 'No additional notes were recorded'. Multiple cashflow reports are on file (baseline, higher inflation, market crash, Tony predeceases, Yvette predeceases) [P3]–[P15], confirming cashflow modelling was conducted. The baseline cashflow [P14] shows a deficit period from 2030–2032 (ages 60–62/64–66) before Yvette's state pension appears to begin in 2033, consistent with the report's description of a shortfall 'for a small period of time'. The market crash scenario [P6] models a -30% return override in 2028. The fact find [P24] records expenditure items including holidays and entertainment. No document in the pack states a CFL rating label of 'Medium-High' or any equivalent named CFL outcome from the questionnaire tool or adviser notes.
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model records the CFL rating as 'Unspecified'; the report text (P11) states the rating is 'Medium-High' — these are inconsistent with each other, and neither is corroborated by a document outside the report.
      - The CFL questionnaire section in the risk profile report [P1] records 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning no adviser rationale, discussion notes or CFL outcome label is documented in the supporting assessment tool — yet the report asserts a named Medium-High rating derived from a specific analysis.
      - The report asserts CFL is separately assessed from ATR, but the risk profile report's CFL section [P1] is blank beyond the raw questionnaire responses [P2]; no documented derivation of a CFL conclusion separate from ATR is evidenced in the file.
      - The report's CFL basis refers to guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions, yet no quantification of those income streams relative to expenditure is stated in the CFL section itself — the cashflow modelling provides context but is not explicitly linked to the CFL conclusion in any supporting document.
    The report asserts a CFL of Medium-High and provides three stated bases: guaranteed income, significant assets, and cashflow analysis. The cashflow files on the case file [P14] do corroborate the existence of a deficit period (2030–2032) before Yvette's state pension begins, which is consistent with the report's description of a 'small period of time' shortfall. Multiple stress scenarios (higher inflation [P3], market crash [P6], mortality scenarios [P7]–[P10]) are on file, showing that cashflow modelling was genuinely conducted and was not merely asserted. To that extent the qualitative narrative in the report is broadly consistent with what the modelling shows.
    
    However, the critical gap is that the risk profile document's CFL section [P1] records 'No additional notes were recorded' throughout. This means there is no externally documented CFL rating label, no adviser note recording how the questionnaire responses translated into a Medium-High conclusion, and no written record of the discussion about the client's ability to absorb losses. The CFL questionnaire responses are captured [P2] but they are not translated into a conclusion anywhere outside the suitability report itself.
    
    The canonical model records the CFL rating field as 'Unspecified', which itself signals the report did not clearly state the rating in a way the extraction could pin down, even though the prose in [P11] says 'Medium-High'. This internal inconsistency is a minor disclosure point.
    
    The absence of corroborating documentation for the CFL rating label is the primary concern. The report cannot corroborate itself ([P11] is an [I] passage). The cashflow files provide supporting context for the narrative but do not record a CFL rating or conclusion. This means the CFL conclusion is asserted in the suitability report without an external document evidencing the rating or the reasoning that produced it. Under the decision logic this is 'capacity for loss asserted with no assessment on file' — the questionnaire section exists but records no outcome — which is a Potential Concern. The severity is Moderate because the cashflow modelling does support the qualitative narrative, and the CFL is not simply restated as a copy of ATR; it is separately reasoned in the report text. However, the absence of any documented CFL conclusion in the supporting tool means the rating is unsupported by external evidence.
    
    No vulnerability information is recorded in the case file (the extraction flags /vulnerability/assessment as NotPresentInDocument), so the vulnerability overlay does not alter this finding.

  [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report states that tolerance for loss was discussed with the client, who confirmed it is within an acceptable tolerance based on their capacity to suffer losses. No explicit maximum acceptable loss percentage or monetary amount is quantified. The capacity for loss is described as Medium-High, supported by good guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions and significant funds. The agreed risk profile is 4 (Cautious Balanced) on a scale of 1–10. No need-level risk figure is extracted from the canonical model.
    File:     The Defaqto risk profile report dated 11 August 2025 [P12, P21] records a natural risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) for Mr Tony Barnes, agreed following the ATR questionnaire completed 09/08/2025. The capacity for loss questionnaire responses are on file [P17] but the notes section records 'No additional notes were recorded' [P22]. The agreed risk level of 4 is confirmed across multiple Defaqto research reports for the pension [P6, P7, P14], the ISA [P3] and the GIA [P2], all mapping portfolios to risk level 4. The personal pension review report [P14] states the existing holdings have a 10-year future volatility mapping to risk level 6, which is outside the client's agreed risk level of 4. No quantified tolerance for loss figure (e.g. maximum acceptable percentage or monetary loss) appears anywhere in the file. No need-level risk figure is evidenced in the file.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not quantify tolerance for loss as a percentage or monetary amount; no such figure appears anywhere in the file either. The report's confirmation statement ('you confirmed this is within an acceptable tolerance') is not supported by any documented loss scenario, stress test or specific figure in the evidence.
      - No need-level risk figure is present in either the report or the file, preventing assessment of whether risk taken is commensurate with need.
      - The capacity for loss notes field in the risk profile document records 'No additional notes were recorded' [P22], meaning the capacity for loss questionnaire discussion produced no documented rationale beyond the questionnaire responses themselves.
      - The existing holdings are noted in [P14] as having volatility mapping to risk level 6, which is outside the agreed risk level of 4. The report does not address this discrepancy between the existing arrangement's risk level and the agreed profile.
    The report's tolerance for loss statement is formulaic: it records that the client 'confirmed' tolerance is within acceptable bounds, but does not quantify what those bounds are — no percentage fall, no monetary loss scenario, no stress-test output is provided. The guard acknowledges that many firms do not quantify tolerance for loss separately and that absence is a documentation gap rather than a contradiction. That guard applies here: the absence is not a contradiction of the report, but it is a documentation gap. The capacity for loss narrative (Medium-High, supported by guaranteed income and significant funds) is supported in substance by the cashflow files [P13, P15, P16] which show the portfolio surviving across multiple stress scenarios including higher inflation and spouse predecease, and by the guaranteed DB income referenced in [P16]. The agreed risk rating of 4 (Cautious Balanced) is consistently evidenced across all Defaqto research reports [P2, P3, P6, P7, P12, P14, P21] and aligns with the recommended fund strategy. The fund risk mappings in the research reports confirm the recommended portfolio (CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc, Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II) carries risk level 4, matching the agreed profile. One internal inconsistency noted in the extraction is that the existing pension holdings map to risk level 6 [P14], which the report does not address. However, the check excludes existing arrangement suitability, so this is noted for context only and does not drive the finding. The capacity for loss is described narratively as Medium-High with supporting rationale (guaranteed income, significant assets), and this is broadly consistent with the financial position evidenced in the cashflow models. The vulnerability assessment path is flagged as absent from the extraction [expectedButAbsent: /vulnerability/assessment]. The notes file [P23, P24] references the client's part-time work and impending retirement but records no vulnerability indicators that would affect this check. The primary concern is the absence of any quantified tolerance for loss — no loss scenario is modelled, no percentage decline is stated, and the confirmation is purely narrative. This is a documentation gap under the guard, but it is a real gap: the low-severity finding is warranted.

  [G3.4] Time horizon used to support the risk profile
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an investment term of 5 years and a time horizon described as 'LongTerm'.
    File:     The research reports (Personal Pension Plan review report [P10], [P12]; GIA research report [P8], [P11]; Investment ISA research report [P2], [P5]) all confirm an agreed risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced). The Personal Pension Plan review report [P10] records an investment of £79,066 and confirms the agreed risk level as 4. The risk profile report [P1] describes how the risk level is agreed by reference to 'investment amount, target and desired time horizon' but records no additional notes in the investment target or agreed risk level sections [P3]. The cashflow models [P4], [P6], [P7] project outcomes across very long horizons (to age 100+), which implies a long-term planning perspective. No passage explicitly states a 5-year investment term as the basis for the risk profile agreement.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts a 5-year investment term, but no supporting document explicitly records or corroborates a 5-year term as the agreed horizon used to determine the risk profile.
      - The report labels the time horizon as 'LongTerm', but a 5-year term sits at the shorter end of what is conventionally described as long-term, and no document explains why 5 years supports a 'LongTerm' classification.
      - The risk profile report [P1]/[P3] records 'No additional notes were recorded' in both the 'Investment target' and 'Agreed risk level' sections, meaning the horizon used to reach the agreed risk level 4 is not documented in the risk profile report itself.
      - The cashflow modelling projects outcomes to age 100+ (e.g. [P4], [P6], [P7]), which implies a planning horizon far exceeding 5 years, yet the risk profile is based on a stated 5-year term — the disconnect between the planning horizon and the investment term used to agree risk is unexplained.
    The report asserts a 5-year investment term and labels the objective time horizon as 'LongTerm'. The research reports confirm the agreed risk level of 4 was reached by considering natural ATR, capacity for loss and required return, and reference 'desired time horizon' as an input to that discussion. However, the risk profile report [P1]/[P3] records no additional notes in the investment target or agreed risk level sections, so the specific 5-year horizon is not documented as the basis for agreeing risk level 4. No passage in the file explicitly confirms that a 5-year term was used and agreed in the risk discussion. The figure 5 is corroborated numerically in [P10] (a derived figure match), but the context in [P10] is the investment amount (£79,066 for 5 years), not a standalone statement that the 5-year term was the agreed horizon underpinning the risk profile. The cashflow models run to age 100+, which represents the wider financial planning horizon. While money left invested through retirement can legitimately carry a longer horizon, the report does not articulate this distinction. The disconnect between the 5-year investment term, the 'LongTerm' classification, and the cashflow modelling horizon of decades is unexplained in any document. The absence of notes in the risk profile report means there is no evidenced justification for how the 5-year term was used to arrive at, or support, risk level 4. This is a documentation/missing evidence concern rather than a direct contradiction of the strategy: the strategy risk level 4 does not exceed the agreed profile, but the time horizon supporting that agreement is not adequately evidenced. No vulnerability indicators have been identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment.

  [G3.5] Investment strategy and asset allocation
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   Target risk rating 4. Pension wrapper: Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II, 100%, risk rating 4, OCF 0.41%. ISA and GIA wrappers: CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc, 100%, risk rating 4, OCF 0.29%. Strategy type: MultiAssetFund. Asset allocation detail not extracted from the report.
    File:     Multiple Defaqto research reports confirm the client's agreed risk level is 4 and that the new portfolio risk mapping is 4 (P5, P8, P9, P14, P22). The Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II carries a Defaqto risk rating of 4 (P24). The CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc carries a KIID SRI of 4 and a Defaqto risk rating of 4 (P6, P24). The pension switching research (P5) states 'The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' The report's internal inconsistency about existing fund risk (level 6 overall vs levels 5 and 7 individually in the alternative solutions section) is noted in the extraction but the recommended funds are consistently described as risk level 4 throughout.
    Discrepancies:
      - The extraction flags an internal inconsistency within the suitability report: the existing pension funds are described as 'risk level 6' in the main body and existing arrangements table, but the alternative solutions section states 'One fund is aligned to a risk profile of 5 and the other to a risk profile of 7'. This does not affect the recommended fund ratings (both rated 4) but is an inconsistency in the report's description of the existing holdings used as the switch rationale.
      - Asset allocation detail for the recommended funds is not present in the canonical model (path absent); the Aviva factsheet (P17) provides asset allocation for the Aviva fund but no equivalent factsheet-level asset allocation breakdown for CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc is in the retrieved passages, only the KIID risk/reward indicator (P6).
      - The report's volatility/performance comparison (P4) labels the CT Universal MAP as the 'benchmark for Risk Level 4' and the Aviva fund as fund C, but the chart legend in P4 labels Aviva as 'C – ISA & GIA / B – Pension', which is inverted from the actual recommendation (Aviva is for pension, CT is for ISA/GIA). This is a labelling inconsistency within the report passage.
    The core requirement — that the recommended funds' risk ratings match the client's agreed risk level of 4 — is well supported. Three independent Defaqto research reports (P5, P8/P14, P9/P22) each state 'the client's agreed risk level is 4' and 'the risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' The Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II is confirmed at Defaqto risk rating 4 (P24), as is CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc (P24, P6). OCFs match (0.41% Aviva per P24; 0.29% CT per P9/P13). The fund-level guard applies: the KIID SRI of 4 for CT Universal MAP is a regulatory risk indicator on a separate scale from the firm's Defaqto-based client risk scale, but in this case both scales converge on 4 so no discrepancy arises. Three discrepancies are noted. First, the internal inconsistency about existing fund risk levels (6 vs 5 and 7) is confined to the description of the existing holdings and does not affect the recommended strategy's risk alignment; it is captured under CHK-001 per the extraction. Second, the asset allocation path is absent from the canonical model; the Aviva factsheet (P17) provides asset allocation for that fund and shows a multi-asset profile consistent with a risk-4 balanced-cautious mandate (equities ~42%, bonds ~34%, alternatives ~5%, cash ~1%), but no equivalent CT Universal MAP asset allocation breakdown is in the retrieved passages beyond the KIID. This is an absence of a supporting document element rather than a contradiction; it is a low-level missing evidence point. Third, a labelling inconsistency in P4 within the suitability report reverses the pension/ISA-GIA fund labelling in the performance chart narrative, which is a minor disclosure accuracy concern internal to the report. No vulnerability information is recorded in the file (extraction confirms /vulnerability/assessment not present), so no vulnerability overlay adjustment is required. Overall the strategy risk alignment is evidenced and consistent; the minor issues noted do not constitute a material contradiction.

  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The client's agreed ATR is risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced) on a 1–10 scale. The recommended investment strategy targets risk rating 4. Capacity for loss is assessed as Medium-High, justified by guaranteed DB and State Pension income and significant liquid assets. The existing pension funds carry risk level 6 on the same scale, which the report acknowledges does not match the agreed profile of 4. The recommended new portfolio (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II for the pension switch, CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc for the GIA, with the ISA transferred) is mapped to risk level 4. The capacity for loss assessment is narrative in form and concludes Medium-High based on income resilience and liquid assets, but no quantified loss tolerance or specific loss-scenario figure is stated.
    File:     [P1] and [P24] (D – Risk Profile Report, 11/08/2025): ATR questionnaire completed 09/08/2025; natural risk level 4 Cautious Balanced confirmed. Capacity for loss section records 'No additional notes were recorded'. [P2] and [P4] (G – Personal Pension Plan review report, 03/12/2025): Agreed risk level 4; existing holdings' 10-year future volatility maps to risk level 6, which is outside the agreed risk level. [P3] and [P12] and [P15] (G – research reports): Agreed risk level 4; new portfolio risk mapping confirmed as 4 matching the client's agreed risk level. [P8] (G – GIA research report): CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc mapped to risk level 4 Cautious Balanced. [P13] (H – CT Universal MAP Cautious KIID): Fund rated 4 on SRRI (medium volatility). [P11] (I – Suitability Report): Capacity for loss stated as Medium-High with narrative justification referencing guaranteed income and liquid assets; no quantified loss scenario. [P24] (D – Risk Profile Report): Capacity for loss section contains no additional notes beyond the absence of notes.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. Capacity for loss rated 'Medium-High' in the suitability report but the underlying risk profile report (D) records 'No additional notes were recorded' in the capacity for loss section — no structured assessment, score or rationale is documented in the supporting file beyond the report's own narrative.
      - 2. The suitability report asserts capacity for loss is Medium-High and quantifies it as 'Unspecified' in the canonical model — no loss-tolerance figure or scenario modelling for capacity for loss is present anywhere in the evidence.
      - 3. The existing pension is described as risk level 6 on the same 1–10 scale as the client's agreed level 4 — the report acknowledges the mismatch but the canonical model flags isComparableToClientScale as true, treating the two scales as the same. The review report [P2] independently confirms the existing volatility maps to risk level 6, outside the agreed level 4.
      - 4. The alternative solutions section (noted in the extraction's internal inconsistencies) refers to risk profiles of 5 and 7 for the two Vanguard LifeStrategy funds individually, whereas the main body and existing arrangements table record a single blended risk level of 6 — these are not fully reconciled in the report.
    The primary requirement is whether the recommended strategy risk rating matches the agreed ATR and capacity for loss, and whether those assessments are supported by evidence.
    
    Risk rating match: The agreed ATR of 4 is well-evidenced. [P1]/[P24] record the questionnaire result of 4 (Cautious Balanced) dated 09/08/2025. [P2], [P3], [P12], [P15] all confirm agreed risk level 4 as the basis for the new recommended portfolio. [P3] explicitly states 'the risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' [P8] confirms the GIA fund (CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc) is mapped to level 4. [P13] shows the fund's SRRI is also 4. The strategy risk rating equals the agreed rating and is corroborated across multiple independent research documents. No override or upward adjustment is present.
    
    Existing arrangement mismatch: The report acknowledges the existing pension funds are at risk level 6 (versus agreed 4) and recommends switching. [P2] corroborates this: 'The client's agreed risk level is 4. The 10 year future volatility for the existing holdings based on the current asset allocation is 12.34. This volatility maps to a risk level of 6 which is outside the client's agreed risk level.' The report's acknowledgment is therefore supported. The internal inconsistency about individual fund risk levels (5 and 7 vs blended 6) is noted in the extraction but is not directly relevant to whether the new strategy aligns with the agreed profile — it relates to how the existing arrangement's risk level was described.
    
    Capacity for loss: This is a concern. The suitability report asserts Medium-High capacity for loss with a narrative rationale (guaranteed income, liquid assets). However, [P24] — the formal risk profile document — records 'No additional notes were recorded' in the capacity for loss section. This means the only evidence for the CFL rating is the report's own narrative [P11], which is category [I] and cannot self-corroborate. The report does not quantify a loss tolerance figure or model a specific loss scenario. The CFL assessment is therefore asserted but not evidenced by any independent document. Furthermore, the canonical model records the CFL rating as 'Unspecified', confirming no formal rated assessment was extracted. This is a gap: the severity guidance identifies 'capacity for loss asserted as a restatement of attitude to risk' as Moderate — here it is not even that, it is a narrative conclusion with no supporting assessment on file. The cashflow analysis [P19] demonstrates no shortfall is projected and this may be read as supporting the CFL conclusion indirectly, but cashflow modelling is not a substitute for a documented capacity for loss assessment and the report itself treats it as a separate exercise.
    
    Tolerance for loss: Not quantified; no loss scenario is modelled specifically for CFL purposes. This is Low severity in isolation but compounds the CFL evidencing gap.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: The extraction flags '/vulnerability/assessment' as NotPresentInDocument. No vulnerability characteristics are identified in the passages. No material vulnerability considerations arise that would affect this assessment.
    
    Overall: The strategy risk rating is properly matched and evidenced. The CFL assessment is the weak point — the report asserts Medium-High but the only document in the file that should record the CFL assessment (the risk profile report) contains no notes on it. This is a Potential Concern at Moderate severity.

  [G3.8] Conflicting risk scores, descriptions or labels across the file
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   Client ATR assessed via risk profiling questionnaire on 09/08/2025. Assessed code 4, agreed code 4, on a scale of 1–10, labelled 'Cautious Balanced'. No override. Description: 'quite a cautious person...inclined to view risk negatively...suited to a Cautious Balanced approach to investing.'
    File:     Risk profile report [P3/P14] records natural risk level 4, label 'Cautious Balanced', assessed 09/08/2025 on a 1–10 scale, with matching description. Agreed risk level is also 4 'Cautious Balanced' [P14]. Defaqto research reports for the pension [P4], ISA [P19/P21] and GIA [P5/P8] each record agreed risk level 4, label 'Cautious Balanced', with portfolio risk mapping confirmed as 4 in each case [P4, P19]. The Aviva fund factsheet [P18] uses a separate 1–7 volatility scale for the fund itself (not the client profile scale). The suitability report [P20] references the existing Vanguard holdings as carrying 'a combined risk level of 6' and notes the existing funds have 'a combined risk level of 6', while also noting one fund is aligned to risk profile 5 and the other to risk profile 7 (per the extraction's internal inconsistency note). The fact-find [P11] does not record a risk profile score.
    Discrepancies:
      - The Aviva fund factsheet [P18] uses a 1–7 volatility scale distinct from the 1–10 client profile scale used throughout the report and research documents — the two scales are not directly comparable by number alone.
      - The suitability report [P20] states the existing Vanguard funds have 'a combined risk level of 6' (on the 1–10 scale), while the alternative solutions section (per extraction internalInconsistencies) states 'One fund is aligned to a risk profile of 5 and the other to a risk profile of 7' — a single blended '6' is used in one place while disaggregated '5 and 7' appear in another. This is an internal inconsistency in the report itself, noted in the extraction.
      - No additional notes were recorded in the agreed risk level, capacity for loss, or attitude to risk discussion sections of the risk profile report [P12, P13, P16], meaning the narrative rationale for arriving at risk level 4 is absent beyond the questionnaire score itself.
    The core requirement — that the report's stated ATR (4, Cautious Balanced, 1–10 scale, no override) is consistent with and supported by the evidence — is substantially met. The risk profile report [P3/P14] corroborates the assessed and agreed code, label, description and assessment date exactly. All three Defaqto research reports (pension [P4], ISA [P19], GIA [P8]) independently confirm agreed risk level 4 with portfolio mapping 4 on the same scale, satisfying the minimum corroboration requirement. There is no override or upward adjustment to explain.
    
    The Aviva fund factsheet [P18] uses a 1–7 volatility scale. The guard explicitly states that a 5 on a 1–10 scale is not a 5 on a 1–7 scale and that profiler, provider and fund-house scales are not interchangeable. The factsheet scale applies to the fund, not the client's risk profile, so no conflict arises between the client's profile of 4 (1–10) and any fund volatility rating.
    
    The only genuine discrepancy is the internal inconsistency in the suitability report itself: the main body and existing arrangements table describe the current Vanguard pension holdings as 'risk level 6' throughout, while the alternative solutions section states 'one fund is aligned to a risk profile of 5 and the other to a risk profile of 7'. A weighted average of 5 and 7 is 6, so the figures are not irreconcilable arithmetically, but the report does not explain the blending, and using a single '6' in one place and '5 and 7' in another creates an inconsistent presentation of the existing fund risk levels. This inconsistency is material to CHK-003 only insofar as it relates to how the existing risk exposure is characterised relative to the client's agreed profile of 4 — the report's argument for switching is that the existing funds (risk 6) exceed the agreed profile (4), which argument holds whether 6 is a blend or the individual funds are 5 and 7. However, the inconsistent labelling across sections of the same document is a data quality issue.
    
    No vulnerability factors are recorded in the file that would materially affect this risk assessment (the /vulnerability/assessment path is absent per the extraction). The capacity for loss sections of the risk profile report [P13/P16] record no additional notes, meaning the CFL rationale is thin, but this is a separate concern from conflicting risk scores/labels which is what G3.8 specifically tests.
    
    Overall: the agreed client risk profile is consistently and correctly recorded as 4 (Cautious Balanced, 1–10) across the report and all supporting research documents. The only conflict is the internal inconsistency in how the existing fund risk level is described (single '6' vs disaggregated '5 and 7') within the suitability report itself. This is a low-severity data quality concern.

  [G3.9] Recommended funds and portfolio align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss and investment term
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   ATR assessed 9 August 2025 via questionnaire, agreed as 4 on a 1-10 scale, labelled 'Cautious Balanced'. Capacity for loss determined as 'Medium-High', based on guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions, significant assets to cover any shortfall, and cashflow analysis. Investment term stated as 5 years. Recommended funds: Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II (pension, 100%, risk rating 4, OCF 0.41%) and CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc (ISA and GIA, 100%, risk rating 4, OCF 0.29%). Both funds are stated to be rated 4 on the same 1-10 scale and described as reflecting the client's risk profile. Strategy is part of the firm's CIP. The report asserts 'The funds reflect your risk profile and are managed to remain within this risk tolerance.'
    File:     [P17] (D – Risk Profile Report, 11/08/2025): Mr Tony Barnes' natural risk level from the ATR questionnaire completed 09/08/2025 is 4 (Cautious Balanced) on the same Defaqto 1-10 scale; agreed risk level also confirmed as 4. [P4] (G – Personal Pension Plan research report, 03/12/2025): 'The client's agreed risk level is 4. The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' [P6] (G – GIA research report): Agreed risk level 4 Cautious Balanced confirmed, referencing ATR questionnaire score and capacity for loss discussion. [P13]/[P14] (G – Personal Pension Plan research reports): Agreed risk level 4 Cautious Balanced; Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II 100% invested. [P1] (H – CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc KIID): Fund rated 4 on the SRRI/risk-and-reward indicator; minimum recommended holding period 5 years. [P5] (H – Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II Factsheet): Risk rating given on a 1-7 volatility scale (not the profiler's 1-10 scale); the factsheet assigns a risk rating to the fund but on its own volatility scale. No explicit numeric risk rating for the Aviva fund on the profiler's 1-10 scale is given in [P5]. [P18] (I – Suitability Report): Existing Vanguard funds described as 'combined risk level of 6', with the report noting the recommended Aviva fund 'matches your Cautious Balanced approach' and demonstrates lower volatility. The report uses the benchmark ABI Mixed Investment 20-60% Shares for risk level 4 comparison. [P6] states capacity for loss was considered in arriving at agreed risk level. No standalone capacity for loss questionnaire or separate scoring document is present in the retrieved passages — the CFL assessment appears only within the suitability report narrative and the Defaqto research reports' risk-profile section which references CFL as a factor discussed.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states both recommended funds carry a risk rating of 4 on the 1-10 scale. The CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc KIID [P1] confirms a risk-and-reward indicator of 4, but this is on the SRRI 1-7 scale, not the profiler's 1-10 scale. The two scales are not directly comparable; the report presents both as '4' on the same basis without distinguishing the scale used for the fund's own indicator.
      - The Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II factsheet [P5] presents risk on a 1-7 volatility scale. The report assigns this fund a risk rating of '4' on the 1-10 profiler scale. [P4] corroborates the portfolio mapping as 4 on the profiler scale, but [P5] does not itself state '4 on a 1-10 scale' — the factsheet's own risk scale is different. This is a scale-comparability issue, though the Defaqto research [P4] independently confirms the mapping.
      - No standalone capacity for loss assessment document or scoring tool output is present in the file. The CFL determination of 'Medium-High' is stated in the suitability report and referenced as a factor in the Defaqto research reports, but no separate evidencing document (e.g. a CFL questionnaire result, cashflow model output confirming the conclusion) is retrievable from the passages provided. The cashflow modelling is referenced in the report but no cashflow model output passage is present in the retrieved evidence.
      - The report's investment term is stated as 5 years. The CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund KIID [P1] states the fund 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years', implying a minimum recommended holding period of more than 5 years. The client's stated term equals the fund's own minimum, placing the term at the boundary of suitability per the KIID's own language.
      - The internal inconsistency noted in the extraction (existing arrangements table records risk level 6 for the existing pension funds; alternative solutions section states one fund is risk profile 5 and the other risk profile 7) is present in [P18]: the report describes the existing funds as 'combined risk level of 6' while the alternative solutions section states individual risk levels of 5 and 7. This does not affect the recommended fund risk alignment but is an inconsistency in the report's own characterisation of the existing funds.
    ATR: The report's assertion of risk rating 4 (Cautious Balanced, 1-10 scale, assessed 09/08/2025) is well-supported. [P17] is the external risk profile report showing the questionnaire was completed on 09/08/2025 and the natural and agreed risk level is 4 Cautious Balanced. [P4], [P6] and [P13]/[P14] (all category G Defaqto research reports) independently confirm the agreed risk level of 4 for the pension, GIA and ISA goals respectively. The scale is consistently identified as the Defaqto 1-10 profiler scale across all these documents.
    
    Fund risk alignment — Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II: [P4] explicitly states 'The client's agreed risk level is 4. The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' This is the Defaqto research report for the pension and corroborates the report's claim on the profiler's own scale. The SRRI guard applies: the Aviva factsheet [P5] uses a 1-7 volatility scale and the SRRI should not be mapped to the profiler's scale. [P4] provides the correct corroboration on the profiler scale.
    
    Fund risk alignment — CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc: [P1] (the KIID) assigns a risk-and-reward indicator of 4, but this is on the SRRI 1-7 scale. The SRRI guard expressly provides that a fund's SRRI 'does not map onto the profiler's' scale. The GIA research report [P6] confirms an agreed risk level of 4 on the profiler scale for the GIA goal, and [P15] confirms the CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc was the selected investment for the GIA. The ISA research report [P19] similarly confirms risk level 4 Cautious Balanced for the ISA. So the profiler-scale corroboration exists in the research reports even though the KIID operates on a different scale. No confusion between scales remains a live concern given the research report corroboration.
    
    Investment term and KIID minimum holding period: The KIID for the CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund [P1] states the fund 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years.' The client's investment term is stated as 5 years. This is a boundary condition: the client's term equals the KIID's stated threshold. The KIID's language is precautionary rather than an absolute prohibition, and the guard notes that a minimum holding period longer than the client's term is a genuine mismatch while a shorter one is not. Here the periods are equal — the client's term meets but does not exceed the fund's own minimum recommendation. This warrants noting but is on the cusp rather than a clear mismatch; it is a potential concern requiring clarification.
    
    Capacity for loss: The CFL is stated as 'Medium-High' in the report, with three bases given (guaranteed income, significant assets, cashflow analysis). The Defaqto research reports reference CFL as a factor considered in the agreed risk level discussion. However, no standalone CFL assessment document, cashflow model output or CFL questionnaire result is present in the retrieved passages. The CFL conclusion is therefore asserted in the report and referenced but not independently evidenced by a separate document in the file. This is a missing evidence concern for a key suitability input. The report's CFL rationale does not appear to be a mere restatement of ATR — it references income sufficiency and assets separately — so the moderate severity flag for CFL = restatement of ATR does not apply, but the absence of an evidencing document is still a gap.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: No vulnerability assessment document is present in the file (noted as NotPresentInDocument in the extraction metadata). No vulnerability factors are identified in the retrieved passages that would materially alter the risk, CFL or strategy assessment here.
    
    Existing fund risk inconsistency: The report's description of existing funds as 'combined risk level of 6' versus individual levels of 5 and 7 in the alternative solutions section is an internal inconsistency in the report, but it does not affect the recommended fund alignment (which is consistently rated 4). It is noted as a discrepancy in the report's own narrative but does not give rise to a separate finding under this requirement beyond what is already captured.
    
    Overall: The core ATR-to-fund alignment is corroborated by multiple category G research reports. The investment term boundary condition with the CT Universal MAP KIID and the absent standalone CFL evidencing document are the material residual concerns.

      - Tolerance for loss is not quantified (no maximum loss percentage or monetary figure) in either the report or the file; the report records only that the client 'confirmed this is within an acceptable tolerance' — no stress scenario or loss figure is modelled or disclosed.

The projection basis does not straightforwardly correspond to a single wrapper amount, though the £160,000 is plausibly a combined total.

This is not directly contradicted by external evidence but creates an unexplained internal inconsistency within the report about the existing fund risk level.
    The core risk and strategy alignment is well evidenced. The risk profile report's CFL section [P8][P19] records 'No additional notes were recorded' and the CFL questionnaire responses are on file [P16] but contain no narrative or scoring output that independently derives 'Medium-High'. The CFL section has responses from the questionnaire, but no document in the file translates those responses into a 'Medium-High' label or confirms the label was produced by a separate assessment tool. The rationale offered in the report is therefore an internal assertion unsupported by external evidence. This is a Weak justification / Missing evidence issue, though not severe given that the CFL is consistent in direction with the evidenced financial position (guaranteed income, assets noted in the fact find).

The report records only verbal confirmation of acceptability. Under the severity guidance this is a Low concern where no large-loss scenario is modelled — and no such scenario is modelled or disclosed in the file.

As the instruction excludes assessment of existing arrangements, this element is noted but not determinative here.
    
    The projection amount of £160,000 in [P17] versus the individual wrapper amounts is not a contradiction: the risk profiling was conducted at a goal level using a combined amount, and the research reports confirm the per-wrapper fund mapping at risk level 4. This is explained by the different bases and is not a finding.
    
    Vulnerability: no vulnerability flags are identified in the evidence; the fact find records no accessible format requirements beyond 'No Requirement' [P15], and no vulnerability assessment is on file. This does not materially alter this check.
    
    Overall: the strategy alignment is evidenced and the ATR is properly corroborated. The CFL rating is asserted without independent corroboration from the file, and tolerance for loss is unquantified. These are Potential Concern at Low-to-Moderate severity.

  [G3.11] Funds inside a retained arrangement align with the agreed risk rating
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The existing Aviva Life & Pensions pension (EA1) holds Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity (35.02%), Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (64.95%) and Cash (0.03%), with a blended risk level of 6 on the provider 1–10 scale. The client's agreed ATR is 4 (Cautious Balanced) on the same 1–10 scale. The report explicitly states the two scales are comparable. The advice action for EA1 is RetainAndSwitchFunds: the report recommends switching the internal funds from risk level 6 to the recommended fund (risk level 4), giving 'Risk Alignment' as a primary rationale. The report states 'The current funds have a risk level of 6, which is too high for your agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4.' The arrangement is retained on the Aviva platform; only the underlying funds are switched. No other retained arrangement (ISA – Unspecified, Chartered, Moneybox) carries an alignment claim or a stated fund risk rating in the report.
    File:     [P3] (Risk Profile Report, category D) records the client's natural risk level as 4 Cautious Balanced, confirmed by the ATR questionnaire completed 09/08/2025, and states the agreed risk level as 4. [P10] (Personal Pension Plan research report, category G) states 'The client's agreed risk level is 4. The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' [P13] (GIA research report, category G) records the agreed risk level as 4 Cautious Balanced following an interactive discussion. The pension switching research report [P9] shows the existing fund (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II) performance data but carries no explicit Defaqto or provider risk level rating for the existing Vanguard funds. The file contains no Defaqto or provider fund factsheet for Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity or Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity that would independently confirm their risk ratings as 5 and 7 (or 6 on a blended basis). The Aviva factsheet [P1, P4, P5, P7] relates to the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II S14, not to either Vanguard LifeStrategy fund. The CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc KIID [P2] and factsheet [P8] relate to the recommended fund, rated 4 on the SRRI scale, not to the existing Vanguard funds. The internal inconsistency noted in the extraction records that the alternative solutions section of the report itself states 'One fund is aligned to a risk profile of 5 and the other to a risk profile of 7', whereas the main body and the existing arrangements table state risk level 6 throughout.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the blended risk level of the existing Vanguard funds is 6; the alternative solutions section of the same report states the two funds individually carry risk profiles of 5 and 7 — these are internally inconsistent labels for the same holdings.
      - The report asserts the existing fund risk level of 6 is on a scale comparable to the client's agreed ATR scale of 1–10; no independent fund factsheet, KIID or provider document in the file confirms a risk level of 6 (or 5/7) for either Vanguard LifeStrategy fund on any scale.
      - The file holds no Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity or Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity factsheet or KIID; the fund-level risk ratings relied upon to justify the switch recommendation are therefore asserted by the report alone with no corroborating evidence in the pack.
      - The advice action is described as RetainAndSwitchFunds; the report recommends switching the internal funds but the arrangement wrapper is retained — the guard notes that 'you should consider switching the funds' type language leaves the client invested outside the agreed profile, but here a firm switch recommendation is made, so the retained-without-action guard does not fully apply. However, until the switch is executed the client remains invested at risk level 6 against an agreed profile of 4, and the report does not record client acknowledgement of this interim mismatch.
      - No fund-level risk rating or alignment claim is made in the report for the three other retained arrangements (ISA – Unspecified adviceAction Retain, Chartered adviceAction Retain, Moneybox adviceAction NoAdviceGiven); no evidence in the file speaks to their fund-level risk ratings.
    The core of this requirement is whether the funds inside the retained arrangement (EA1) align with the agreed risk rating of 4, and whether the report's assertion about the existing fund risk level of 6 is supported by evidence. The report does explicitly identify the misalignment (existing funds at risk level 6 vs agreed profile 4) and recommends a fund switch to remedy it — which is the correct response to a misalignment finding. However, the fund-level risk ratings for both Vanguard LifeStrategy funds (the source of the mismatch claim) are asserted by the report alone. No Vanguard fund factsheet, KIID or Defaqto fund risk map is present in the pack to corroborate the ratings of 5, 6 or 7 ascribed to these funds. [P1], [P4], [P5], [P7] are Aviva factsheets for the Multi-Asset Plus II S14, which is the recommended new fund — not the existing ones. [P2], [P6], [P8] are CT Universal MAP documents, also for the recommended fund. [P9] and [P10] are research reports for the new solutions; they confirm the new portfolio maps to risk level 4 but say nothing about the Vanguard fund risk levels. The report cannot corroborate itself, so [P12] and the internal rationale passages are assertion not evidence. There is therefore a missing evidence concern: the claim that the existing funds sit at risk level 6 (or 5 and 7 individually) and are thus misaligned is unsupported by any independent source. Additionally, the internal inconsistency — risk level 6 in the main body vs risk profiles 5 and 7 in the alternative solutions section — is a data inconsistency that undermines confidence in which rating was applied and on what scale. The report claims the scales are directly comparable (both 1–10), but without a fund factsheet this comparability cannot be verified. The guard about incomparable scales therefore also applies: where the report asserts comparability and no document confirms it, the assertion is unsupported. The three other retained arrangements (Unspecified ISA, Chartered, Moneybox) have no alignment claim and no fund rating in the report or file; per the decision logic these are gaps rather than breaches, but they remain noted. No vulnerability indicators are identified in the evidence that would materially affect this assessment. The overall finding is a Potential Concern at Moderate severity: the risk-misalignment diagnosis and switch recommendation are directionally correct, but the fund-level evidence underpinning the risk level claim is absent and there is a labelling inconsistency within the report itself.

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). The report characterises the client's knowledge as 'Limited', which is broadly consistent with the questionnaire responses of 'Very little understanding / knowledge' overall and 'No understanding / knowledge' for key product types (Unit Trusts, GIAs, stocks and shares). However, the questionnaire also records the client has invested in stocks and shares 'by myself' within the last 5 years, which is a marginally elevated practical experience point not explicitly reconciled in the report's narrative. The report states the client has 'limited or moderate experience' (echoing the risk profile wording), but the questionnaire records 'Very little understanding / knowledge' as the overall self-assessment — 'very little' is more restrictive than 'limited or moderate', though the risk profile document uses the same 'limited or moderate' framing in its standard band description for a risk level 4 investor. No explicit grading or mapping of questionnaire responses to a formal knowledge classification (e.g. 'basic', 'informed', 'advanced') is present in the file; the 'Limited' label in the report is not traced back to a specific assessed score or classification in any supporting document. 1 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report records the client's knowledge level as 'Limited'. It describes the client as having 'limited or moderate experience of investments' and as not finding 'financial matters particularly easy to understand', suited to a Cautious Balanced approach.
    File:     The Investor Experience questionnaire (P1, P12) completed on 09/08/2025 records: overall self-description as 'Very little understanding / knowledge'; understanding of Current Accounts/Cash ISAs as 'A fair degree of understanding / knowledge'; understanding of Unit Trusts and GIAs as 'No understanding / knowledge'; understanding of stocks and shares as 'No understanding / knowledge'; understanding of EIS/VCTs as 'No understanding / knowledge'; understanding of pension planning as 'Very little understanding / knowledge'. Experience: Cash/savings products invested independently; stocks and shares invested independently (last 5 years); pension products through a financial adviser; Unit Trusts/GIAs — No. The risk profile report (P2, P4) describes the agreed risk level 4 as suited to someone with 'limited or moderate experience of investments' who does not find financial matters 'particularly easy to understand'. The recommended products are a personal pension (Aviva Platform Personal Pension, invested in Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II at risk level 4), a stocks and shares ISA, and a GIA — all multi-asset, cautious balanced, risk level 4 products.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report characterises the client's knowledge as 'Limited', which is broadly consistent with the questionnaire responses of 'Very little understanding / knowledge' overall and 'No understanding / knowledge' for key product types (Unit Trusts, GIAs, stocks and shares). However, the questionnaire also records the client has invested in stocks and shares 'by myself' within the last 5 years, which is a marginally elevated practical experience point not explicitly reconciled in the report's narrative.
      - The report states the client has 'limited or moderate experience' (echoing the risk profile wording), but the questionnaire records 'Very little understanding / knowledge' as the overall self-assessment — 'very little' is more restrictive than 'limited or moderate', though the risk profile document uses the same 'limited or moderate' framing in its standard band description for a risk level 4 investor.
      - No explicit grading or mapping of questionnaire responses to a formal knowledge classification (e.g. 'basic', 'informed', 'advanced') is present in the file; the 'Limited' label in the report is not traced back to a specific assessed score or classification in any supporting document.
    The report records the client's knowledge level as 'Limited'. The Investor Experience questionnaire on file (P1, P12) corroborates a low level of knowledge: the client's overall self-description is 'Very little understanding / knowledge', and specific product categories (Unit Trusts, GIAs, stocks and shares understanding) are also recorded as 'No understanding / knowledge'. The one tension is that the questionnaire records the client has invested in stocks and shares independently within the last 5 years, and checks valuations annually, which implies some practical engagement — but this is experience of holding, not necessarily comprehension of product mechanics, and the report does not rely on this to elevate the knowledge level. The 'Limited' characterisation is therefore not overstated relative to the file evidence. The risk profile's standard wording for risk level 4 ('limited or moderate experience… does not find financial matters particularly easy to understand') appears in both P2/P4 and is echoed in the suitability report (P22) — this is internal consistency, not independent corroboration, but the underlying questionnaire data (P1, P12) does independently support the low-knowledge picture. The recommended products — multi-asset cautious balanced funds inside a personal pension, ISA and GIA on the Aviva Platform at risk level 4 — are not complex bespoke or illiquid instruments. They are standard retail wrappers holding a risk-rated multi-asset fund, proportionate to a client with limited knowledge. No vulnerability indicators are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment. The minor discrepancy between 'Very little' (questionnaire) and 'Limited or moderate' (risk profile band description) is explained by the guard that self-assessed comfort and assessed knowledge are distinct data points and by the fact that the risk profile wording is a band descriptor, not a precise knowledge grade. No formal mapping of questionnaire responses to the 'Limited' label is documented, which is a minor gap but not material given the directional consistency. Overall the evidence supports the report's characterisation.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states the client has invested in current accounts, savings accounts, Cash ISAs, and stocks and shares within the last 5 years (made by himself), and has invested in pension planning through a financial adviser. The report also states the client has no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes.
    File:     The risk profile questionnaire [P1] records: current accounts/savings/Cash ISA/NSI — 'Yes, by myself'; unit trusts/GIA — 'No'; stocks and shares within the last 5 years — 'Yes, by myself'; EIS/VCTs — 'No'; pension planning within the last 5 years — 'Yes, through a financial adviser'. Understanding of stocks and shares — 'No understanding / knowledge'; understanding of EIS/VCTs — 'No understanding / knowledge'; understanding of pension planning and income in retirement — 'Very little understanding / knowledge'. The fact find [P3] confirms an existing personal pension with Aviva Platform (AV2075926-001). The fact find [P8] notes the client wants to review his Aviva pension and improve upon it. No fact find section records explicit experience with unit trusts or EIS.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the client has 'stocks and shares investments made by yourself' (implying active holding/investment experience) but the risk profile questionnaire simultaneously records 'No understanding / knowledge' of how stocks and shares work. The report does not reconcile holding stocks and shares with having no understanding of them — the two assertions sit side by side without explanation.
      - The report states 'no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes' but separately asserts the client has held stocks and shares within the last 5 years. Holding an asset class and having no understanding of it is an internal tension the report does not address.
      - The report characterises pension experience as 'invested in pension planning through a financial adviser' which matches the file; however the risk profile records only 'Very little understanding / knowledge' of pension planning, which is a materially low level given that the primary recommendation is a personal pension plan.
    The risk profile questionnaire [P1] is the primary evidence document for knowledge and experience. It corroborates the report's claims about product holdings: cash/savings/Cash ISA held by himself (confirmed), stocks and shares held by himself (confirmed), pension planning through an adviser (confirmed), no unit trusts (confirmed), no EIS (confirmed). To that extent the report is consistent with the file on what was held.
    
    However, a guard in the instructions states: 'Plans held do not prove understanding — do not treat plan ownership as evidence of knowledge.' The more material question is whether the understanding level recorded in the file is consistent with the complexity of the recommendation. The risk profile records 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares and 'Very little understanding / knowledge' of pension planning [P1]. The primary recommendation is a personal pension invested in market-linked funds (Aviva Platform Personal Pension). This is not a simple cash product. A client with 'very little understanding' of pension planning is being recommended a pension with flexi-access drawdown, multi-asset fund investment, platform charges and ongoing adviser oversight.
    
    The report's Knowledge & Experience section acknowledges 'no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares' but then states stocks and shares were held within the last 5 years by the client himself. This is an internal tension: the report does not explain how the client held stocks and shares independently while having no understanding of them. This is not a contradiction between two documents — both statements appear in the report — but it reduces the reliability of the knowledge narrative as a whole.
    
    The report's description of pension experience as investment 'through a financial adviser' is accurate per [P1], but the risk profile's 'very little understanding' of pensions is not reflected in the report's characterisation of the client's knowledge base. The report does not note that the client's self-declared understanding of pensions is at the lowest substantive level, nor does it explain how a pension product of moderate complexity is suitable for a client at that knowledge level.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: the fact find [P4] records no accessible format requirement and no vulnerability indicators are recorded in the risk profile [P7, P9, P15, P17]. The extraction notes /vulnerability/assessment as absent from the report. No vulnerability characteristics are evidenced that would materially alter this assessment.
    
    On balance: the product experience claims are corroborated by the file, but the report overstates or fails to qualify the client's understanding level relative to what the risk profile records, and does not address the apparent tension between holding stocks and shares and having no understanding of them. Given the primary recommendation is a pension — a product the client has 'very little understanding' of — the absence of any explanation in the report of how complexity is managed or how the client's comprehension was assessed is a material gap.
