  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report recommends three products: (1) Aviva Pension Portfolio (SIPP) — classified by the extraction as Complex, with complexity drivers including SIPP with flexi-access drawdown options, fund switching within pension wrapper, annual allowance/tax relief considerations, and IHT implications from April 2027; (2) Aviva Platform ISA Portfolio (Stocks & Shares ISA) — classified as NonComplex; (3) Aviva Investment Portfolio (GIA) — classified as NonComplex. The report notes the client (Mr Anthony Barnes) has existing pension arrangements including an Aviva Life & Pensions pension and other arrangements. The research reports record an agreed risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced). The report does not itself apply an explicit complexity label to any product.
    File:     The file contains: (a) Defaqto research reports for the Personal Pension Plan (03-12-2025), GIA (03-12-2025) and Pension Switching (16-12-2025), all recording agreed risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced) and selecting the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II as the investment vehicle; (b) Key Features documents for the Aviva Pension, ISA and GIA; (c) a pension illustration; (d) a fund factsheet for Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II S14. The Aviva Pension Key Features document confirms the product includes flexi-access drawdown options, self-select phased income drawdown and capped drawdown features, and that the client is classified as a retail client. The fund factsheet confirms multiple risk warnings (A, B, C, E, F — General, Foreign Exchange, Emerging Markets, Fixed Interest, Derivatives). The research reports describe the client as a 'Cautious Balanced' investor at risk level 4 with limited-to-moderate experience of investments. However, no fact-find document, no explicit record of the client's stated knowledge level or investment experience, and no record of client questions or comprehension checks in relation to the SIPP/drawdown complexity features is present in the retrieved passages. The extraction notes that /vulnerability/assessment is absent from the document.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not itself apply an explicit complexity label to any product; complexity classifications are inferred by the extraction model, not stated in the report. This means the report contains no explicit acknowledgement that the SIPP is a complex product or that the client's knowledge is sufficient for it.
      - No fact-find or client knowledge/experience record is present in the retrieved passages — the file does not contain a document recording the client's stated investment knowledge level, prior pension or investment experience, or investment understanding that can be compared to the report's implicit assertions.
      - No record of client questions, adviser explanations or comprehension checks specific to the SIPP/drawdown complexity features (flexi-access drawdown, IHT implications from April 2027, annual allowance considerations) appears in the retrieved passages.
      - The Defaqto research report describes the client as 'quite a cautious person' who 'may have limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters straightforward' — this is the closest the file comes to recording knowledge/experience, but it is a generic risk-profile description, not a client-specific knowledge assessment, and it suggests limited-to-moderate experience rather than confirming the client has sufficient knowledge for a complex product.
      - The vulnerability assessment path (/vulnerability/assessment) is recorded as absent from the document; no vulnerability overlay evidence is available to assess whether any vulnerability characteristic affects the knowledge/experience suitability conclusion.
    The SIPP with flexi-access drawdown is correctly identified as a complex product by regulatory classification. The file contains product literature (Aviva Pension Key Features, fund factsheet, Defaqto research report) that evidences the product's features and risks. However, the requirement is not only that product literature exists — it is that the recommended product's complexity is consistent with the client's demonstrated knowledge and experience, and that comprehension is corroborated where the product is complex. On those two limbs: (1) No fact-find or explicit knowledge/experience record is in the retrieved passages. The only file-side characterisation of the client's experience is the generic Defaqto risk-profile description at risk level 4 ('Cautious Balanced'), which notes the client 'may have limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters straightforward.' This is not a knowledge assessment; it is a risk-profile descriptor. It cannot corroborate a claim that the client has sufficient knowledge for a complex product. (2) The suitability report does not itself apply a complexity label, does not record the client's knowledge level explicitly, and does not record any comprehension check, client question or adviser explanation specific to the SIPP's complex features (drawdown mechanics, IHT treatment post-April 2027, annual allowance interactions). These are material omissions where the product is complex. The ISA and GIA are correctly classified as non-complex (single multi-asset fund in mainstream wrappers) and the guard confirms a mainstream personal pension in multi-asset funds is not itself escalatable on product volume alone — but the SIPP here includes flexi-access drawdown and specific IHT complexity drivers that go beyond a standard personal pension. No vulnerability assessment is on file, so no overlay adjustment can be made. The absence of a client knowledge record and of any comprehension evidence for the complex product constitutes a Potential Concern at Moderate severity: the file does not record that the client's knowledge and experience are commensurate with the complexity of the recommended SIPP, and no comprehension check is evidenced.

  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The client (Mr Tony Barnes) has a stated knowledge level of 'Limited'. Three products are recommended: one classified as 'Complex' (the pension) and two as 'NonComplex' (ISA and GIA). The report states the client has very little understanding of investing, a fair degree of understanding of cash accounts/savings/Cash ISAs, no understanding of unit trusts/stocks and shares/EIS, has invested in pension planning through a financial adviser, and describes the client as having a Cautious Balanced risk profile (risk level 4). The report asserts that 'you confirmed you understand' the recommended products.
    File:     The risk profile questionnaire (D, [P4], [P7], [P16]) completed on 09/08/2025 records: no understanding/knowledge of stocks and shares; no understanding of EIS/VCTs; very little understanding/knowledge of pension planning and income in retirement; invested in current accounts/savings/Cash ISAs by himself; NOT invested in unit trusts or GIAs in the last 5 years; invested in stocks and shares by himself (last 5 years); invested in pension planning through a financial adviser (last 5 years). The risk profile description for level 4 Cautious Balanced states 'If you have limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand then you may be suited to a Cautious Balanced approach.' The fact find [P21] notes the ATRQ was not completed at the meeting and was sent by email for the client to complete himself. No category C record (e.g. questions asked, explanations given, comprehension confirmed by an independent record) appears in the evidence pack for the pension product, which the report classifies as Complex.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report classifies the pension as 'Complex' but records the client's knowledge level as 'Limited', with very little understanding of pension planning and no experience of unit trusts or GIAs. No category C corroboration of comprehension of the Complex product is present in the evidence pack.
      - The report states the client has invested in stocks and shares 'by yourself' and uses this as part of the knowledge narrative, but the risk profile questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' of how stocks and shares work despite confirming recent investment — the experiential record and the understanding record are inconsistent.
      - The report's assertion that 'you confirmed you understand' the recommended products is an adviser assertion, not a corroborated record of demonstrated comprehension. No independent category C document evidences client understanding of the Complex (pension) product.
      - The fact find notes the ATRQ was not completed at the meeting but was sent by email for self-completion — the circumstances of completion reduce the quality of the knowledge/experience evidence base.
      - The risk profile questionnaire records no investment in unit trusts or GIAs in the last 5 years, yet a GIA is one of the recommended products. The report does not address the absence of prior GIA experience in the context of product complexity suitability.
    The central concern under G4.4 is whether a Complex product (the pension) has been recommended to a client whose file records Limited knowledge and very little understanding of pension planning. The report acknowledges Limited knowledge but classifies the pension as Complex. Under the decision logic, this is a high-severity scenario: a complex product recommended where the file records limited relevant knowledge, with no category C corroboration of comprehension. The adviser's assertion that the client 'confirmed understanding' cannot self-corroborate: an [I] passage cannot serve as evidence of the fact it asserts. The risk profile description includes the precondition 'limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand' as a suitability qualifier for Cautious Balanced, which is consistent with Limited knowledge but does not itself establish comprehension of the pension product's features, risks or complexity. The stocks-and-shares discrepancy (investment made but no understanding recorded) is an internal inconsistency in the knowledge record but is a secondary concern. The absence of GIA experience on record (never invested in unit trusts/GIAs per P16) is noted: the report classifies GIA as NonComplex, so this does not trigger the highest severity threshold, but it adds to the picture of limited experience. The ATRQ completion by email self-completion (P21) slightly weakens the quality of the underlying knowledge assessment. Vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability flags are identified in the evidence that would further aggravate or mitigate this finding. The finding stands on the Complex pension product being recommended to a client with Limited stated knowledge and no category C comprehension record.

  [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report states the client describes himself as having very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing; has a fair degree of understanding of cash accounts, savings accounts and Cash ISAs but no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares or enterprise investment schemes; checks valuations on an annual basis only; and that an investor experience questionnaire was completed on 9 August 2025. The client's risk profile was agreed as 4 on a scale of 1–10, described as cautious with limited or moderate investment experience who does not find financial matters particularly easy to understand.
    File:     The fact find (P18) records the meeting date as 06/08/2025 (telephone, recorded) and notes the ATRQ was not completed at that meeting — the client was sent an email version to complete on Defaqto (P10). The risk profile report (P13, P19) records no additional notes in the capacity for loss, investment target or agreed risk level sections. Adviser notes (P8, P9) record: the client is retired with DB income, works part-time, is about to receive circa £100k inheritance, only ATR 4, and raise questions about earnings and pension contributions. No passage records the client's questions during the advice process, no passage records any explanation of product features given to the client, and no passage records the client's comprehension of the products recommended. The recommended products include an Aviva Platform Personal Pension (with a CIP fund strategy), an ISA and a GIA — all involving unit-trust-type collective investments, which the report itself records the client has no understanding of. Search Q4.5.2 returned nothing.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report records the investor experience questionnaire was completed on 9 August 2025; the fact find meeting was on 6 August 2025 and notes the ATRQ was sent by email after the meeting — no passage in the file confirms the questionnaire was completed or its results.
      - The report states the client has no understanding of unit trusts or stocks and shares, yet recommends products (pension CIP, ISA, GIA) that are invested in collective funds — no evidence in the file that the gap between the client's knowledge and the product complexity was bridged by explanation or comprehension testing.
      - No passage records the client's questions, clarifications sought or comprehension of the recommended products' features or risks, despite the products being complex relative to the client's stated knowledge level.
      - The risk profile report sections on capacity for loss, investment target and agreed risk level all record 'No additional notes were recorded', providing no corroborating qualitative evidence of client understanding.
    The report is explicit that the client has very little investment knowledge and no understanding of unit trusts or stocks and shares. The products recommended — a personal pension invested in a CIP (collective fund strategy), a stocks and shares ISA and a GIA — all involve precisely the instrument types the client is recorded as not understanding. This creates a direct tension between stated knowledge level and product complexity. For such a client, COBS 9.2 requires the adviser to be satisfied the recommendation is suitable in light of knowledge and experience; where there is a material gap, the file should contain evidence that the gap was addressed — typically through recorded questions, explanations and some form of comprehension confirmation. No such evidence exists in the file: the risk profile report records no qualitative notes in any relevant section, the fact find records the ATRQ was not completed at the meeting and was sent by email, and the sole reference in the report to the questionnaire (completed 9 August 2025) is unconfirmed by any document. Search Q4.5.2 returned nothing, confirming the absence of comprehension evidence. The guard correctly notes that absence of recorded questions is not proof of non-understanding, but the instructions treat that as an evidence gap to be weighted by product complexity — here, the gap is material because (a) the client's own stated knowledge is 'very little' / 'no understanding' of the relevant product types, and (b) the products are complex relative to that baseline. The vulnerability overlay does not add a separate concern here beyond the general knowledge gap, as no specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded that would independently heighten the risk. The finding is a Potential Concern: the report's own characterisation of the client's knowledge level, combined with the absence of any file evidence that comprehension was established, means the suitability basis for recommending complex products to this client is unsupported.

      - The report asserts the client has experience of stocks and shares investments made by himself; the transaction history shows only Vanguard LifeStrategy funds held within a pension (an arrangement set up through a financial adviser), not direct self-directed stocks and shares investments.
      - The report states the client has no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares (direct), or enterprise investment schemes, yet simultaneously asserts experience of stocks and shares investments made by himself — this is potentially internally inconsistent within the report's own knowledge/experience narrative.
      - The fact find (P2/P12) records a policy start date of 09/03/2026 for AV2075926-001, which post-dates the suitability report date of 16/12/2025 and the client report period starting 24/11/2022; this appears to be a data entry anomaly in the fact find rather than a substantive discrepancy, but the file does not clearly record the original pension inception date.
      - No graded or scored knowledge level for the client appears in the fact find or any supporting document in the file.
    The core pension holding (Vanguard LifeStrategy funds in the Aviva SIPP) is well corroborated: the transaction history (P9, P10, P11) shows holdings going back to at least January 2018 in both Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity, and the client report (P3) confirms the current pension value. This directly supports the report's assertion of personal pension experience through a financial adviser. The guard that an arrangement set up by a previous adviser says little about the client's own experience is relevant here: the transaction history shows these funds were selected and maintained (with regular charge deductions and periodic unit sales to fund charges) over many years, but the fact find (P4) records the client wants to 'review' the pension, and the report notes pension planning was done 'through a financial adviser' — so the experience is adviser-led rather than self-directed, which is consistent with the report's own narrative. Regarding the assertion of self-directed stocks and shares experience: the report states 'stocks and shares investments made by yourself' yet simultaneously records no understanding of stocks and shares in the unit trust/direct equity sense. The file contains no evidence of direct self-directed equities or stocks-and-shares holdings — only LifeStrategy funds within an adviser-managed pension. This creates an internal tension in the report's knowledge narrative. The report's claim of experience with current accounts, savings accounts, and Cash ISAs is entirely self-referential — no fact find field, bank statement, or other document in the file records these holdings. While it is common for clients to hold such products, the file provides no corroboration. This is an absence of evidence rather than a contradiction, but under the evidence-absent rule it constitutes a Potential Concern for those product types. The Moneybox ISA (£20,000) and unspecified ISA (£20,000) listed in existing arrangements do corroborate ISA experience to a degree, though these appear in the suitability report's own existing arrangements section rather than a separate evidenced document. The lack of any graded knowledge assessment in the fact find or supporting documents means the knowledge level stated in the report is unsupported by a scored or graded file record. Vulnerability: no vulnerability assessment is present in the file (/vulnerability/assessment is noted as absent in the extraction metadata), so no vulnerability overlay materially affects this check. Overall: the pension experience claim is substantially corroborated by the transaction history and client report; the stocks-and-shares self-directed experience claim is weakly supported and internally inconsistent with the no-understanding statement; and the current accounts/savings/Cash ISA experience claims have no file corroboration beyond the report itself.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report classifies the pension recommendation as 'Complex' (with complexity drivers: SIPP with flexi-access drawdown options; fund switching within pension wrapper; annual allowance and tax relief considerations; IHT implications from April 2027). The ISA and GIA are classified as NonComplex. The rationale for the pension recommendation covers: lower platform costs (0.32% to 0.21%), professional oversight and investment monitoring, adviser charge facilitation, platform integration across pension/ISA/GIA, risk alignment (current funds at risk level 6 are too high for agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile 4), CIP governance (quarterly investment committee, external research), diversification through multi-asset exposure, and performance/volatility comparison. The client is described as having relevant knowledge and experience, though no explicit knowledge grading is quoted in the canonical rationale items.
    File:     The fact find (P20) records the client as Anthony Barnes, with advice areas covering Retirement Planning. The notes (P10, P11) indicate he is retired with DB income, works part-time, has ISAs (Moneybox), and has an existing Aviva pension. P10 notes he 'only ATR 4', confirming a cautious balanced risk profile. The risk profile report (P14, P18) records no additional notes in the investment target or agreed risk level sections, and no notes in the capacity for loss section. The ATRQ was not completed face-to-face — P16 states 'Client did not have time to complete an ATRQ today, so have sent him an email version to complete himself on Defaqto.' The pension switching research report (P9) evidences product and platform selection methodology including comparison with generic stakeholder pension and alternatives. The investment research reports (P12, P13, P15) and ISA/GIA key features documents (P1–P8) evidence product selection for the non-complex wrappers. No passage in the file records any explanation given to the client about the complex features of the pension (flexi-access drawdown, annual allowance/tax relief, IHT implications from 2027), nor any client questions or confirmation of comprehension regarding these features. The fact find contains no section recording the client's knowledge level or investment experience in graded terms.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report classifies the pension as 'Complex' with complexity drivers including flexi-access drawdown options and IHT implications from April 2027, but the file contains no record of client comprehension being tested or confirmed in respect of these complex features — no meeting note, no client questions recorded, no explanation noted in any category C document.
      - The fact find (P16) records that the ATRQ was not completed at the meeting and was sent by email for the client to complete himself; the risk profile report (P14) records no additional notes in the agreed risk level section. There is therefore no face-to-face corroboration of the client's knowledge or experience level.
      - The risk profile report (P14, P18) records no notes in either the investment target or capacity for loss sections, providing no corroborating narrative on knowledge or experience.
      - The rationale items in the report address costs, risk alignment, platform integration and CIP governance but do not explain how the client's knowledge or experience was assessed as sufficient for a product classified as complex, nor do they address the client's understanding of the specific complexity drivers (flexi-access drawdown, IHT from 2027).
      - No passage in the file records an explicit knowledge/experience grade or level for the client against which the complexity of the recommended product can be assessed.
    The pension recommendation is self-classified as Complex in the canonical model, with four identified complexity drivers including flexi-access drawdown options and IHT implications from April 2027. Where a complex product is recommended, the adequacy of the rationale requires not only that the product selection be justified (costs, risk alignment, platform) but also that the client's knowledge and experience be shown to be commensurate with the product's complexity, and ideally that comprehension of those complex features be evidenced. The rationale provided in the report covers legitimate commercial and suitability grounds for the recommendation (lower charges, risk realignment, CIP governance), but it does not address whether the client understands or has experience of the specific complex features — particularly flexi-access drawdown mechanics and the forthcoming IHT treatment of pensions from April 2027. The file holds no category C meeting note, no recorded client questions, and no adviser note confirming that these complex features were explained and understood. The risk profile report records no notes at all in the relevant sections (P14, P18). The fact find records no graded knowledge or experience assessment. The ATRQ was not completed in the meeting (P16). While the research reports (P3, P9) evidence thorough product and platform selection methodology, they do not substitute for evidence of client comprehension of the complexity drivers. The absence of any meeting record or note confirming the client was taken through the complex features of the recommended pension — and understood them — is a gap in the rationale. The severity is Moderate: the product selection rationale itself is present and reasoned, but there is no record of client questions, explanations given, or confirmation of comprehension where the product has unusual/complex features (drawdown access, IHT change). No vulnerability indicators in the file materially affect this finding beyond what is already noted.

CHK-005 — Financial Resilience, Affordability and Liquidity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.6, G5.7, G5.8). Monthly expenditure is stated in the report's 'Your current situation' section as approximately £2,493 (essential) plus £800 discretionary, but the cashflow modelling inputs section within the same report states approximately £2,206 per month — a difference of £287/month that is unexplained. The cashflow model annual expenditure of £29,915 (P7) divided by 12 equates to approximately £2,493/month, which is closer to the higher figure, creating further confusion about which figure was actually used as the modelling input. The extraction flags an ambiguity over whether the £100,000 inheritance had been received at report date (16/12/2025): the narrative says it was 'due this month' implying not yet received, but the balance sheet includes it at full value (£190,000 total). The report's affordability claim rests materially on these inheritance funds. The report states 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' without quantifying current-year earnings, while elsewhere acknowledging uncertainty about this tax year's earnings due to retirement followed by a brief part-time role. This leaves the earnings basis for the £3,600 gross pension contribution unverified in the file evidence.

  [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts that the initial commitment of £2,880 net (£3,600 gross) single pension contribution is affordable from inheritance funds without affecting the client's lifestyle. It further asserts that current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level and that joint expenditure is covered leaving surplus income each month. Monthly expenditure is stated in the 'Your current situation' section as approximately £2,493 (essential) plus £800 discretionary; the cashflow modelling inputs section states approximately £2,206 per month. The report claims affordability is supported by inheritance funds (£100,000 due or received) and a healthy liquid asset base. No ongoing regular contribution commitment is created — the contribution is a single net payment of £2,880.
    File:     The cashflow files (P1, P3, P4, P7) show combined first-year income of £38,800 (Anthony DB pension £22,000 + Yvette salary £16,800) and total regular expenditure of £29,915 in year one, producing a positive cashflow. Liquid assets at outset are shown as £225,018. The cashflow modelling uses an expenditure figure consistent with approximately £2,206/month (£29,915 / 12 ≈ £2,493/month in annual terms per the income statement in P7, but the cashflow inputs section references £2,206/month — see extraction ambiguity). An emergency fund of £10,000 cash is identified as a separate ring-fenced item liquidated last (P3, P4). The cashflow scenarios (base, market crash, Tony predeceases, Yvette predeceases) all show liquid assets remaining positive throughout life. The GIA illustration (P19) confirms the initial adviser charge of £4,794.37 from the single payments. The contribution is a one-off single payment of £2,880 net, not a recurring income commitment.
    Discrepancies:
      - Monthly expenditure is stated in the report's 'Your current situation' section as approximately £2,493 (essential) plus £800 discretionary, but the cashflow modelling inputs section within the same report states approximately £2,206 per month — a difference of £287/month that is unexplained. The cashflow model annual expenditure of £29,915 (P7) divided by 12 equates to approximately £2,493/month, which is closer to the higher figure, creating further confusion about which figure was actually used as the modelling input.
      - The extraction flags an ambiguity over whether the £100,000 inheritance had been received at report date (16/12/2025): the narrative says it was 'due this month' implying not yet received, but the balance sheet includes it at full value (£190,000 total). The report's affordability claim rests materially on these inheritance funds.
      - The report states 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' without quantifying current-year earnings, while elsewhere acknowledging uncertainty about this tax year's earnings due to retirement followed by a brief part-time role. This leaves the earnings basis for the £3,600 gross pension contribution unverified in the file evidence.
      - The cashflow model inputs section references approximately £2,206/month expenditure whereas the first-year expenditure statement (P7) totals £29,915 annually (≈£2,493/month), yet P1 shows Total Money Out in 2025 as £29,915 — the cashflow model appears to use the higher annual figure regardless of the inputs section narrative figure.
    The single most material affordability question here is whether the £2,880 net / £3,600 gross one-off pension contribution and the total initial advice fee of £4,794 are affordable from the client's financial position. The cashflow files (P1, P3, P4, P7) are consistent in showing liquid assets at outset of approximately £225,000, first-year combined income of £38,800 and first-year expenditure of £29,915, leaving a comfortable surplus and substantial liquid reserves. On this basis the one-off contribution presents no affordability challenge. The emergency fund of £10,000 is separately ring-fenced and liquidated last in all scenarios. The first guard applies: the one-off transfer and associated fees are drawn from capital, not from income, and should not be tested against monthly surplus. The ongoing commitment is nil from an income perspective — there is no regular contribution.
    
    However, two concerns remain. First, the expenditure figure inconsistency is a genuine data quality issue: the 'Your current situation' section cites ~£2,493/month essential expenditure (plus £800 discretionary) while the cashflow modelling inputs section cites ~£2,206/month — a £287/month difference that is not explained within the report. The annual cashflow output of £29,915 (P7/P1) is consistent with ~£2,493/month, suggesting the modelling used the higher figure and the '£2,206' inputs reference may be an error or exclude a component, but this is not stated. This is a data quality/consistency issue flagged by the extraction as an internal inconsistency. It does not undermine the affordability conclusion materially — even at £2,493/month the surplus is positive — but it is a disclosure and data quality concern.
    
    Second, the report's earnings sufficiency assertion is unsupported by evidence in the file. The report itself acknowledges uncertainty about current-year earnings and never quantifies them. The contribution could be justified under the £3,600 non-earner allowance, but the report claims it is justified by earnings sufficiency without providing the earnings figure. No fact-find or file note in the evidence pack corroborates a specific current-year earnings figure. This is a weak justification and a missing evidence concern, though it does not affect the affordability conclusion for the one-off contribution itself.
    
    The inheritance ambiguity (£100,000 'due this month' vs already included in the balance sheet) is a data quality concern but does not fundamentally undermine affordability: even without the inheritance the client holds £90,000 liquid plus cashflow-positive income, and the one-off contribution of £2,880 is easily manageable. No vulnerability indicators in the file appear to have bearing on this affordability check specifically. Overall the affordability conclusion is likely sound but the expenditure figure inconsistency and the unquantified earnings basis are material data quality and weak justification concerns that require clarification.

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

(3 days/week), state pensions £11,973 p.a. each from age 67, current expenditure ~£2,206/month.     Discrepancies:
      - 1. Expenditure in cashflow model (~£2,206/month) vs suitability report 'Your current situation' section (£2,493/month essential + ~£800 discretionary): two different expenditure figures within the same report, unexplained. The cashflow models [P20] show first-year total regular expenditure of £29,915 (≈£2,493/month), not £2,206/month — suggesting the cashflow model actually uses the higher figure, contradicting the cashflow inputs narrative which states £2,206/month.
      - 2. Yvette's employment income: suitability report states £28,000 gross p.a. (3 days/week); fact find [P24] records total gross annual earnings of £14,000. The cashflow models [P12, P20] use £16,800 p.a. None of these three figures agree.
      - 3. Emergency fund: fact find [P1] records £10,000 emergency fund (met, no shortfall). The suitability report narrative references £190,000 in liquid assets but does not separately address the adequacy of the emergency fund relative to ongoing commitments or the £10,000 fact find figure.
      - 4. Initial advice fee: suitability report charges section states £4,794; cashflow inputs state £4,794.37 — minor rounding discrepancy internally flagged.
      - 5. State pension of £11,973 p.a. each: not directly corroborated by any fact find or supporting document in the pack.
      - 6. Total assets figure of £669,092: not matched in any supporting document (nearest is £383,679 in [P12]), and the derivation from the canonical model (arrangements summing to £159,092 plus £190,000 cash/savings plus property) is not transparent from the evidence provided.
      - 7. Cashflow first-year 'Total Regular Expenditure' in models (£29,915 per [P20]) annualises to ~£2,493/month, aligning with the 'Your current situation' figure, but the cashflow inputs narrative states £2,206/month — the report and the cashflow model output are internally inconsistent about which expenditure figure was actually used.
    Three material issues arise. First, and most significant, is the conflict over Yvette's employment income. The suitability report states £28,000 gross p.a. (3 days/week). The fact find [P24] records total gross annual earnings of £14,000. The cashflow models use £16,800 p.a. All three figures differ. The cashflow modelling uses a figure (£16,800) that is intermediate between the fact find (£14,000) and the report narrative (£28,000), but none of the three agree. The fact find is the primary client-recorded document; if the correct figure is £14,000 gross, the cashflow inputs are overstated by about 20% and the report narrative by 100%. This is a material data inconsistency that affects the income side of all scenarios and the surplus calculation. The report cannot corroborate itself on this point. Second, the expenditure figure used in modelling is ambiguous. The cashflow inputs section of the report states ~£2,206/month. However, the cashflow model's own first-year 'Total Regular Expenditure' statement across all scenarios [P9, P12, P14, P20] shows £29,915 (i.e. ~£2,493/month), which aligns with the 'Your current situation' section but contradicts the stated cashflow input of £2,206/month. The fact find [P1] records £2,206.67/month as the calculated total, which matches the stated input. The discrepancy between the cashflow model output (£29,915/year) and the stated input (£2,206/month ≈ £26,472/year) is approximately £3,443/year — material, and unexplained. One possible explanation is that the cashflow model includes Tax & NI (£3,435 per [P9]) on top of the £26,472 of other expenditure, which would reconcile the two figures (£26,472 + £3,435 ≈ £29,907 ≈ £29,915). If so, the report's statement that 'current expenditure is approximately £2,206 per month' is misleading because it excludes tax, which is a real outgoing. This is not clearly explained and represents a disclosure shortfall. Third, the emergency fund. The fact find records £10,000 as both the amount held and the amount required [P1]. The suitability report does not separately address whether this £10,000 is preserved within the recommended structure or whether it forms part of the assets being restructured. With cash/savings of £90,000 currently held (plus an expected £100,000 inheritance), the overall liquid position appears strong, but the emergency fund is not separately ring-fenced or discussed in the report. This is a low-level concern. The total assets figure of £669,092 cannot be reconciled from the passages provided; no supporting document shows this total. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability is identified in the file that would alter the conclusions here, but the income discrepancy (Yvette's part-time earnings) is relevant because her income ceases on predeceasing Anthony, a scenario modelled; if her income is overstated in the model, the stress test results would be more favourable than they should be. The cashflow modelling files are on file and show no shortfall under all tested scenarios, which is positive evidence. However, the reliability of those models depends on the accuracy of the income inputs, which is where the material inconsistency lies. The rounding difference on the initial fee (£4,794 vs £4,794.37) is trivial. The guard on growth assumptions between cashflow and provider illustrations is noted but does not affect this income/expenditure comparison.

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states the client holds £10,000 as an emergency fund, confirmed as sufficient (required amount also £10,000, shortfall £0). The report confirms the client has sufficient liquid capital to meet unexpected emergencies.
    File:     The Fact Find [P11] records the emergency fund as £10,000 held and £10,000 required, giving a zero shortfall — consistent with the report. No document in the pack quantifies what the required emergency fund amount should be relative to monthly expenditure (e.g. number of months' expenditure), nor does the report state a required amount beyond repeating the held amount.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report's narrative states monthly essential expenditure of approximately £2,493 plus £800 discretionary, whereas the cashflow modelling inputs and Fact Find [P11] use £2,206.67 per month — a difference of approximately £287/month for essential costs alone, unexplained in the report.
      - The Aviva GIA opening value in the cashflow models [P3, P6] is stated as £52,326, whereas the suitability report's assets table records £57,120 — a difference of approximately £4,794 which coincidentally matches the initial advice fee of £4,794, suggesting the cashflow may have been run on a post-fee basis, but this is not explained in the report.
      - The £60,000 remaining inheritance figure stated in the report is not directly corroborated in the file at that figure; the nearest figure in the passages is unrelated.
      - The emergency fund sufficiency is asserted on a self-confirmatory basis ('you have confirmed') without the report quantifying what amount is required relative to income or expenditure — the required amount equals the held amount (£10,000) per the Fact Find, but no rationale for adequacy of that level is provided given monthly expenditure of £2,206-£2,493.
      - The report states the client has 'significant liquid cash available' to support the 10-15 working day disinvestment timeline, referencing the inheritance proceeds, but the ambiguity over whether the £100,000 inheritance had actually been received at report date (16/12/2025) is unresolved in the file.
    The core emergency fund figure of £10,000 is corroborated by the Fact Find [P11] which records exactly £10,000 held and £10,000 required with zero shortfall. The cashflow models treat the emergency fund as the last asset to be liquidated [P3, P6], preserving it throughout the plan horizon, which is consistent with the report's liquidity narrative. The multiple cashflow scenarios (current situation, market crash [P4], Tony predeceases [P5], Yvette predeceases [P7], higher inflation [P12]) all show positive liquid assets and accessible pensions maintained well into the future, providing broad support for the affordability and resilience conclusions. The planned £40,000 renovation spend is corroborated in the cashflow [P3]. However, three concerns remain. First, the expenditure discrepancy (£2,493+£800 in the report narrative versus £2,206 in the cashflow/Fact Find) is a material internal inconsistency: if the higher figure is correct, the cashflow inputs understate expenditure and the modelled surplus is overstated. This is Moderate severity per the decision logic. Second, the emergency fund sufficiency is asserted entirely on client self-confirmation. The Fact Find records required amount = held amount (£10,000), but no rationale is given for why £10,000 is adequate against monthly outgoings of £2,206-£2,493. At the lower expenditure figure, £10,000 represents approximately 4.5 months of expenditure, which is a defensible level, but the report does not state this reasoning. The guard 'an asserted sufficiency with no quantified requirement cannot be verified — record it as unquantified' applies: this is an unquantified assertion rather than a contradiction, but it remains an unquantified claim. Third, the ambiguity over whether the £100,000 inheritance had been received at report date materially affects the liquidity picture: the report relies on 'significant liquid cash' to justify acceptance of the 10-15 working day disinvestment timeline, but if £100,000 of the £190,000 had not yet arrived, the immediately available cash position is materially different. The GIA value discrepancy (£57,120 in the report versus £52,326 in the cashflow, a difference of ~£4,794 matching the initial fee) suggests the cashflow was run on a post-fee basis, which is actually a conservative and appropriate approach, but it is not explained. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability indicators are identified in the evidence pack that would materially alter this assessment. Overall, the affordability conclusion is broadly supported by the cashflow modelling evidence, but the expenditure input inconsistency and the unresolved inheritance receipt ambiguity represent material unclarified items.

  [G5.4] Initial and ongoing commitments are unaffordable based on income, expenditure and cashflow
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts affordability on three bases: (1) the £2,880 net initial contribution is 'an amount you can comfortably afford from your inheritance funds without affecting your current lifestyle'; (2) current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level; (3) ongoing costs are covered by joint expenditure and leave surplus income each month. The report does not state a quantified monthly surplus figure, and the cashflow modelling section uses £2,206/month expenditure while the 'Your current situation' section states approximately £2,493/month essential expenditure plus £800 discretionary.
    File:     The Fact Find records net monthly income of £2,590.00 (basic employed income £890 net + Aon Final Salary pension £1,700 net), total monthly expenditure of £2,206.67 (from detailed expenditure analysis), giving a monthly disposable income of £383.33. Emergency fund: £10,000 set aside, £10,000 required, shortfall £0. The Fact Find notes the client is expecting £60k of inheritance money 'coming in this month'. Cashflow modelling scenarios (Market Crash, Tony Predeceases, Yvette Predeceases) show liquid assets remaining positive and growing through to late 2050s. The Fact Find records no expected changes in income or expenditure in the foreseeable future. The cashflow model uses £26,480 total annual expenses (approximately £2,207/month), consistent with the Fact Find expenditure figure of £2,206.67/month but inconsistent with the suitability report's 'Your current situation' figure of ~£2,493 essential + £800 discretionary = ~£3,293/month.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report's 'Your current situation' section states essential expenditure of approximately £2,493/month plus £800 discretionary (total ~£3,293/month), whereas the Fact Find and cashflow model both use £2,206–£2,207/month. The difference is £287–£1,087/month depending on whether discretionary is included, and neither the report nor the Fact Find explains the reconciliation.
      - The monthly surplus implied by the Fact Find is £383.33 (income £2,590 minus expenditure £2,206.67). If the suitability report's higher expenditure figure of £2,493 essential alone were used, the surplus would be approximately £97/month, and if discretionary £800 is added the position would be a monthly deficit of approximately £703. The report does not quantify the surplus figure it relies on.
      - The report's assertion that the £2,880 net initial contribution is afforded from inheritance funds is qualitative only; the inheritance of £100,000 is described in the fact find as 'coming in this month' and may not have been received at report date (16/12/2025), creating uncertainty about whether the asset was available at the point of recommendation.
      - The evidenced monthly surplus of £383.33 does not support the ongoing adviser charge of 1% p.a. (approximately £571/month based on total invested assets of ~£159,000+) from regular income alone — though the report does not assert that ongoing charges are met from income, only that surplus income exists each month. The basis on which ongoing product and adviser charges are to be met is not stated explicitly in the report's affordability section.
      - The initial commitment figure of £2,880 net is not corroborated by any passage within 1% (nearest figure is £2,999.67 in the Fact Find, which is the monthly income total, 4.2% away). No passage confirms £2,880 as a separate stated figure.
    The core affordability question has two parts: (a) the initial £2,880 net contribution, and (b) ongoing commitments relative to surplus income.
    
    (a) Initial contribution: The report states this comes from inheritance funds. The Fact Find records £100,000 inheritance 'coming in this month' and the ambiguity note flags uncertainty whether it had been received at report date. If received, the £2,880 is easily afforded from a £100,000 lump sum and liquidity is not impaired. The emergency fund is fully funded (£10,000 set aside, £10,000 required, no shortfall per P2). The cashflow scenarios all show liquid assets remaining positive and growing. This part of the affordability assertion is broadly supported, subject to the timing ambiguity on the inheritance.
    
    (b) Ongoing surplus: The Fact Find evidences a monthly surplus of £383.33 (income £2,590, expenditure £2,206.67 per P2). The cashflow model uses the same ~£2,207/month expenditure figure (P16: total regular expenses £26,480/year = £2,207/month). The report's 'Your current situation' section states ~£2,493 essential + £800 discretionary = ~£3,293/month, which would produce a deficit of approximately £703/month — an irreconcilable difference from both the Fact Find and cashflow model. The extraction flags this as an internal inconsistency. The report asserts 'surplus income each month' without quantifying it. The basis used in the cashflow model (£2,206.67) supports a modest surplus of £383/month; the basis stated in the report narrative (~£3,293) does not. This is a material discrepancy between the report's own narrative and the supporting evidence.
    
    The ongoing adviser charge of 1% p.a. on approximately £159,000+ of assets equates to roughly £1,590/year or £133/month. Combined with product charges this is a meaningful ongoing cost, but the report's affordability narrative is silent on how ongoing charges are funded (income surplus vs. investment returns). The cashflow modelling does account for ongoing fees (P16 references 1% ongoing financial planning fee), so the cashflow model implicitly addresses this, even if the suitability report's affordability narrative does not explicitly do so.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence pack that would materially alter this assessment beyond the general point that a client near or at retirement (target retirement age 60) with modest income surplus needs careful affordability analysis — which the inconsistent expenditure figures make harder to assess confidently.
    
    The expenditure inconsistency (£2,206 vs ~£3,293 in the report narrative, or even £2,493 essential-only vs £2,206) is a genuine data inconsistency between the suitability report's own narrative and the Fact Find/cashflow inputs. It is not explained in any document. The report asserts surplus income exists but does not quantify it, and the figure used in cashflow modelling differs materially from what the report's own 'Your current situation' section states. This is a Moderate concern under the severity guidance (cashflow inputs differ from fact find figures).

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report's canonical model records that liquidity needs are funded from 'Inheritance proceeds' (twice) and 'Emergency fund (cash)'. The emergency fund shortfall path is absent from the extracted model — the extraction metadata records this as NotPresentInDocument, meaning the report makes no explicit statement about whether the emergency fund is sufficient or quantified. The report does assert that the emergency fund exists as a cash source.
    File:     The cashflow model files (P1, P2, P3) consistently show a 'Cash Emergency Fund £10,000' liquidated 8th (last) in the drawdown order, behind the Surplus Account (1st), Chartered Savings Account £40,000 (2nd), Aviva ISA £20,000 (5th), Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA £20,000 (6th), and Aviva GIA ~£52,326 (7th). The emergency fund is therefore retained throughout most of the plan horizon and only drawn upon after all other liquid assets are exhausted. It is not depleted by the recommendation itself. Its value is £10,000 across all cashflow scenarios. No cashflow scenario shows the emergency fund being liquidated before the end of the plan. The cashflow files do not contain any narrative quantifying whether £10,000 is sufficient relative to the clients' expenditure level; the figure simply appears as an asset in the liquidation schedule.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not quantify or justify the adequacy of the £10,000 emergency fund (e.g., by reference to monthly expenditure or a months-of-expenditure benchmark) — the cashflow files record its existence and value but contain no such assessment either.
      - Monthly expenditure in the report is internally inconsistent between the 'Your current situation' section (~£2,493 essential + £800 discretionary = ~£3,293/month) and the cashflow modelling inputs (~£2,206/month); the £10,000 emergency fund represents between approximately 3 months (at £3,293) and 4.5 months (at £2,206) of expenditure, but neither calculation is stated anywhere in the file.
      - The cashflow files show the emergency fund liquidated 8th and last — after all other liquid assets are consumed — meaning it is structurally the least accessible reserve, yet it is labelled an 'emergency fund'. No commentary explains whether this ordering is intentional or whether the clients can access it out of sequence in a genuine emergency without following the modelled drawdown order.
    The emergency fund of £10,000 exists in the file and is evidenced across all cashflow scenarios (P1, P2, P3) at a consistent value. It is not depleted by the recommendation — the pension switch is a transfer between wrappers and does not consume cash savings, and the guard confirms that a wrapper-to-wrapper transfer does not deplete an emergency fund. The ISA and GIA contributions are funded from inheritance proceeds per the canonical model, not from emergency cash. So the fund is not consumed by the recommendation itself.
    
    However, the requirement asks whether the emergency fund is 'taken into account' in the suitability assessment in terms of adequacy. The file records the fund's existence and its position in the liquidation order, but neither the suitability report nor the cashflow files contain any narrative or calculation confirming that £10,000 is sufficient for the clients' circumstances. At the lower cashflow expenditure figure of ~£2,206/month, £10,000 covers approximately 4.5 months; at the higher narrative figure of ~£3,293/month it covers approximately 3 months. Neither figure nor assessment appears anywhere in the evidence pack.
    
    Further, the cashflow liquidation order places the emergency fund last (8th) — it is the least liquid of all modelled assets in the plan sequence, which is structurally the opposite of what an emergency fund should be. No commentary in any document addresses whether the clients can access this fund independently of the modelled drawdown order in a true emergency. This is not a case where the fund is absent; it is a case where it is on file but its adequacy is never assessed and its accessibility in practice (vs. the modelled order) is never addressed.
    
    The internal expenditure inconsistency (£2,493 vs £2,206/month) noted in the extraction metadata is relevant here because it affects the calculation of how many months the emergency fund covers, but since neither calculation is performed in the file, this compounds rather than resolves the concern.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability flags are present in the extracted model (the vulnerability/assessment path is absent per extraction metadata), so no vulnerability-specific uplift to this finding is required.
    
    This is a Low finding: the emergency fund is asserted as sufficient (implicitly, by the report treating it as adequate) but never quantified against expenditure, which is the paradigm Low severity case per the severity guidance.

  [G5.6] Liquidity mismatch with access needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The recommended wrappers are SIPP, GIA and ISA. The selected retirement age for the SIPP is 60. The canonical model does not record any specific liquidity or access need dates (/financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds[]/requiredFrom is absent) nor any planned withdrawals from the SIPP (/solution/recommendedPlans[]/plannedWithdrawals[] is absent).
    File:     The cashflow modelling (Current Situation scenario, [P19]) shows the household in surplus through to age 60 (2030), at which point Yvette's employment income ceases and a deficit of approximately £7,101 per year arises from age 60 to 62, met by drawings from liquid assets and accessible pensions. Anthony is born 06/01/1970 (age 55 at plan date per [P14]); his State Pension age is 67 ([P14]). The cashflow model classifies pension assets as 'accessible' once minimum pension age is reached ([P12], [P13]). The current minimum pension access age is 55 (rising to 57 in 2028). Anthony is already 55 and will be 57 before the 2028 rise, so his pension is accessible throughout. The GIA and ISA are fully liquid at all times ([P20], [P6]). No specific lump-sum access need is evidenced in the passages beyond what the cashflow model captures. The inheritance of £100,000 is noted as an ambiguity in the extraction but is included in the £190,000 liquid assets figure used in the cashflow model. The cashflow modelling uses monthly expenditure of approximately £2,206 while the suitability report narrative states £2,493 essential plus £800 discretionary — a £287/month discrepancy flagged in the extraction as an internal inconsistency.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report's 'Your current situation' section states monthly essential expenditure of approximately £2,493 (plus £800 discretionary) but the cashflow modelling inputs use approximately £2,206 per month — a difference of £287/month that is unexplained and material to the surplus/deficit calculation underpinning affordability.
      - The SIPP retirement age is stated as 60 in the canonical model, but no explicit access need at age 60 is documented in the passages — the cashflow model deficit beginning at age 60 (when Yvette's employment stops) is met from liquid assets and accessible pensions, but the basis on which the pension is treated as 'accessible' at age 60 is not explicitly stated in the suitability report itself.
      - The £190,000 liquid assets figure in the balance sheet may overstate currently available cash if the £100,000 inheritance had not yet been received at report date (16/12/2025), yet the cashflow model uses it at full value — the extraction flags this as unresolved.
    The core liquidity mismatch test — whether the recommended wrappers restrict access during a period when the client needs funds — does not produce a concern on the pension access age point. Anthony is 55 at the plan date (born 06/01/1970) and the minimum pension access age rises to 57 only in 2028; the report's selected retirement age of 60 is comfortably above both the current and future minimum access age. The pension is therefore accessible before any evidenced need arises. The GIA and ISA carry no access restrictions. The access-age guard in the instructions confirms that pension access restrictions are irrelevant where the client is already at or above the minimum pension age, and Anthony is already 55. No liquidity mismatch on access timing arises.
    
    However, two affordability concerns remain. First, the expenditure figure used in the cashflow model (approximately £2,206/month) is materially lower than the figure stated in the suitability report narrative (approximately £2,493 essential + £800 discretionary). The cashflow model is the primary tool used to demonstrate that the recommendation is affordable and that reserves remain adequate; if the higher expenditure figure is the correct one, the modelled surpluses and the adequacy of liquid reserves are overstated. The extraction identifies this as an internal inconsistency across paths /financialPosition/expenditure/monthlyEssential and /cashflowModelling/inputs/monthlyExpenditure. This is a data inconsistency that directly bears on the financial resilience conclusion and is not explained in the suitability report. Second, the inheritance ambiguity means the starting liquid assets in the model may be overstated by £100,000, which would reduce the modelled buffer further. Neither concern rises to the level of capital being inaccessible, but the unexplained expenditure discrepancy of £287/month (approximately £3,444/year) is material relative to the annual deficit of £7,052–£7,101 shown in the cashflow model at ages 60–62 — it could turn a modest surplus in earlier years into an earlier deficit, or deepen the already-present deficit at age 60. This lands as a Moderate concern under the severity guidance (cashflow inputs differ from the fact find figures).

      - The cashflow model first-year income statement ([P9]) shows Yvette's employment income as £16,800 (3-day pro-rata), not £28,000; the report's 'Your current situation' section states £28,000 full-time, while the cashflow analysis section references £28,000 pro-rata for 3 days — these are internally consistent descriptions of the same income on different bases, but the £16,800 figure used in the cashflow is not explicitly stated in the suitability report's income table, only implied.
      - No independent third-party evidence (e.g. State Pension forecast, HMRC letter, BR19) corroborates the £11,973 State Pension figure for either client; the figure appears only in the suitability report and the cashflow model prepared by the same adviser.
    The report's State Pension figure of £11,973 p.a. from age 67 for both clients is internally corroborated by the cashflow model ([P1], [P2]), but the cashflow model is adviser-prepared supporting documentation and not an independent source. Critically, the fact find State Pension section ([P4]) is entirely blank — no BR19 projection, no basic or additional State Pension amounts, no State Pension retirement age — meaning there is no independently sourced corroboration of the entitlement figure for either client. Yvette's State Pension is confirmed in [P1] at £11,973 from age 67, consistent with the report. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability drivers identified in the evidence materially affect this specific finding. The core concern is the absence of any State Pension forecast or entitlement statement on file for either client, leaving the full State Pension claim unsupported by independent evidence.

  [G5.8] Planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) Yvette intends to reduce to 3 days/week from next year; (2) Yvette plans to fully retire when Mr Barnes turns 60; (3) both expect full State Pension of £11,973 p.a. from age 67; (4) a £100,000 inheritance from the late father's estate is due 'this month', with ~£40,000 earmarked for renovations and £60,000 to invest; (5) an emergency fund of £10,000 is retained for instant access; (6) a shortfall period is anticipated when Yvette retires until her State Pension begins at age 67; (7) from 6 April 2027 unused DC pensions will be included in the taxable estate; (8) pension minimum access age rises to 57 from 6 April 2028; (9) monthly essential expenditure is approximately £2,493 (per 'Your current situation' narrative) but the cashflow modelling inputs use approximately £2,206/month; (10) disposal of £60,000 inheritance split across GIA/ISA/pension contributions; (11) 10–15 working days disinvestment access timeframe accepted.
    File:     The Fact Find (P2, P14, P15, P20) records: total monthly household expenditure of £2,206.67; total net monthly income £2,590.00; monthly disposable income £383.33; emergency fund of £10,000 held and required (shortfall £0); assets including cash 'Incoming funds from sale of Dad's property' £100,000 (Fact Find dated 06/08/2025), Chartered Savings Account £40,000, MoneyBox Cash ISA £20,000, Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA £20,000, Cash Emergency Fund £10,000; holidays £5,000 annually and entertainment £800/month recorded as non-essential expenditure; no expected income or expenditure changes recorded in the Fact Find. (≈£2,493/month); retirement event for Anthony at age 67 in 2037; State Pension age 67 modelled. The Fact Find notes (P17) confirm retirement goal by age 60, minimum income in retirement £2,200 net/month, £60,000 from father's property plus £40,000 cash for investment/tax efficiency purposes. The cashflow modelling assumptions (P10, P12) show general inflation 2%, State Pension inflation 2.5%, 0% surplus saved. Multiple stress scenarios modelled (market crash, higher inflation, predeceasing scenarios) all showing no red/shortfall bars.
    Discrepancies:
      - Monthly expenditure figure used in the cashflow modelling inputs (£2,206/month per Fact Find P2 and the report's own cashflow inputs section) differs from the monthly equivalent of the first-year cashflow expenditure statement (£29,915/12 ≈ £2,493/month per P13), a difference of approximately £287/month. The report itself flags this ambiguity but does not resolve which figure was used for suitability assessment.
      - The Fact Find (P14) records no expected changes in income in the foreseeable future, yet the suitability report asserts Yvette will reduce to 3 days/week from next year and retire when Mr Barnes turns 60 — these are foreseeable income changes not captured in the Fact Find's formal income-changes field.
      - The Fact Find (P14) records no expected changes in expenditure in the foreseeable future, yet the suitability report identifies the £40,000 renovation spend as a foreseeable liquidity call — not captured in the Fact Find expenditure-changes field.
      - The inheritance asset is described in the Fact Find (P15) as 'Incoming funds from sale of Dad's property' valued at £100,000 (dated 06/08/2025), whereas the suitability report describes it as 'inheritance from your late father's estate (due this month)' at report date 16/12/2025 — the Fact Find appears to predate the report by approximately four months, raising a question about whether the inheritance was already received or still pending at the advice date.
      - The cashflow modelling (P13) shows Anthony's income in year one as DB Pension £22,000 p.a. + Yvette employment £16,800 p.a. = £38,800 total, whereas the Fact Find (P14) shows total net monthly income of £2,590 (£31,080 p.a.), a difference of £7,720 p.a. — the basis difference (gross cashflow vs net fact find) is a potential explanation but is not stated.
      - No evidence in the file corroborates the specific State Pension figure of £11,973 p.a. for each client — the cashflow models include a State Pension age of 67 but do not confirm the annual amount per person.
      - The Fact Find (P16/P17) notes minimum retirement income of £2,200 net/month, consistent with the report's narrative, but the formal income-changes and expenditure-changes fields record 'No' — the planned reduction in Yvette's hours and her future retirement are not formally captured as expected changes.
    The most material discrepancy is the unexplained £287/month difference between the expenditure figure in the cashflow modelling inputs section (£2,206/month, matching the Fact Find total) and the first-year cashflow expenditure statement (£29,915/year ≈ £2,493/month, matching the report narrative). The extraction ambiguity flags this directly. The cashflow modelling expenditure statement (P13) shows the higher figure as the sum of individual line items (Household £8,760 + Transport £2,640 + Miscellaneous £480 + Entertainment & Leisure £9,600 + Holidays £5,000 + Tax & NI £3,435 = £29,915), while the Fact Find and the cashflow inputs section use £2,206.67/month. The difference appears to be that the Fact Find records only cash outgoings the client identified in the detailed expenditure analysis, whereas the cashflow model adds holidays (£5,000 p.a.) and entertainment (£800/month) which appear in the Fact Find as non-essential expenditure (P20) but may not have been summed into the £2,206.67 total shown in the Fact Find's calculated total. However, the Fact Find's 'Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure' of £2,206.67 (P2) is the figure the suitability assessment formally relies on, and it is lower than what the cashflow model actually runs. This is a data inconsistency in the inputs used for the cashflow modelling relative to the formal fact find total — the report does not explain the difference. The foreseeable changes (Yvette's hours reduction, renovation spend, retirement) are asserted in the report narrative and are consistent with the Fact Find notes (P17) and the cashflow stress-test scenarios, but the formal Fact Find fields for income and expenditure changes record 'No', meaning the formal record does not capture these known foreseeable changes. The emergency fund is consistently recorded at £10,000 in both the report and the Fact Find (P2, P15), with no shortfall, which is satisfactory. The cashflow modelling (P1, P3, P4, P6, P7, P8, P13) demonstrates that across multiple stress scenarios the plan remains sustainable with no red bars, supporting the report's conclusion that the shortfall period when Yvette retires is manageable. The inheritance timing ambiguity (Fact Find dated August 2025 vs report dated December 2025) is noted but does not change the substantive picture, as both documents treat the £100,000 as available for the purpose of the advice. The State Pension figure of £11,973 p.a. is stated in the report but not confirmed by any document in the file. On vulnerability: no vulnerability assessment is present in the file (noted as absent in the extraction), so no vulnerability overlay applies to this specific check. Overall, the primary concern is the unexplained discrepancy between the two expenditure figures used within the same report, combined with foreseeable income changes not formally recorded in the Fact Find.

CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 9 requirement(s). 9 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.3, G6.4, G6.5, G6.6, G6.7, G6.8, G6.9). The canonical model records no alternatives considered with reasons for rejection across any of the five recommendation groups; the check requires alternatives to be presented with reasons for rejection, and no passage in the case file evidences any alternatives section in the suitability report or supporting documents. The Policy Research file [P8] has blank cells for 'Alternative Strategies', 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' and 'Why/Why isn't this scheme suitable for the client?' — fields that should corroborate the recommendation rationale but are unpopulated. The ISA research report [P6] states 'No additional justification text has been entered' for the recommended product, and the shortlist contained only 1 product from 168 after filters — no genuine comparison of alternatives is documented. 5 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report provides rationale for five recommendation groups covering: (1) pension platform transfer to TFAS agency on Aviva Platform — reducing platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%, gaining ongoing oversight, enabling adviser charge deduction, and consolidating onto one platform; (2) pension fund switch from Vanguard LifeStrategy mix (risk level 6) to Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II (risk level 4) — matching agreed Cautious Balanced profile, citing performance analysis showing lower volatility and more consistent performance, noting client preference for simplicity; (3) £3,600 gross pension contribution from inheritance — citing earnings uncertainty, minimum allowance basis, affordability, and immediate tax relief; (4) GIA on Aviva Platform — offering capital growth, CGT planning via Bed & ISA, and administrative consolidation; (5) ISA transfer from Moneybox Cash ISA to Aviva Platform ISA — citing variable 4.5% cash rate versus 4.72% average 5-year fund performance, tax efficiency, and platform consolidation. Rationale is linked to objectives OBJ1/OBJ4/OBJ6 (recommendations 1–2), OBJ5/OBJ3 (recommendation 3), OBJ2/OBJ3/OBJ4 (recommendations 4–5). Several rationale bullets are marked isClientSpecific=false (adviser charge deduction mechanics, CIP description, tax-efficient ISA wrapper). No alternatives considered section is evidenced in the report's canonical rationale fields. The report asserts the recommended Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II provides 'more consistent performance aligned with Risk Level 4' and 'lower volatility than the current Vanguard mix'.
    File:     Research files confirm: (1) A pension switch report [P4] confirms existing Aviva Life & Pensions pension (AV2075926) with value £79,092.32 and 0.32% platform charge and 4-year horizon to retirement age 60. (2) A Personal Pension Plan research report [P2] and pension switch report [P4] were conducted dated 03/12/2025 and 16/12/2025 respectively, evidencing research activity. (3) An Investment ISA research report [P3] confirms the risk profile 4 — Cautious Balanced — description and an Aviva ISA Portfolio was the recommended product from a shortlist of 1 out of 168, using filters including Bed and ISA availability [P6]. (4) A GIA research report [P5] was conducted. (5) A Personal Pension Plan review report [P7] was conducted. (6) A Policy Research file [P8] exists but its 'Overall File Strategy' and scheme-level rationale cells (Summary of the Advice, Alternative Strategies, Justification for fees, Which objective/goal is this achieving, Why/Why isn't this scheme suitable) are all blank. (7) Performance data in the suitability report itself [P14] shows the current Vanguard strategy (A) at 45.28% cumulative 5-year vs Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II (C) at 30.12% — current funds outperformed — with the report acknowledging this but arguing lower volatility justifies the switch. (8) The ISA research [P3/P6] shortlisted 1 product from 168 with no additional justification text entered. (9) The Client Agreement [P12] confirms initial fee of 3% = £4,794 and 1% ongoing. (10) The risk profile report [P10] records no additional notes in the investment target or agreed risk level sections. (11) No alternatives considered or rejected with reasons are evidenced in any retrieved passage.
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model records no alternatives considered with reasons for rejection across any of the five recommendation groups; the check requires alternatives to be presented with reasons for rejection, and no passage in the case file evidences any alternatives section in the suitability report or supporting documents.
      - The Policy Research file [P8] has blank cells for 'Alternative Strategies', 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' and 'Why/Why isn't this scheme suitable for the client?' — fields that should corroborate the recommendation rationale but are unpopulated.
      - The ISA research report [P6] states 'No additional justification text has been entered' for the recommended product, and the shortlist contained only 1 product from 168 after filters — no genuine comparison of alternatives is documented.
      - The Personal Pension Plan research report [P2] shortlisted 1 product; no evidence of alternatives compared or rejected with reasons.
      - The risk profile report [P10] records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both the investment target discussion and the agreed risk level discussion, meaning the personalised basis for agreeing risk level 4 is undocumented in the supporting file.
      - Three rationale bullets are marked isClientSpecific=false by the extraction: adviser charge deduction mechanics, CIP/quarterly investment committee description, and the generic ISA tax-efficiency statement — these appear verbatim-templated rather than client-specific.
      - The performance rationale asserts the recommended fund provides 'more consistent performance aligned with Risk Level 4' but the performance table in [P14] shows the current strategy outperformed (45.28% vs 30.12% over 5 years) — the report acknowledges this but the rationale in the canonical model does not explicitly qualify this outperformance acknowledgement in the rationale bullets themselves.
    The suitability report contains a reasonable volume of rationale linked to named objectives, and several bullets are genuinely client-specific (named figures, named funds, client-stated preferences, specific charge comparison). The fund switch rationale is supported by performance and volatility analysis in [P14], which correctly acknowledges that the current funds outperformed whilst arguing the switch is justified on risk-alignment and volatility grounds — this is a coherent, if arguable, position and is not a contradiction. The charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21% platform charge is corroborated by [P4]. The Bed & ISA facility is confirmed as available by [P6]. The pension switch report [P4] and research reports [P2][P5][P7] confirm research was conducted. However, the most significant gap is the complete absence of alternatives considered and rejected with reasons across all five recommendation groups. The Policy Research file [P8] had dedicated fields for 'Alternative Strategies' and scheme-level justification that are entirely blank. The ISA research [P6] and pension research [P2] each returned a single shortlisted product with no justification text, meaning no genuine comparative analysis is documented anywhere in the file. Under the decision logic, absence of alternatives with reasons is a Potential Concern at High severity. The three generic rationale bullets (adviser charge mechanics, CIP description, ISA wrapper tax efficiency) are boilerplate but occur alongside specific bullets — this does not render the overall rationale wholly generic, so it is a lower-order concern. The blank risk profile notes [P10] mean the personalised basis for agreeing risk level 4 is undocumented. Vulnerability: no vulnerability assessment is on file (noted as absent in extraction metadata), so no vulnerability overlay affects this finding. The primary finding is the absence of documented alternatives with reasons, supported by the blank Policy Research fields and single-item shortlists with no justification text.
