  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report recommends the Aviva Platform as provider, citing: FTSE 100-listed status, security and flexibility, 6,900+ funds, flexible ISA, competitive charges, online tools, and — the only client-specific rationale — that keeping pension/ISA/GIA on one platform provides administrative convenience and a single point of contact (linked to OBJ4). For the investment strategy, the report states funds reflect risk profile (OBJ1), provide diversification, blend active and passive, keep investment simple (OBJ4), have competitive charges, have performed well against peers, and are part of the CIP governed by a quarterly investment committee. The report describes five options considered for the pension (leave untouched; switch funds internally; switch to another existing arrangement; switch to employer scheme; set up new arrangement) and states internal fund switches are recommended in the client's best interests. The recommended funds are Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II (pension/GIA) and CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc (ISA/GIA). The provider selection rationale items flagged isClientSpecific=false for five of six bullets; the sixth (platform integration) is client-specific. The investment rationale items flagged isClientSpecific=false for five of eight bullets; three are client-specific. Financial strength is stated as Aviva rated A (Stable) by S&P with £407bn AUM.
    File:     Research documents on file: (1) Personal Pension Plan research report (03-12-2025) — Defaqto brochure comparison for Aviva Platform pension showing platform features; no DNA analysis carried out; no additional platform justification text entered; panel stated as TFAS panel; no template used. (2) Personal Pension Plan review report (03-12-2025) — single product result: Pension Portfolio - Choice, Aviva, no DNA applied. (3) Pension Switching research report (16-12-2025) — agreed risk level 4 Cautious Balanced; investment £79,092; risk-rated multi-asset fund at level 4 selected; combined performance table vs Risk Level 4 benchmark. (4) Investment ISA research report (03-12-2025) — agreed risk level 4; 100% CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc; fund costs 0.29% ongoing + 0.22% transaction = 0.51% total. (5) GIA research report (03-12-2025) — agreed risk level 4; CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc; same fund costs. Performance comparison in suitability report (P2) shows current strategy (A) cumulative 5yr return 45.28% vs recommended Aviva Pen Multi-asset Plus II (C) 30.12%, with report noting recommended fund has lower volatility than existing Vanguard LifeStrategy blend (risk level 6 vs agreed level 4). The research reports show Aviva Platform was the recommended platform but record 'No additional justification text has been entered' and 'No DNA analysis was carried out'. The five alternative options for the pension are listed in the report. No separate alternatives-considered document for the ISA or GIA wrapper/provider is in evidence. Search Q6.2.5 returned nothing.
    Discrepancies:
      - The platform selection research report records 'No additional justification text has been entered' and 'No Product DNA analysis was carried out', meaning the research tool contains no documented comparative rationale for selecting Aviva Platform over alternatives — the report's provider rationale is asserted in the suitability letter itself without corroboration from the research file.
      - Five of six provider-selection rationale items and five of eight investment-strategy rationale items are flagged isClientSpecific=false, meaning the bulk of the stated justification is generic/boilerplate rather than client-specific.
      - The report states funds 'have performed well in comparison with their peers' and charges 'are competitive', but no peer-comparison evidence for the pension or GIA fund (Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II) is provided in the retrieved passages; search Q6.2.5 returned nothing. The ISA/GIA CT Universal MAP fund has a research report but no fund-level peer comparison table is provided for the pension fund.
      - The report's five-option consideration is present for the pension only; no alternatives-considered section is evidenced for the ISA or GIA wrapper/product recommendations.
      - The report states the Aviva Platform ISA 'allows withdrawals and replacements in the same tax year' as a provider rationale item but this is a generic product feature, not linked to any client-specific ISA flexibility need.
      - The report claims the existing funds carry 'risk level 6' throughout the main body, but the alternatives section states one fund is risk profile 5 and the other risk profile 7 — an internal inconsistency noted in the extraction (though a weighted average of 6 is arithmetically consistent, the report uses '6' everywhere without explaining the blended basis).
    The report provides a structured recommendation rationale covering platform, investment strategy and fund selection. It documents five alternatives considered for the pension switch, with a stated conclusion that internal changes are in the client's best interests. Research documents are on file and confirm the agreed risk level (4, Cautious Balanced) for all three products and the fund selection for ISA and GIA. The pension switching research confirms risk level alignment and investment amount. This partial corroboration is meaningful.
    
    However, several concerns arise:
    
    1. PROVIDER SELECTION: The Defaqto platform research report explicitly records 'No additional justification text has been entered' and 'No Product DNA analysis was carried out' [P3, P17]. The TFAS panel is referenced as the basis, which under the CIP/panel guard is a legitimate justification — but the report does not itself invoke the panel process as the reason for provider selection; instead it lists a series of absolute marketing claims (FTSE 100 listing, fund range, online tools) that are not comparative. The one genuinely client-specific rationale (platform integration for OBJ4) is present and plausible, but the bulk of the provider justification is generic.
    
    2. INVESTMENT RATIONALE: The claim that funds 'have performed well in comparison with their peers' is asserted but no peer-comparison evidence for the pension fund (Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II) is in the pack; Q6.2.5 returned nothing. The performance comparison in [P2] shows the recommended pension fund underperforming the client's existing strategy over 5 years (30.12% vs 45.28%), with the report's justification being lower volatility — which is a valid risk-alignment argument, but the 'performed well against peers' assertion is not supported by any document.
    
    3. ALTERNATIVES FOR ISA AND GIA: The five-option framework applies only to the pension. No alternatives-considered section for the ISA transfer or GIA establishment is evidenced. The research reports confirm product selection but do not document rejected alternatives for these wrappers.
    
    4. GENERIC LANGUAGE: The majority of rationale items are flagged as non-client-specific. While some generic justification is acceptable in a CIP context, the balance here tips toward boilerplate — particularly for the ISA and GIA recommendations which carry no client-specific rationale beyond the platform integration point shared across all three products.
    
    5. RISK LEVEL INCONSISTENCY: The '6 vs 5+7' internal inconsistency (noted in extraction) does not materially affect the recommendation rationale for CHK-006 because the core conclusion (existing holdings exceed agreed risk level 4) is supported by the research evidence. This is noted but does not drive the outcome here.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: No vulnerability assessment is recorded in the extraction (path absent). No vulnerability-related communication or support needs are evidenced in the retrieved passages that would materially affect the recommendation rationale assessment.
    
    Overall: The recommendation rationale is present and partially client-specific, with some corroboration from research documents. However, the provider selection lacks documented comparative justification, the 'performed well against peers' claim for the pension fund is unsupported, and alternatives for the ISA and GIA recommendations are absent. These are material gaps that constitute a Potential Concern at Moderate severity — rationale is present but substantially generic across recommendations, and the peer-performance assertion is contradicted by the available performance data (which shows underperformance vs existing, justified only on volatility grounds).

  [G6.3] Personalised language linked to individual circumstances
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The canonical model asserts that the majority of rationale entries are client-specific (isClientSpecific = true for 13 of 18 rationale items; false for 5). The report claims personalised language is used throughout, linking recommendations to Anthony Barnes's individual circumstances: retirement by age 60, minimum net income of £2,200/month in retirement, tax-efficiency on inherited property proceeds, existing Aviva pension review, ISA transfer consideration, GIA for taxable investment, and a small personal pension contribution. Alternatives are stated to have been considered and rejected in an alternatives section. The switch from existing Aviva pension to new Aviva Platform Personal Pension is justified on improved fund choice and cost comparison (new plan 0.08% more expensive but justified by better alignment). The five rationale items flagged as not client-specific (isClientSpecific = false) are acknowledged within the model itself.
    File:     The fact find [P4] records the client's objectives as: review Aviva pension and improve upon it; wants to be fully retired by 60; minimum income in retirement £2,200 net per month; investment advice on £60k from late father's property sale and £40k cash to avoid paying tax on interest; potentially interested in transferring cash ISA to S&S ISA but not committed. [P13] (policy research) records the same objectives. [P6] confirms target age 60 and £2,200/month income target. [P14] (pension switching research) contains a brochure comparison of the existing and recommended pension. The fact find [P2] notes 'Client did not have time to complete an ATRQ today, so have sent him an email version to complete himself on Defaqto' — meaning the ATR questionnaire was not completed at the meeting and may not have been completed before advice was given. The extraction ambiguities note uncertainty about whether the ISA transfer recommendation was personalised and whether the pension contribution basis (earnings vs non-earner allowance) was confirmed. The extraction flags 5 of 18 rationale items as not client-specific. The extraction also flags an internal inconsistency: the switch charges assessment implies the new pension annual charge is £490 (£427 + £63), but the annual recurring charges table states £512.69 — a £22.69 discrepancy that appears in the rationale supporting the pension switch recommendation. Additionally, the alternative solutions section states 'one fund is aligned to a risk profile of 5 and the other to a risk profile of 7' while the main report describes the existing funds as risk level 6 — the alternatives rationale uses risk level descriptions inconsistent with the main body.
    Discrepancies:
      - 5 of 18 rationale items are flagged as not client-specific (isClientSpecific = false) within the canonical model itself, indicating the report itself acknowledges generic language in those sections.
      - The switch charges assessment implies a new pension annual charge of £490 (£427 existing + £63 increase), but the annual recurring charges table for the Aviva Platform Personal Pension states £512.69 — a £22.69 difference that undermines the cost comparison rationale used to justify the pension switch.
      - The alternatives section describes one existing fund as risk profile 5 and the other as risk profile 7, while the main recommendations body consistently describes the existing arrangement as risk level 6 — an internal inconsistency in the risk-based rationale for alternatives rejection.
      - The ATRQ was not completed at the meeting (fact find [P2] records it was sent for the client to complete himself) — it is not confirmed in the pack that it was received and reviewed before the advice was given, which calls into question whether the risk profile underpinning the investment rationale was established from a completed questionnaire.
      - The report's rationale for the pension contribution (REC3) states current earnings are sufficient but does not quantify them; the extraction flags ambiguity about whether this is based on confirmed earnings or the non-earner £3,600 allowance — leaving the earnings basis of the contribution rationale unsupported by documentary evidence in the pack.
      - The ISA transfer rationale acknowledges the client was 'not dead set' on transfer (per fact find [P4]) yet the recommendation proceeds — the report's personalised framing of this as meeting the client's objective requires stronger linkage to the stated conditional preference.
    The report does contain substantial personalised language tied to Anthony Barnes's stated objectives — retirement at 60, £2,200/month net income, tax-efficiency on inherited proceeds, pension review — and these are corroborated by the fact find [P4] and policy research [P13]. For the majority of recommendations the client-specific linkage is present and supported. However, several concerns arise. First, 5 of 18 rationale items are acknowledged as non-client-specific within the canonical model itself; while individually these may be standard regulatory caveats, the pattern across five items suggests sections of generic boilerplate remain. Second, the monetary cost comparison underpinning the pension switch rationale contains a demonstrable internal inconsistency: the switch charges table implies a new annual charge of £490 while the charges table states £512.69 — a £22.69 discrepancy. The rationale states the plan must outperform by £63 in year one, but this figure is derived from the £490 implied figure, not the £512.69 stated figure; on the higher figure the required outperformance would be approximately £85.69. This is not a minor rounding difference: it means the cost-benefit rationale for the pension switch, which is a replacement business recommendation, uses an incorrect baseline and therefore the stated hurdle rate is understated. This is a data inconsistency materially affecting the recommendation rationale. Third, the ATRQ was not completed at the meeting and the pack does not contain confirmation it was returned and reviewed before the suitability report was issued (16/12/2025 versus fact-find 06/08/2025 and research 16/12/2025). Risk level 4 is referenced in [P13] as 'Natural ATR from Questions: 4' and 'Selected ATR: 4', but no completed ATRQ document is in the pack. The investment rationale is built on this risk profile. Fourth, the alternatives section's risk description (profiles 5 and 7) contradicts the main body (risk level 6), weakening the coherence of the alternatives rejection rationale. The two values are not on different bases — both purport to describe the same existing funds. The guards around standard regulatory wording do not apply to these substantive discrepancies. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability assessment is in the pack (extraction confirms /vulnerability/assessment is absent); no vulnerability indicators are noted in the fact find passages retrieved, so no material effect on this check is identified beyond what CHK-010 will assess separately. Overall: the report has genuine client-specific rationale for the principal objectives but is undermined by the monetary inconsistency in the pension switch cost comparison, the unconfirmed ATRQ, and the risk level contradiction in the alternatives section.

  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report presents six alternatives considered: (1) Leave existing pension untouched — rejected because current funds have risk level 6, not matching agreed Cautious Balanced profile of 4. (2) Stakeholder pension — rejected as the recommended plan is more cost-effective, stakeholder pensions typically lack flexible access and have limited fund ranges. (3) Investment pathways — rejected because client requires a more tailored solution than the four generic options; pathways do not reflect individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements and risk profile, and have not performed as well as recommended strategy. (4) Retain current Vanguard LifeStrategy funds — rejected because one fund is risk profile 5 and the other risk profile 7, both higher than agreed level 4, with portfolio drift risk. (5) VCTs and EIS — rejected as high-risk, illiquid, often more suitable for experienced/sophisticated investors; deemed inappropriate given client's objectives. Additionally, four options within the pension options section are listed: Option 1 (leave untouched), Option 2 (in-situ fund switch — no explicit standalone rejection rationale, inferred from overall rationale), Option 3 (switch into existing arrangement — no explicit rejection rationale, inferred), Option 4 (switch into employer scheme — client has retired, no employer arrangement). The report asserts the recommended Aviva Platform Personal Pension with Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II at risk level 4 is recommended, with fund selection justified in part by performance comparison against alternatives at risk level 4.
    File:     The Defaqto pension switching research report [P1, P2, P3, P4] confirms the switch from existing Aviva Life & Pensions to new Aviva Platform Personal Pension was researched, with top-10 solutions ranked by maturity value. A stakeholder pension comparison is included. The comparison shows at mid-growth the new solution projects £86,700 vs existing £86,800 (£100 or 0.1% decrease), and at high growth £97,300 vs £97,400 (£100 or 0.1% decrease). The effect of switching including adviser charges shows slightly better outcomes for the new solution. The personal pension plan research report [P8, P9, P10] confirms a whole-of-market panel was used and filters were applied; 1 product from 56 matched. A data comparison [P10] shows Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II at Defaqto risk rating 4 with 5-year cumulative return of 36.21% and AMC 0.41%, outperforming CT Universal MAP Cautious (31.28%, 0.29% AMC), HSBC Global Strategy Conservative (25.90%, 0.10% AMC) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 40% (21.49%, 0.41% AMC). Risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced) is confirmed across multiple research documents [P14, P15, P20]. The pension switch report confirms no transfer penalty and fund value day one equals existing value of £79,092.32 [P4]. The research report for the ISA [P23] shows whole-of-market panel, 1 product from 168 matched filters. The GIA research [P14] confirms agreed risk level 4. No research passage explicitly documents a standalone rejection rationale for Option 2 (in-situ fund switch) or Option 3 (switch into existing arrangement) — these rejections are inferred in the extraction. The performance comparison table [P10] shows the recommended fund has the highest 5-year return among the risk-4 comparators but also the highest AMC at 0.41% vs HSBC at 0.10%. No passage documents VCT/EIS consideration in the research file (Q6.4.6 returned nothing).
    Discrepancies:
      - The report rejects the existing Vanguard funds on the basis that one is risk profile 5 and one is risk profile 7 (combined stated as risk level 6 elsewhere in the report), but the main recommendations section also describes the existing funds as 'risk level 6' — the two descriptions are internally inconsistent within the report (blended vs. individual fund risk levels), though not irreconcilable.
      - The switch comparison [P2] shows the new solution projects £100 less than the existing pension at both mid and high growth rates before adviser charges — i.e. the recommended solution produces a lower projected maturity value than the existing arrangement. The report's narrative does not explicitly acknowledge this unfavourable projection differential; it asserts the switch is suitable without directly addressing that the projected value is lower.
      - The stakeholder pension rejection reason ('more cost effective') is not client-specific — the extraction flags isClientSpecific: false — and no specific cost comparison between the recommended plan and a stakeholder pension is provided in the suitability report itself (though the research file contains a ranked comparison).
      - Option 2 (in-situ fund switch) has no explicit standalone rejection rationale in the report; the extraction describes the rationale as inferred with 0.80 confidence. The report lists it as an option considered but does not state a reason for rejection.
      - Option 3 (switch into existing arrangement) has no explicit rejection rationale in the report; the extraction describes it as inferred with 0.75 confidence.
      - VCTs and EIS are mentioned as considered alternatives in the canonical model, but no research document or file passage evidences this consideration; Q6.4.6 returned nothing.
      - The recommended fund (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II, AMC 0.41%) has a higher AMC than two of the three comparators at the same risk level (CT Universal MAP 0.29%, HSBC Global Strategy Conservative 0.10%), yet the report does not explain why the higher-cost fund is preferred despite lower-cost risk-4 alternatives existing.
      - The pension switch report charges comparison [P2] shows the new solution has a higher reduction in yield (0.7%) than the existing pension (0.6%) at mid and high growth rates, consistent with the report's acknowledgement that the new plan is 0.08% more expensive, yet the report's rationale for the pension switch does not directly address why higher charges are justified in terms of net client benefit beyond the risk-alignment argument.
    The report presents a structured set of alternatives with reasons for rejection across multiple recommendation areas, which is positive. However, several concerns arise on consistency and completeness grounds.
    
    1. PROJECTED VALUE LOWER FOR NEW SOLUTION: The pension switch research [P2, P3] shows that at mid-growth the new solution projects £86,700 against the existing £86,800, and at high growth £97,300 against £97,400 — the recommended solution produces a lower projected maturity value in both scenarios before adviser charges. The 'effect of switching including adviser charges' table in [P2] shows the new solution outperforms after adviser charges are factored in (because the existing plan carries no adviser charge currently but the 0.08% charge differential is offset by the performance of the recommended fund). However, the report's narrative does not make this explicit — it does not tell the client that the raw projected value is lower and explain why, on a charges-inclusive basis, the switch is still beneficial. A ranked comparison placing the recommendation below the existing arrangement in the before-charges scenario is a finding under the decision logic.
    
    2. OPTIONS 2 AND 3 — MISSING EXPLICIT REJECTION RATIONALE: The report lists Option 2 (in-situ fund switch) and Option 3 (consolidate into existing arrangement) as considered but provides no explicit rejection rationale for either. The extraction treats these as inferred. Under the guard 'Options listed without individual rejection reasons are a partial answer', this is a finding. The absence of a stated reason for rejecting Option 2 is particularly material because Option 2 would achieve risk-alignment without the higher charges of the new platform, and the reason for preferring a full agency switch (platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21%) is not stated in the suitability report — it appears only as an inference in the extraction.
    
    3. STAKEHOLDER REJECTION NOT CLIENT-SPECIFIC: The stakeholder pension rejection ('more cost effective than a typical stakeholder pension') is generic and not client-specific. The research file [P1] contains a ranked comparison but the report does not cite specific figures to support the assertion.
    
    4. VCTs AND EIS — NO EVIDENTIAL SUPPORT: The report asserts VCTs and EIS were considered and rejected. No research document or file passage supports this; Q6.4.6 returned nothing. The claim is unsupported.
    
    5. HIGHER-COST FUND SELECTION NOT JUSTIFIED IN REPORT: The recommended fund has a higher AMC (0.41%) than two risk-4 comparators (CT Universal MAP 0.29%, HSBC 0.10%). The research file [P10] shows the recommended fund has superior 5-year cumulative returns (36.21% vs 31.28%, 25.90%, 21.49%), which provides a basis for preferring it. However, the suitability report does not articulate this justification — it does not explain why a higher-cost fund is preferred when lower-cost alternatives at the same risk level exist.
    
    6. RISK LEVEL INCONSISTENCY IN REJECTION RATIONALE: The report's rejection of the existing Vanguard funds states one is risk 5 and one is risk 7, while elsewhere the existing pension is described as risk level 6. This is internally inconsistent within the report, even if arithmetically reconcilable as a weighted average. This is flagged in the internal inconsistencies log.
    
    7. VULNERABILITY OVERLAY: No vulnerability assessment is in the file (extraction confirms /vulnerability/assessment is absent). No vulnerability characteristics are identified in the passages that would materially affect this check.
    
    The combination of: (a) projected maturity value being lower in before-charges scenarios without explicit client disclosure; (b) Options 2 and 3 lacking explicit rejection rationales; (c) higher-cost fund not justified in the report; and (d) VCT/EIS consideration unsupported by evidence — together constitute material concerns about the completeness and quality of the rationale.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   Four existing arrangements are identified: (1) Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd - Pension Portfolio (SIPP) [EA1] – action: RetainAndSwitchFunds, rationale: lower costs (platform charge 0.32% to 0.21%), risk alignment (current funds risk level 6 too high for agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile 4), professional oversight, adviser charging facilitation, tax-efficient contribution (£3,600 gross), platform integration. (2) Cash ISA [EA2] – action: FullTransfer, rationale includes platform integration and relevant objectives. (3) Chartered savings account [EA3] – action: Retain, with rationale. (4) Cash ISA (Moneybox) [EA4] – action: NoAdviceGiven. Each arrangement has a stated outcome and at least one rationale in the canonical model. The 'LowerCosts' rationale claims moving to agency terms will reduce the platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%. The RiskAlignment rationale states current funds have a risk level of 6, which is too high for the agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4, and switching to the recommended fund will ensure investments match risk tolerance.
    File:     Policy research [P1–P5, P24] confirms the existing Aviva pension (AV2075926) with fund value £79,092.32, current platform charge confirmed at 0.32% [P4: Fund Charge 0.0022, and platform charge rows], no exit penalties [P5], no guarantees [P4], transferable via Origo [P2], over 3,000 funds available [P3]. The pension switch report [P6, P21] confirms existing plan platform charge 0.32%, existing total annual charge 0.54% (platform 0.32% + fund 0.22%), new solution total 0.62%, implying an increase of 0.08%, not a decrease. The research report [P19, P20] confirms the recommended fund (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II) at risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced), with weighted AMC 0.41%. The personal pension plan review report [P17] confirms existing holdings at risk level 6 (Balanced Growth). The policy research file [P24] contains an 'Overall File Strategy' template with blank notes fields (Summary of Advice, Alternative Strategies, etc.), suggesting a suitability note template exists but its fields are unpopulated. No documentary evidence supports the 'LowerCosts' rationale as the switch charges assessment shows the new plan costs 0.08% MORE than the existing plan (0.62% vs 0.54%). The RiskAlignment rationale is supported by evidence that existing funds are at risk level 6 and recommended fund is at risk level 4.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states 'Lower Costs – Moving to our agency terms will reduce your platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%' as a rationale for the pension switch. While the platform charge element does fall from 0.32% to 0.21%, the total annual recurring charge increases from 0.54% to 0.62% (an increase of 0.08%). The switch charges assessment [P6] confirms the new solution is 0.08% more expensive in total. The 'lower costs' rationale is therefore misleading at the total charge level, even if the platform component alone is lower.
      - The derived figures show a monetary inconsistency: the switch charges table implies a new plan annual charge of £490 (£427 + £63), but the annual recurring charges table for the new pension states £512.69. The report does not reconcile these two figures.
      - The report's rationale for EA1 states the 'LowerCosts' rationale is client-specific and linked to OBJ1 and OBJ4, but the net effect of the switch is higher total cost, which contradicts the stated basis of that rationale.
      - EA4 (Cash ISA Moneybox) has an action of 'NoAdviceGiven' – the canonical model records this arrangement but the rationale for no advice being given is not detailed beyond the bare action label; it is not clear from the report why this arrangement was excluded from the advice scope.
    The central finding is that the 'LowerCosts' rationale for the pension switch (EA1) is contradicted by the evidence. The report claims moving to agency terms reduces the platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%, and this narrow statement is true: the platform-only charge does fall. However, the investment management charge on the new fund (0.41%) is higher than on the existing funds (0.22%), meaning the total annual recurring charge rises from 0.54% to 0.62%. The pension switch report [P6] expressly records this as a +0.08% increase and states the plan must outperform by £63 in year one. The report presents this as a 'LowerCosts' point, which is factually misleading at the total charge level. The severity guidance says 'rationale that contradicts the case facts (e.g. lower costs where the charge comparison shows an increase)' is the highest severity. The RiskAlignment rationale is well-supported: [P17] confirms existing holdings at risk level 6 and [P19/P20] confirms the recommended fund is at risk level 4, consistent with the client's agreed Cautious Balanced profile. The PlatformIntegration and AdviserChargeFacilitation rationales are generic in nature (isClientSpecific flagged false in two of the four relevant entries) but are not contradicted. For EA4 (Moneybox Cash ISA), the action is 'NoAdviceGiven' with no substantive rationale recorded beyond that label. The check's guard notes that 'No action required is a recommended outcome provided a reason is given for it'; here the reason is absent. This is a secondary missing component. The monetary inconsistency between implied new plan charge of £490 and stated £512.69 is an arithmetic discrepancy internal to the report, noted in the extraction's own inconsistencies. No vulnerability flags are recorded in the extraction (path absent), and no retrieved passages raise vulnerability concerns that would affect this assessment.

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   Five recommendations are made: (1) Retain/agency transfer — add TFAS as servicing adviser, reduce platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%, marked Recommended, implementer = Adviser, intended outcome: bring SIPP under TFAS agency to reduce charges and provide oversight; (2) Switch — switch pension funds from Vanguard LifeStrategy 60%/80% to Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II, marked Recommended, implementer = Adviser, intended outcome: align with Risk Level 4 Cautious Balanced profile; (3) IncreaseContribution — single pension contribution £2,880 net/£3,600 gross, marked Recommended, responsible party = NotStated, intended outcome: utilise annual allowance tax-efficiently for 2025/26; (4) NewPlan — open Aviva Platform GIA and invest £57,120, marked Recommended, implementer = Adviser, intended outcome: invest inheritance tax-efficiently with Bed & ISA strategy; (5) Transfer — transfer £20,000 Cash ISA to Aviva Stocks & Shares ISA, marked Recommended, implementer = Adviser, intended outcome: achieve greater growth potential while maintaining tax-free status. Implementation timescale: 10-15 working days. Cancellation rights disclosed. Next steps include Expression of Wish form and annual Bed & ISA review.
    File:     The Client Agreement to Proceed (P14) confirms that the adviser arranges and oversees investments on the client's behalf and that fees are set out in the suitability report. The GIA Illustration (P5/P6) shows the Aviva Platform Investment Portfolio with CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc at 0.29% OCF and confirms the initial adviser charge of £4,794.37 and ongoing 1% adviser charge, consistent with the report's stated implementation. The ISA Key Features (P1-P3) and Pension Key Features (P4, P7-P8) confirm Aviva product details consistent with the recommended wrappers. The risk profile report (P9, P13) shows Risk Level 4 Cautious Balanced as the agreed outcome, consistent with the fund switch rationale. No passage explicitly names who will implement the pension contribution (REC3). No passage contradicts any of the five recommendations being adviser-implemented except the NotStated responsible party for REC3.
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model records /recommendations[]/implementation/willAdviserImplement as containing only 4 entries (true, true, true, true) for 5 recommendations — the array has one fewer element than there are recommendations, leaving one recommendation without an explicit willAdviserImplement value.
      - The canonical model records /recommendations[2]/implementation/responsibleParty as 'NotStated' for REC3 (the pension contribution), whereas all other four recommendations record 'Adviser'. The report therefore does not state who will implement the pension contribution recommendation.
      - The willAdviserImplement array has 4 elements but there are 5 recommendations — it is ambiguous which recommendation is unmatched (likely REC3 given the NotStated responsible party).
    Four of the five recommendations clearly state the action, implementer (Adviser), and intended outcome. REC3 — the single pension contribution of £2,880 net/£3,600 gross — is the exception: the canonical model records the responsible party as 'NotStated' and the willAdviserImplement array contains only four entries for five recommendations. This means the report does not clearly state who will implement this change. The intended outcome for REC3 is stated (utilise annual allowance), the advice status is Recommended, and the amount and timing (2025/26 tax year) are stated, so those elements are present. The missing element is solely implementer identity. Given the extraction notes that the willAdviserImplement array has only 4 entries and REC3 has NotStated as responsible party, this appears to reflect a genuine gap in the report rather than an extraction error. The remaining four recommendations satisfy G6.6 fully: each states the action, confirms the adviser will implement, and articulates the intended outcome with client-specific rationale. The Bed & ISA next step is flagged as intended/ongoing rather than immediate, but it is attributed to the adviser and linked to annual review, which is adequate. The vulnerability overlay does not surface any specific driver or characteristic that would heighten the concern about this particular gap. The missing implementer on REC3 is a Low-severity process gap — the recommendation itself is clear, the amount and tax year are stated, and the only absent element is a statement of who arranges the contribution.

  [G6.7] Recommendations that present multiple possible outcomes without stating which is recommended and why
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report presents five discrete recommendations, each marked as Recommended with no unresolved options. For the pension, five alternatives are listed (Options 1–5): Option 1 (leave untouched) rejected because risk level 6 mismatches agreed risk profile 4 and charges can be reduced; Option 2 (stay in place, switch funds only) rejected because agency change also reduces platform charge making it more beneficial; Option 3 (switch to another existing pension) rejected because Aviva Platform is excellent and internal changes sufficient; Option 4 (transfer to employer scheme) rejected as client is retired with no employer scheme; Option 5 (new pension plan) rejected because no need to establish a new plan. All five recommendations (Retain with agency change, Fund switch, Pension contribution, New GIA, ISA transfer) are each stated as Recommended with client-specific rationale linking to named objectives. The canonical model records presentsUnresolvedOptions as false for all recommendations.
    File:     Research file passages confirm: risk profile of 4 (Cautious Balanced) agreed [P10]; CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund selected for GIA at risk level 4 [P6]; Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II selected for the pension switch at risk level 4 [P10]; client objectives on file include reviewing existing Aviva pension, investing inheritance for growth and tax-efficiency, planning for full retirement at 60, and completing ATR questionnaire [P9]; client notes confirm ATR 4 and that only the pension exists with no contributions made [P1]; client is retired/working part-time [P2]; the agreement to proceed confirms TFAS will implement recommendations and provide ongoing service [P4]. No passage in the file records any option left open or unresolved. No passage contradicts the report's identification of which option is recommended.
    Discrepancies:
      - The internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction — existing pension fund risk described as risk level 6 in the main body but the alternative solutions section says 'one fund is aligned to a risk profile of 5 and the other to a risk profile of 7' — appears within the report. This does not create an unresolved recommendation option but is an internal inconsistency in the rationale for the fund switch recommendation (risk mismatch justification).
      - The monetary charge figures for the new pension differ: the switch charges assessment implies £490 (£427 + £63) whereas the annual recurring charges table states £512.69. This affects the 'lower cost' rationale claimed for certain elements but does not leave an option unresolved.
      - Options 3 and 5 share essentially the same rejection reason ('Aviva Platform is excellent') without meaningful differentiation between them, which is a thin rationale rather than an unresolved option.
    The core requirement for this check is whether the report presents multiple possible outcomes without stating which is recommended and why. The canonical model records presentsUnresolvedOptions as false for all five recommendations, and the file evidence is consistent with this: every recommendation has a stated action, a named rationale, and where alternatives were listed (Options 1–5 for the pension), each alternative is explicitly rejected with a reason. No passage in the file contradicts this structure or suggests an option was left open. The three discrepancies noted are real differences within the report's own text, but none of them amounts to an unresolved option: the risk level 5/7 versus 6 inconsistency is an internal inconsistency in characterising the existing funds (addressed under CHK-001/CHK-009) rather than an unresolved choice between two recommendations; the charge figure discrepancy (£490 vs £512.69) is an arithmetic inconsistency in the cost justification (addressed under CHK-007) but does not leave a recommendation unresolved; and the thin rejection reasons for Options 3 and 5 are a weak justification issue rather than an unresolved option. The vulnerability overlay: the file records the client as retired and working part-time with an ATR of 4. No passage identifies a vulnerability driver that would materially affect the resolution of options. The requirement is met: every recommendation states what is recommended, why alternatives were rejected, and no option is left open.

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report states: (1) the pension switch/transfer will take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes; (2) the Bed & ISA facility will be used to gradually transfer GIA investments into ISA/Pension each tax year, recommended to be completed at each annual review (timescale: 2025/26 tax year); (3) an Expression of Wish form has been posted out for the client to sign and return; (4) responsible party is recorded as 'Adviser' for four of five recommendations (Retain, Switch, NewPlan, Transfer), and 'NotStated' for IncreaseContribution; (5) out-of-market risk is disclosed; (6) 30-day cancellation rights are stated.
    File:     The Aviva Pension Key Features [P2, P3, P4] and Pension Illustration [P1] confirm the Aviva Platform Personal Pension product and its administrative features, including that the adviser will arrange investment changes and that transfers are possible. The Key Features documents describe how the platform operates but do not independently confirm a specific implementation timescale. The GIA Key Features [P5] and research reports [P9, P10] confirm the GIA product context. No passage independently corroborates or contradicts the 10-15 working day timescale stated in the report, the responsible party assignments, or the next-steps actions. The file holds no document that disputes the report's implementation assertions.
    Discrepancies:
      - The responsible party for the IncreaseContribution recommendation is recorded as 'NotStated' in the canonical model, meaning the report does not identify who will arrange or implement the pension contribution increase, where this is at least potentially material.
      - No independent corroborating evidence in the file confirms the 10-15 working day timescale, though this is a provider service standard and the guard permits a stated timescale of this kind.
      - The Bed & ISA strategy is described as an ongoing annual action to be completed at each year review, but no specific date or mechanism for the first execution is stated beyond the 2025/26 tax year reference, and the report does not state whether the adviser will arrange this or the client must initiate it.
    The report provides materially adequate implementation detail for the principal recommendations: the pension switch carries a 10-15 working day timescale (a provider service standard, which the guard confirms satisfies the timescale requirement), the adviser is identified as responsible party for the Switch, NewPlan, Transfer and Retain actions, and specific next steps (Expression of Wish, Bed & ISA) are named with client-specific language. The 10-15 working day figure is not independently corroborated by a passage but the guard explicitly states a timescale quoted as a provider service standard is a stated timescale, so absence of a corroborating passage is not a finding here. The Bed & ISA is described as a recurring annual action linked to OBJ2; the 2025/26 tax year gives a first-year frame but neither the report nor any passage clearly states whether the adviser will initiate each annual execution or whether the client must request it — this is a minor process clarity gap but not suitability-material given the annual review context. The more notable gap is that the IncreaseContribution recommendation records 'NotStated' as the responsible party. A pension contribution increase may require either the client or the adviser to instruct Aviva; if the adviser will arrange it, that should be stated; if the client must act, that should equally be clear. This is a low-level process clarity issue rather than a suitability-altering gap, because the contribution amount and timing (2025/26 tax year) are stated, and the action itself is simple. No vulnerability factors are recorded in the file that would heighten the significance of these gaps. Overall, the report substantially meets this requirement with one low-severity gap on responsible party for the contribution recommendation.

  [G6.9] Ancillary recommendations — beneficiary nomination, Power of Attorney, will review — are stated with an owner and are consistent with what the file records
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) An Expression of Wish form has been posted out for the client to sign and return, to arrange a beneficiary nomination on the Aviva plan — the adviser is the party who posted the form; (2) A Bed & ISA facility is recommended for gradual transfer of GIA investments into ISA/Pension each tax year, to be completed at each annual review; (3) Both clients (Anthony Barnes and Yvette McGealy) have wills that are up to date; (4) Both clients have Power of Attorney granted. The responsible party for implementation of the Bed & ISA and Expression of Wish steps is stated as the adviser (or the client returning the form). No will review or POA setup is recommended as a next step because both are recorded as already in place.
    File:     The fact find (P2, P8) records Anthony Barnes and Yvette McGealy as clients. The fact find does not contain a section recording will status or Power of Attorney status that has been extracted into the passages provided. The policy research document (P11) records the fact find as fully complete for the sections listed, but no will or POA status fields are visible in the retrieved passages. The pension illustration (P15) records a transfer of £79,092.32 from the existing Aviva plan, consistent with the switch recommendation. No passage records that the Expression of Wish form was posted, returned, or actioned. No passage confirms or contradicts the reported will or POA status for either client.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts that both clients have up-to-date wills; no passage in the case file confirms or records this.
      - The report asserts that both clients have Power of Attorney granted; no passage in the case file confirms or records this.
      - The report states the Expression of Wish form has been posted to the client; no passage in the case file confirms or corroborates this action having been taken.
    The report makes three ancillary assertions relevant to this requirement: (1) beneficiary nomination via Expression of Wish form posted by the adviser; (2) wills in place and up to date for both clients; (3) Power of Attorney granted for both clients. The retrieved passages do not include any estate planning section of the fact find, nor any case note or letter that would confirm the will status, POA status, or the posting of the Expression of Wish form. Per the instruction on missing evidence: where the report asserts a will or attorney status the file does not record, that is a gap. The same applies to the POA. These are therefore unsupported assertions — not contradicted, but not corroborated by any file document. The Expression of Wish step has an identified owner (adviser posted, client to return), which satisfies the ownership element of the requirement. However, no file evidence confirms the action was taken. The Bed & ISA recommendation is client-specific and linked to OBJ2 with the adviser as responsible party and a clear timing trigger (each annual review). The guards applied: 'will' in context here clearly refers to a legal testament given the estate planning context, not the modal verb. The adviser is recommending the client nominate a beneficiary (not that the client set up a POA), and the POA is recorded as already in place — but the file does not corroborate that record. The absence of corroborating file evidence for will status, POA status, and the Expression of Wish posting constitutes missing evidence supporting report assertions, which is a Potential Concern at Low severity given the assertions are plausible and not contradicted, but remain unsupported.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 11 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). Switch charges assessment table implies new pension annual charge of £490 (£427 + £63), but the annual recurring charges table for the Aviva Platform Personal Pension states £512.69 — a difference of £22.69 for the same plan. Cashflow modelling inputs reference initial fee of £4,794.37, while the charges section states £4,794 — a £0.37 rounding difference. The report states cumulative charge impact is referenced to the personal illustration, but no illustration figure for the new pension's total annual monetary charge appears in the retrieved passages that confirms either £490 or £512.69 as the definitive figure. 14 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report discloses the following cost layers: (1) Aviva Platform Personal Pension – platform charge 0.21% / £173.65 pa; investment management charge 0.41% / £339.04 pa; total 0.62% / £512.69. (2) Aviva Platform ISA – platform 0.21% / £42 pa; investment management 0.29% / £58 pa. (3) Aviva Platform GIA – platform 0.21% / £119.95 pa; investment management 0.29% / £165.65 pa. Initial advice fee 3% = £4,794. Ongoing adviser charge 1% pa. Transaction costs excluded with stated reasons (variable, already reflected net in fund performance). Aggregated total for pension described as 1.62% pa including adviser fee. Existing pension total charge 0.54% / £427 pa. Switch charges table shows new plan costs £63 more per year (+0.08%), implying new plan annual charge of £490. Annual recurring charges table states new plan total £512.69. Report notes monetary figures may differ from illustration due to calculation method differences. Cashflow modelling inputs reference initial fee of £4,794.37 (£0.37 more than £4,794 stated in charges section). Cumulative effect of charges referenced to the personal illustration.
    File:     Provider illustrations and factsheets confirm: (P20/P21) Aviva GIA illustration confirms CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc OCF 0.29%; adviser charges £4,794.37 initial and 1% pa ongoing. (P6) Pension projection (existing plan) confirms Vanguard LifeStrategy funds at 0.22% weighted average OCF and Aviva platform charge. (P5) Personal pension research report confirms ongoing charges 0.29% / 0.21% / 0.22% for new and existing funds. (P10) Pension switch report confirms Aviva platform flat rate 0.21%. (P3) CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc KIID confirms entry/exit charges 0.00%; ongoing charges figure present. (P15) Suitability report investment fund tables confirm Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II at 0.41% and CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc at 0.29%. (P16) GIA illustration shows projected values net of charges over 10 years. (P19) ISA illustration shows projected values. (P21) Pension illustration references adviser charges. The illustrations confirm an initial adviser charge of £4,794.37. The switch charges table states existing £427 / new impact +£63, arithmetically implying new plan = £490 pa. The annual recurring charges table states £512.69 for the new pension. These two figures for the same new plan's annual charge are inconsistent by £22.69.
    Discrepancies:
      - Switch charges assessment table implies new pension annual charge of £490 (£427 + £63), but the annual recurring charges table for the Aviva Platform Personal Pension states £512.69 — a difference of £22.69 for the same plan.
      - Cashflow modelling inputs reference initial fee of £4,794.37, while the charges section states £4,794 — a £0.37 rounding difference.
      - The report states cumulative charge impact is referenced to the personal illustration, but no illustration figure for the new pension's total annual monetary charge appears in the retrieved passages that confirms either £490 or £512.69 as the definitive figure.
      - The report's 1.62% total cost including adviser fee implies: 0.62% product/platform + 1.00% adviser = 1.62%. The switch charges assessment compares 0.62% new vs 0.54% existing on a like-for-like (ex-adviser) basis, which is correct, but the two monetary presentations (£490 and £512.69) arise from different fund value bases, which the report does not explain to the client.
    The report discloses all material cost layers: platform, investment management, initial advice, ongoing advice, and transaction costs (excluded with stated and acceptable reasons). The KIID, illustrations and research reports in the file corroborate the fund OCF figures and the platform charge rate. The initial adviser fee of £4,794.37 in the illustration (P20) matches the cashflow modelling figure; the £0.37 difference against the charges section's £4,794 is a trivial rounding matter.
    
    The substantive concern is the inconsistency between the two monetary figures for the new pension's annual charge. The switch charges assessment table states existing = £427 and impact = +£63, arithmetically implying new = £490. The annual recurring charges table states £512.69 for the new pension. The derived figures confirm both computations are internally consistent with different implied fund values: £490 / 0.62% implies a fund value of ~£79,032 (close to the existing £79,092.32), while £512.69 / 0.62% implies ~£82,692 (a higher opening value, likely reflecting the £3,600 new contribution). The report itself acknowledges 'the charge in monetary terms shown here may differ from the illustration due to differences in calculation methods', and the extraction notes that the switch charges assessment uses the current fund value of the existing plan while the annual recurring charges table uses the amount being invested (existing value plus new contribution). This is a legitimate basis difference, but it is not explained to the client in a way that reconciles the two figures. A client reading both tables would see £490 and £512.69 as two answers to the same question.
    
    The pension illustration in the file (P21) references adviser charges but the retrieved passages do not contain a line confirming the new pension's total annual monetary product/platform charge as either figure, so independent corroboration of £512.69 vs £490 cannot be established from the retrieved passages alone.
    
    The transaction cost exclusion is adequately disclosed with reasons. No DFM fee applies. The aggregated total (1.62% including adviser) is stated. The cumulative effect is referenced to the illustration.
    
    No vulnerability indicators are identified in the evidence that would affect this assessment.
    
    Overall: the cost disclosure is materially complete and most figures are corroborated. The unreconciled £22.69 / basis-difference between the two monetary new-pension charge figures, presented without explanation to the client, is a disclosure shortfall of moderate concern. The £0.37 rounding difference is low severity on its own.

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   Initial advice fee: 3% of total amount invested, stated as £4,794 based on total investment values of £79,092 (pension transfer) + £57,120 (GIA) + £20,000 (ISA) + £3,600 (pension contribution) = £159,812. Fee paid from GIA funds. Ongoing advice fee: 1% per annum of total Aviva Platform investments, estimated at £1,558 for the upcoming year based on total platform value of £155,812 after initial fee deduction. Both fees facilitated via platform.
    File:     Client Agreement To Proceed [P19] states: 'Initial Service - There will be a charge of 3% of the value of the funds that we advise on with a minimum of £1,350 fee. Based on the value of your funds, our fee will be £4,794.' Ongoing: '1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100), which is automatically taken from the funds invested.' IMPORT_2 [P1] states 3% initial with £1,350 minimum. Aviva GIA Illustration [P4] records initial adviser charge as £4,794.37 and ongoing as 1.00% p.a. Aviva Pension Illustration [P5] records adviser charges consistent with the arrangement. The client agreement confirms the £4,794 monetary figure and the 3% and 1% rates.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the initial fee in monetary terms as £4,794 [Our charges section]; the Aviva GIA illustration [P4] records it as £4,794.37; the client agreement [P19] also states £4,794. This is a £0.37 rounding difference across documents.
      - The report states the ongoing fee estimate as £1,558 based on a platform value of £155,812; no passage corroborates either the £1,558 figure or the £155,812 base value from an independent source — both figures appear only in the suitability report itself.
      - The report's stated investment values used to compute the initial fee (£79,092 pension + £57,120 GIA + £20,000 ISA + £3,600 contribution = £159,812) are not independently corroborated in the retrieved passages; the nearest comparator in the file is £55,000 (an illustrative example in [P20]).
      - 3% of £159,812 = £4,794.36, which rounds to £4,794; the report states £4,794 and the client agreement [P19] states £4,794 — these are consistent; the GIA illustration [P4] states £4,794.37 which is the unrounded figure.
    The two core fee rates — 3% initial and 1% ongoing — are confirmed in both the suitability report and the client agreement [P19] and the firm's service document [P1]. The monetary initial fee of £4,794 is confirmed in the client agreement [P19] and is arithmetically consistent with 3% of £159,812 (= £4,794.36, rounded to £4,794). The £0.37 difference between the report (£4,794) and the GIA illustration [P4] (£4,794.37) is a trivial rounding artefact, not a substantive discrepancy. The 1% ongoing rate is confirmed in [P19] and [P4]. The ongoing monetary estimate of £1,558 and the base of £155,812 appear only in the suitability report (category [I]) and cannot be independently corroborated from the retrieved evidence; however, 1% of £155,812 = £1,558.12, which rounds to £1,558, so the arithmetic is internally consistent. The base of £155,812 is plausible as £159,812 minus the £4,794 initial fee (= £155,018, not £155,812), but the report states it as post-fee-deduction. Actually: £159,812 − £4,794 = £155,018, not £155,812. This is a discrepancy: the report states the post-fee base as £155,812, but subtracting the stated initial fee from the stated investment total gives £155,018, a difference of £794. This means either the investment total used for the ongoing base differs from the sum stated in the initial fee calculation, or there is an arithmetic error in the ongoing fee base. At 1%, the difference in annual fee would be £7.94 (£1,558 vs £1,550.18). This is a low-severity data inconsistency within the report's own figures. No vulnerability indicators are present in the evidence that would affect this assessment. Overall, the rates match the client agreement exactly, and the monetary figures are largely consistent with minor rounding or base-value discrepancies.

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts the following in its Critical Yield section for the pension switch (at age 60): Low growth — Existing RIY 1.6%, Recommended RIY 1.6%, Critical yield 0.0%, Effect of switching £0 / 0.0% decrease. Mid growth — Existing RIY 1.7%, Recommended RIY 1.7%, Critical yield 2.9%, Effect of switching £0 / 0.0% decrease. High growth — Existing RIY 1.8%, Recommended RIY 1.8%, Critical yield 5.9%, Effect of switching £0 / 0.0% decrease.
    File:     The pension switch report [P20][P21] provides a different set of figures. Low growth — Existing RIY not stated in the table excerpt but mid-row shows Existing 0.6%, New 0.7%; Critical yield 2.9%; Effect of switching £100 or 0.1% decrease. Mid growth (2.94%) — Existing projected value £86,800, New £86,700; Existing RIY 0.6%, New RIY 0.7%; Critical yield 2.9%; Effect of switching £100 or 0.1% decrease. High growth (5.88%) — Existing £97,400, New £97,300; Existing RIY 0.7%, New RIY 0.7%; Critical yield 5.9%; Effect of switching £100 or 0.1% decrease. The Aviva pension illustration [P11] shows a RIY of 1.9% (all charges including adviser) and 0.6% (product and investment charges only) at a real-terms growth basis of 2.6% after inflation. The figure-match exercise found the report's 1.6% and 1.7% RIY figures unmatched in any passage; the report's 1.8% RIY is also unmatched; the switch report carries 0.6%/0.7% (mid) and 0.7%/0.7% (high). The critical yield of 2.9% (mid) and 5.9% (high) are corroborated by the switch report. The effect-of-switching figures in the switch report are £100 / 0.1% decrease at both mid and high, not £0 / 0.0% as stated in the suitability report.
    Discrepancies:
      - RIY for existing pension: report states 1.6% (low), 1.7% (mid), 1.8% (high); switch report [P20][P21] states 0.6% (mid) and 0.7% (high); Aviva illustration [P11] states 0.6% (product/investment only) and 1.9% (all charges). None of the switch-report figures match the suitability report's figures at any growth rate.
      - RIY for recommended solution: report states 1.6% (low), 1.7% (mid), 1.8% (high); switch report states 0.7% (mid) and 0.7% (high); Aviva illustration states 1.9% (all charges). Again, none match the suitability report's values.
      - Effect of switching (low growth): report states £0 / 0.0% decrease; no corresponding low-growth row appears in the switch report passages to confirm or deny, but the pattern at mid and high is £100 / 0.1% decrease.
      - Effect of switching (mid growth): report states £0 / 0.0% decrease; switch report [P20] states £100 or 0.1% decrease.
      - Effect of switching (high growth): report states £0 / 0.0% decrease; switch report [P20] states £100 or 0.1% decrease.
      - Aviva pension illustration [P11] shows product-and-investment RIY of 0.6% and all-charges RIY of 1.9%; neither matches the suitability report's 1.6%/1.7%/1.8% figures across the three growth scenarios.
      - The monetary charge inconsistency already identified in the extraction (£490 implied by switch-charges table vs £512.69 in the annual recurring charges table) is a separate data inconsistency bearing on the same cost disclosure.
    The suitability report's Critical Yield table asserts RIY figures of 1.6% / 1.7% / 1.8% for both the existing and recommended pension across three growth scenarios. The pension switch report [P20][P21], which is the primary supporting evidence document for the switch recommendation, states RIY figures of 0.6% (existing, mid) and 0.7% (new, mid), and 0.7%/0.7% at high growth. The Aviva pension illustration [P11] states a product-and-investment RIY of 0.6% and an all-charges RIY of 1.9%. None of these corroborate the suitability report's 1.6%–1.8% range. One guard to consider: the illustration [P11] uses a real-terms basis (inflation at 2%), which could produce a different RIY from a nominal basis. The switch report uses nominal growth rates (mid 2.94%, high 5.88%) and real-terms projections ('today's money'). However, the switch report's own RIY is stated at 0.6%/0.7%, not 1.6%–1.8%, so a basis difference between illustration and suitability report does not explain the gap against the switch report. The suitability report's 1.9%-ish range is closer to the Aviva illustration's all-in figure of 1.9%, but the report attributes these figures to the critical yield section, not a rounded illustration figure, and attributes identical RIY to both existing and recommended — which is inconsistent with the switch report showing that the new solution has a marginally higher RIY than the existing. Furthermore, the effect-of-switching figures diverge materially: the report says £0 / 0.0% decrease at every growth rate, but the switch report shows £100 / 0.1% decrease at both mid and high growth. While £100 on an £86,800 fund is trivial, the report explicitly states zero difference, which contradicts the switch report. The critical yield figures (2.9% mid, 5.9% high) are corroborated by the switch report, so that element is consistent. However, the suitability report's RIY figures are consistently higher than those in the switch report by approximately 1 percentage point, and the effect-of-switching assertion of zero impact is contradicted by the switch report showing a £100 decrease. These discrepancies in the cumulative impact disclosure constitute a material concern: a client reading the suitability report would see different RIY figures from those in the switch report, and would see no adverse effect of switching where the switch report records one. No vulnerability characteristics are identified in the file that alter this assessment. The additional monetary charge inconsistency (£490 vs £512.69 for the new pension annual charge) further undermines confidence in the arithmetic underpinning the cost disclosure.

  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment table states: existing plan (AV2075926-001) total annual recurring charge = 0.54% / £427; impact of recommended alternative = +0.08% / +£63, implying new plan annual charge = £490. The annual recurring charges table for the Aviva Platform Personal Pension separately states the new plan total ongoing charge as £512.69 (platform 0.21% = £173.65 + investment management 0.41% = £339.04). The 'Like for Like' section states 'the recommended fund strategy is 0.08% more expensive than your current plan' and 'your proposed plan will need to outperform your current plan by £63 in the first year'. The existing plan charges are built from 0.32% platform + 0.22% weighted fund charges = 0.54%.
    File:     [P18] reproduces the switch charges table verbatim: existing 0.54% / £427, impact +0.08% / +£63. [P17] confirms the existing platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21% and references the switch. [P4] (policy research) records the existing fund charge as 0.0022 (i.e. 0.22%), consistent with the report. No passage in the evidence provides the new plan's monetary annual charge figure of £512.69 or £490 from a source independent of the suitability report itself. The pension switch research report [P13]/[P14] is in the file but no passage reproduces its new-plan charge table with a monetary total. The derived figures note that £512.69 at 0.62% implies a fund value of ~£82,692, and £63 at 0.08% implies ~£78,750, while £427 at 0.54% implies ~£79,074 — all three implied fund values differ from one another, indicating the monetary figures in the two tables within the report were computed on different fund value bases.
    Discrepancies:
      - The switch charges assessment table implies a new plan annual charge of £490 (£427 + £63), but the annual recurring charges table for the Aviva Platform Personal Pension states the new plan total monetary charge as £512.69 — a difference of £22.69.
      - The £63 monetary impact at 0.08% implies a fund value of approximately £78,750, whereas £427 at 0.54% implies a fund value of approximately £79,074 — two different fund values used within the same switch charges table.
      - The new plan monetary charge of £512.69 at 0.62% implies a fund value of approximately £82,692, which is higher than the existing plan value (~£79,074 implied by £427/0.54%), suggesting the new plan charge may have been calculated on a higher base (possibly including the £3,600 new contribution), while the switch charges table comparison uses only the existing fund value — but this basis difference is not disclosed.
      - The initial advice fee is stated as £4,794 in the 'Our charges' section but £4,794.37 in the cashflow modelling inputs section (minor rounding inconsistency, noted for completeness).
      - No external provider document in the evidence independently corroborates the new plan monetary charge of £512.69 or the £490 implied by the switch charges table.
    The central finding is an internal monetary inconsistency within the report itself. The switch charges assessment table states the existing plan costs £427 p.a. and the recommended alternative costs £63 more, arithmetically implying the new plan costs £490 p.a. However the annual recurring charges table for the Aviva Platform Personal Pension independently states the new plan total ongoing charge as £512.69 p.a. These are two answers to the same question — the annual monetary cost of the new pension plan — and they differ by £22.69. This is an internal contradiction identified by the extraction's own internalInconsistencies and confirmed by the derived figures. The guard on 'different bases' does not explain this away: both figures purport to be the annual ongoing product/platform/fund charge for the new pension, and the report does not state they are on different bases. The most likely explanation, evidenced by the derived figures, is that the switch charges table was calculated on the existing fund value (~£78,750–£79,074) while the annual recurring charges table was calculated on a higher projected fund value (~£82,692, probably incorporating the £3,600 new contribution and/or a different starting value). This difference in base is material to the cost comparison but is not disclosed — the report presents both figures without caveat, and the 'Like for Like' narrative relies on the switch charges table's £63/0.08% figures as if they were the complete picture. The report's own note in [P1] and [P19] acknowledges that monetary charges are estimates based on 'the amount being invested into the plan, plus one year of contributions', which would explain a higher base for the annual recurring charges table, but this distinction is not carried through to the switch charges assessment, making the two presentations inconsistent and potentially confusing to the client. The minor £0.37 rounding difference on the initial fee (£4,794 vs £4,794.37) is de minimis. The absence of any external provider document independently corroborating either new-plan monetary charge means the report's own figures are the only source, and they disagree with each other. No vulnerability factors are identified in the evidence that would alter this finding. The overall cost comparison is therefore internally unreliable at the monetary level, even though the percentage figures (0.08%, 0.62%) are consistent across the report and corroborated by [P18].

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states five benefits to justify the recommendation: (1) Lower Costs — platform charge reduces from 0.32% to 0.21%; (2) Risk Alignment — current funds at risk level 6 are too high for the agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4, switching aligns investments; (3) Professional Oversight — ongoing fund monitoring and portfolio review; (4) Tax-Efficient Contribution — £3,600 gross contribution with 20% tax relief; (5) Platform Integration — pension, ISA and GIA on single Aviva Platform. The cost justification states: the new plan charges 0.08% more than the existing plan (£63 more in year one), the new total annual charge is 0.62% vs existing 0.54%, and the required outperformance is £63 p.a. in monetary terms or 1.08% p.a. including the adviser fee. A critical yield of 2.94% is stated as achievable 'based on past performance of recommended funds and improved risk alignment and professional oversight.'
    File:     [P12] (fund factsheet): Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II carries a total fund charge of 0.41% (AMC 0.30% + Additional Expenses 0.11%), consistent with the report. [P8] (research report for new pension): recommended fund risk mapping is 4, matching client agreed risk level 4, consistent with risk alignment benefit. [P11] (research report): new fund 5-year cumulative return 36.21%; [P2]/[P9] (review report): existing Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% 5-year return 42.45% and LifeStrategy 80% 5-year return 66.42%. The existing combined 5-year return shown in [P9] is 58.01% (combined). The derived figures show: (a) the switch charges table implies a new plan annual charge of £490 (£427 existing + £63 increase), but the annual recurring charges table for the new pension shows £512.69 — a £22.69 discrepancy, noted as an internal inconsistency in the extraction. (b) The £63 outperformance figure implies a fund value of £78,750 at 0.08%, while the existing Aviva pension value is £79,092.32 — a small rounding difference but not arithmetically impossible. The cashflow model in [P23] records Aviva GIA fees as '0.5% ongoing platform/fund charges and 1% ongoing financial planning fee' and Aviva ISA fees as '0.5% ongoing platform/fund charges and 1% ongoing financial planning fee', whereas the report states the new platform charge is 0.21% (not 0.5%). [P15] (policy research) records the existing plan Aviva AV2075926. No provider illustration or key features document for the new pension plan is in the pack (noted as absent at /solution/NP1/illustration).
    Discrepancies:
      - The switch charges table implies a new pension annual charge of £490 (£427 + £63), but the annual recurring charges table for the new Aviva Platform Personal Pension states £512.69 — a £22.69 discrepancy within the report itself.
      - The cashflow model inputs ([P23]) record Aviva ISA and Aviva GIA platform/fund fees as 0.5%, not 0.21% as stated in the report's charges section for the new Aviva Platform products. The cashflow inputs are therefore on a different (higher) cost basis than the charges disclosed in the report.
      - The report justifies achievability of the 2.94% critical yield by reference to 'past performance of recommended funds', but the recommended fund (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II) has a 5-year cumulative return of 36.21% ([P11]/[P12]), whereas the existing combined portfolio has a 5-year cumulative return of 58.01% ([P9]). The recommended fund has materially underperformed the existing fund over the relevant comparison period, which undercuts the achievability assertion rather than supporting it.
      - No provider illustration for the new pension plan (NP1) is present in the pack (confirmed absent at /solution/NP1/illustration). The report's projected values and cumulative cost disclosures for the new pension therefore cannot be verified against a provider illustration.
      - The 'Lower Costs' benefit asserts platform charges reduce from 0.32% to 0.21%, but the overall recommendation increases total annual charges from 0.54% to 0.62%. The report acknowledges the net increase but leads the benefit statement with a cost reduction framing that is potentially misleading about the overall cost impact.
    There are four substantive concerns. First, the internal monetary inconsistency between the switch charges table (implying £490 new plan annual charge) and the annual recurring charges table (£512.69) is a data inconsistency within the report itself: the two figures are £22.69 apart and no explanation is given. This is the internal inconsistency flagged by the extraction at /costsAndCharges/switchChargesAssessment. Second, the cashflow model files [P23] and [P24] record ISA and GIA fees at 0.5% platform/fund charge, while the report's charges section discloses 0.21%. These are not the same document and the cashflow is an input document, not the suitability report, but the discrepancy means the cashflow projections relied upon in the suitability assessment may be on a different cost basis than the charges actually disclosed. This is a data inconsistency that could affect the reliability of the cashflow conclusions. Third, and most materially, the report asserts that the 2.94% critical yield is 'achievable given the improved risk alignment and professional oversight' and invokes past performance of the recommended funds. The guard notes that 'an assertion that required outperformance is achievable is a claim, not an analysis; require supporting performance data before accepting it.' The supporting performance data in the file contradicts the assertion: the recommended Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II has a 5-year return of 36.21% ([P11]), while the existing combined portfolio has a 5-year return of 58.01% ([P9]). The recommended fund has underperformed the existing arrangement by approximately 22 percentage points over five years. The report does not address this comparison, and does not explain why a fund with lower historical returns will now outperform. The achievability claim is therefore unsupported and is in tension with the available evidence. Fourth, no provider illustration for NP1 is in the pack, so projected values cannot be cross-checked. The 'Lower Costs' framing is noted as potentially misleading — the platform charge does fall, but the total all-in charge rises — but this is disclosed in the body of the report, so it is a disclosure quality concern rather than a concealment. The vulnerability overlay: no formal vulnerability assessment was completed (/vulnerability/assessment absent). The fact-find records no specific vulnerability characteristics that would alter this finding. On balance, the weak justification for achievability, backed by performance evidence that cuts against the claim, and the internal monetary discrepancy, constitute Potential Concerns.
