  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states that the recommended pension plan costs 0.08% more than the existing plan on a charges-only basis (existing 0.54% vs new 0.62%), and that including the 1% ongoing adviser fee the new plan must outperform the existing plan by approximately 1.08% annually to match the same net growth. The monetary equivalent of the charges-only difference is stated as £63 per year (existing £427, impact +£63, implying new plan £490). The annual recurring charges table for the new Aviva Platform Personal Pension states £512.69 as the total monetary charge. The report provides a value justification referencing platform quality, investment strategy alignment, consolidation benefits and ongoing adviser service as benefits to offset the higher cost.
    File:     This requirement is assessed from the report alone. No external corroborating document was provided. The canonical model and derived figures are the sole basis of comparison. The derived figures confirm: (a) £427 at 0.54% is consistent with the existing Aviva plan value of ~£79,074; (b) the switch charges table implies the new plan annual charge is £427 + £63 = £490; (c) the annual recurring charges table for the new pension states £512.69 at 0.62%, implying a fund value of ~£82,692 — a figure that matches no single arrangement value and neither portfolio total; (d) the 1.08% required outperformance figure at £63 implies a fund value of only £5,833, which is arithmetically inconsistent with any stated plan value.
    Discrepancies:
      - The switch charges assessment table implies the new pension annual charge is £490 (£427 existing + £63 impact), but the annual recurring charges table for the new Aviva Platform Personal Pension states £512.69 — a difference of £22.69.
      - The 1.08% required outperformance is stated as the hurdle 'including adviser fee', but at the fund value of ~£79,074 (the existing pension), 1.08% of that value would be approximately £854, not £63. The £63 figure at 1.08% implies a notional base of only £5,833, which is not any stated fund value. The 1.08% and £63 figures are therefore arithmetically inconsistent with each other on any stated fund value.
      - The 0.08% charges-only difference at £63 implies a fund value of £78,750, which is broadly consistent with the existing Aviva pension (~£79,074) but not precisely reconciled to any stated figure.
      - The annual recurring charges table for the new pension implies a fund value of ~£82,692 (£512.69 at 0.62%), which is higher than the existing pension value of £79,092.32, suggesting a contribution or growth uplift has been applied to the new plan but not to the existing plan in the switch charges comparison — this asymmetric basis is not explained.
    Two material arithmetic inconsistencies are present in the report's own cost disclosure. First, the switch charges assessment table implies the new pension annual charge is £490 (£427 + £63), but the annual recurring charges table states £512.69. These two figures for the same charge in the same report differ by £22.69 and no explanation is given. This is an internal data inconsistency. Second, and more significantly, the report states that the plan must outperform by 1.08% annually including the adviser fee, and separately states the monetary impact is £63 per year. These two figures are irreconcilable: 1.08% of the existing fund value (~£79,074) is approximately £854; £63 at 1.08% implies a base of only £5,833. The £63 figure corresponds to the 0.08% charges-only difference, not to the 1.08% all-in hurdle. It appears the report has correctly calculated the charges-only monetary impact (£63 = 0.08% × ~£78,750) and separately stated the all-in percentage hurdle (1.08%), but has not provided, or has conflated, the monetary equivalent of the 1.08% hurdle. The reader is left unable to verify what outperformance in pounds is required when the adviser fee is included. This weakens the value justification because the stated 1.08% hurdle — which is the more meaningful consumer-facing figure — is not supported by a consistent monetary equivalent, and the only monetary figure given (£63) corresponds to the lower, charges-only comparison. The asymmetric fund value between the charges-only comparison (~£79,074 existing) and the new plan charge table (~£82,692 new) also suggests different bases are used on each side of the comparison, which is not disclosed. The guard acknowledges that higher cost is not automatically poor value, and the report does articulate benefits (platform, investment strategy, consolidation, ongoing service). However, the arithmetic inconsistencies in the cost figures themselves — particularly the mismatch between the 1.08% percentage hurdle and the £63 monetary figure — mean the value case is undermined by the report's own numbers rather than by an external contradiction. No vulnerability indicators are noted in the extraction that would materially alter this assessment.

  [G7.7] Alternatives provide better cost or benefit to the client
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The canonical model holds no extracted value at /recommendations[]/alternativesConsidered[]/comparativeCost. The suitability report (via passage evidence) states that the recommended Aviva Platform Personal Pension is justified over alternatives primarily on non-cost grounds: lower platform charge (0.32% reduced to 0.21%), risk alignment (existing funds at risk level 6 reduced to risk level 4), professional oversight, platform integration, and tax-efficient contribution. The report acknowledges the new fund strategy is 0.08% more expensive than the current plan (£63 more per year) and sets out a critical yield and RIY comparison. The switch charges assessment shows existing total charge 0.54% vs new 0.62%, with the report arguing the higher cost is justified by risk alignment, lower platform charge and other benefits.
    File:     The pension switch report [P3] provides a side-by-side comparison of projected values at age 60 under existing and new solution across low, mid and high growth rates, showing £0 or 0.0% decrease in effect of switching at low and mid growth rates and £0 decrease at high rate — i.e. the projected outcomes are materially identical. Reduction in yield is 1.6%/1.7%/1.8% existing vs 1.6%/1.7%/1.7% new. Critical yield is shown as 0.0%, 2.9% and 5.9% for the new solution. [P4] shows a separate scenario where there is a £100 or 0.1% decrease effect of switching at high growth rate. [P1] states that available solutions were ranked by maturity value including a generic stakeholder pension and the existing pension, with the top 10 shown. [P5] (suitability report) states the switch is recommended for lower costs, risk alignment, professional oversight, adviser charging facilitation, tax-efficient contribution and platform integration. [P6] (Personal Pension Plan review report) records the existing fund charges as 0.22% ongoing plus 0.04% transaction costs = 0.26% total fund costs for the alternative Vanguard funds considered. The pension switch report [P7] records the existing plan platform fee as 0.32%. The switch charges assessment (referenced in the extraction) records existing total 0.54%, new total 0.62%, difference +0.08% / +£63. The extraction records an internal inconsistency: the switch charges table implies new plan charge = £427 + £63 = £490, but the annual recurring charges table for the new pension states £512.69 — a £22.69 discrepancy. The extraction also records a minor rounding difference of £0.37 in the initial advice fee (£4,794 vs £4,794.37).
    Discrepancies:
      - The switch charges assessment implies the new plan annual monetary charge is £490 (£427 existing + £63 increase), but the annual recurring charges table for the new Aviva Platform Personal Pension states £512.69 — a difference of £22.69 that is not explained in the report.
      - The effect of switching at the high growth rate is shown as £0 or 0.0% decrease in one table [P3] but as £100 or 0.1% decrease in another table [P4], with no explanation of which scenario each table applies to or why they differ.
      - The canonical model records no extracted comparative cost value at /recommendations[]/alternativesConsidered[]/comparativeCost, meaning the report's treatment of alternatives on a cost basis is not captured in the structured model, though narrative and switch report evidence is present.
      - Initial advice fee is stated as £4,794 in the charges section but as £4,794.37 in the cashflow modelling inputs — a £0.37 rounding discrepancy.
    The requirement is whether the report demonstrates that the recommended solution provides better cost or benefit than alternatives, and whether any higher cost is justified. The switch report [P1] confirms a ranked comparison of solutions by maturity value was performed, including the existing pension and a generic stakeholder, with the top 10 shown — this provides a cost and outcome basis for selection. The report [P5] explains the higher 0.08% annual charge is offset by: risk realignment from level 6 to level 4, lower platform charge (0.32% to 0.21%), professional oversight and platform integration. These are evidenced non-cost reasons that fall within the guard (a cheaper option rejected for evidenced non-cost reasons is valid). However, two discrepancies require attention. First, the £22.69 monetary discrepancy between the implied new plan charge (£490 = £427 + £63) in the switch charges table and the stated new plan charge (£512.69) in the annual recurring charges table is a genuine internal inconsistency identified in the extraction. The two figures are on different bases: the switch charges table uses the existing fund value (£79,092.32 / £79,074.07 implied) as its basis, while the annual recurring charges table implies a fund value of approximately £82,692 — reflecting the addition of the £3,600 gross contribution and possibly growth assumptions. This difference in basis (pre-contribution vs post-contribution fund value) likely explains the monetary gap without it being an error, but the report does not make this explicit, leaving the comparison opaque. Second, the two tables in the switch report showing different 'effect of switching' figures at the high growth rate (£0 in [P3] vs £100 in [P4]) are unexplained. These appear to be two different scenarios (one with and one without the £3,600 contribution, or different charge bases), but neither table is labelled to make this clear. The overall cost disclosure is substantive — RIY, critical yield, projected values, charge percentages and monetary figures are all present — and the benefits justifying higher cost are stated and supported by research evidence. The monetary inconsistency and the divergent high-growth-rate switching impact figures are Potential Concerns rather than fatal flaws, but they require clarification because a reviewer cannot reconcile them without additional explanation. The £0.37 rounding difference in the initial fee is trivial and does not affect the finding materially. No vulnerability indicators are recorded in the file (the extraction notes /vulnerability/assessment as absent), so the vulnerability overlay does not alter this finding.

  [G7.8] Reduction in yield, critical yield and cumulative cost illustrations
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report (Critical Yield section) states RIY and critical yield figures at three growth rates for both existing and recommended pensions, all at age 60. Low: existing RIY 1.6%, recommended RIY 1.6%, critical yield 0.0%, effect of switching £0 / 0.0% decrease. Mid: existing RIY 1.7%, recommended RIY 1.7%, critical yield 2.9%, effect of switching £0 / 0.0% decrease. High: existing RIY 1.8%, recommended RIY 1.7%, critical yield 5.9%, effect of switching £0 / 0.0% decrease.
    File:     The pension switch report [P4] contains a matching comparison table at age 60. Low growth: existing RIY 1.6%, new solution RIY 1.6%, critical yield 0.0%, effect of switching £0 or 0.0% decrease. Mid growth: existing RIY 1.7%, new solution RIY 1.7%, critical yield 2.9%, effect of switching £0 or 0.0% decrease. High growth: existing RIY 1.8%, new solution RIY 1.7%, critical yield 5.9%, effect of switching £0 or 0.0% decrease. A second table [P3] appears to show different figures: mid growth existing RIY 0.6%, new solution RIY 0.7%, effect of switching £100 or 0.1% decrease; high growth existing RIY 0.7%, new solution RIY 0.7%, effect of switching £100 or 0.1% decrease. The Aviva pension illustration [P6] states a RIY of 0.6% for product and investment charges only, and 1.9% for all charges (including the 1% ongoing adviser charge), at a retirement projection to age 60. The pension projections at age 65 [P7] and age 67 [P8] show a product/investment charge RIY of 0.6% each, with no adviser charge facilitated.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report quotes the existing RIY under the high growth scenario as 1.8%; [P4] confirms 1.8% for the existing pension but [P3] shows 0.7% for the existing pension at the same scenario — the two evidence passages are internally inconsistent, with [P4] matching the report and [P3] not matching.
      - [P3] shows a mid-growth existing RIY of 0.6% and new solution RIY of 0.7%, with an effect of switching of £100 or 0.1% decrease — all of which differ from the report's Mid figures (1.7% / 1.7% / £0 / 0.0%).
      - [P3] shows a high-growth effect of switching of £100 or 0.1% decrease, versus the report's £0 / 0.0% — a difference that may reflect a different comparison run (different charges input or date).
      - The Aviva pension illustration [P6] states a total RIY (all charges) of 1.9%, whereas the report states 1.7% for the recommended plan under the mid-growth scenario; the illustration's 1.9% figure appears to include the 1% ongoing adviser charge while the switch report comparison may exclude it — the basis is not stated consistently.
      - The Aviva pension illustration [P6] states a product/investment-only RIY of 0.6%, while the switch report [P4] shows the new solution's RIY as 1.6%/1.7%/1.7% — suggesting the switch report includes additional charges (platform, adviser) that the illustration's product/investment column excludes.
      - The pension projections at age 65 [P7] and age 67 [P8] show a product/investment RIY of 0.6% with no adviser charge facilitated; these are clearly on a different basis (different age, no adviser charge) and are not directly comparable to the age-60 switch report figures.
      - The derived figures note that the implied fund value for the £63 / 0.08% annual charge difference is £78,750, while the existing pension value is £79,092.32 — a £342 difference producing a minor arithmetic inconsistency in the charge basis used for the switch comparison.
    The primary comparison — the report's RIY and critical yield figures against the pension switch report [P4] — shows full consistency across all three growth scenarios at age 60. Every figure in the report (Low 1.6%/1.6%/0.0%/£0; Mid 1.7%/1.7%/2.9%/£0; High 1.8%/1.7%/5.9%/£0) is corroborated directly by [P4]. This is the substantive finding on the consistency limb.
    
    However, a second table in the same pension switch report document [P3] presents materially different figures for the same comparison: mid-growth existing RIY 0.6%, new solution 0.7%, effect of switching £100 or 0.1% decrease; high-growth existing RIY 0.7%, new solution 0.7%, effect of switching £100 or 0.1% decrease. These are irreconcilable with [P4] within the same document and with the report's figures. The most likely explanation is that [P3] reflects an earlier run of the switch report (dated earlier or using different charges inputs), and [P4] reflects the final run that was used in the suitability report. However, both passages appear to be within the same file (Pension switch report 16-12-2025 10-06.md), and the document provides no explicit disambiguation. The co-existence of two apparently contradictory switch analyses in the file is a data quality concern even if one set is superseded.
    
    The Aviva pension illustration [P6] discloses a total RIY (all charges) of 1.9% versus the switch report's new solution RIY of 1.6%–1.7%. The guard on charge basis applies here: the illustration's 1.9% explicitly covers 'all charges' including the 1% ongoing adviser charge (per the canonical model ambiguity note), whereas the switch report comparison is conducted 'before the effect of any advice charges' per [P12]. On that basis the 1.9% and 1.7% are not the same question and the difference is explained. The illustration's product/investment-only RIY of 0.6% is also consistent with the switch report's existing pension product/investment-only charge disclosure.
    
    The minor £342 difference between the implied fund value for the charge difference (£78,750 at 0.08%) and the stated transfer value (£79,092.32) is a rounding artefact noted in the derived figures and does not constitute a material discrepancy.
    
    The vulnerability assessment path is absent from the canonical model; no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence pack that would materially affect this assessment.
    
    Overall: the report's critical yield section is consistent with the primary switch report table [P4]. The concern is the presence of a second, contradictory table [P3] within the same supporting document, which creates a data quality risk — a reviewer or client reading both tables would encounter inconsistent figures without explanation.

  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report charges 1% per annum across all Aviva Platform investments for an ongoing service that includes: (1) annual review of attitude to risk and capacity for loss; (2) review of ongoing suitability of the investment portfolio; (3) market updates and legislative changes; (4) access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of advisers for ad-hoc advice; (5) access to full holistic financial planning; (6) ongoing professional fund monitoring and portfolio review; (7) adviser charges deducted directly from the platform; and (8) annual Bed & ISA facility to transfer GIA investments into ISA/pension tax-efficiently. Review frequency is stated as annually.
    File:     The client agreement (IMPORT_2.md / Client Agreement To Proceed.md) confirms: (a) an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum of £100), automatically taken from funds invested; (b) initial fee of £4,794 (3% of funds); (c) the ongoing service includes a review meeting at least once a year and recommendations, which may include reviewing attitude to risk, suitability of plans, and market/legislation updates; (d) access to advisers for ad-hoc advice and holistic financial planning is referenced in the service description. The client agreement references the 'Important Information about our Services' document for the full service description. The Aviva key features documents confirm adviser charges can be deducted from the platform. No separate 'Important Information about our Services' document is present in the pack. The client agreement states fees 'are set out in the suitability report and any subsequent charges confirmation'.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report includes a specific Bed & ISA facility (annual transfer of GIA to ISA/pension at each review) as a named component of the ongoing service; this specific service element is not mentioned in the client agreement or any other evidential document in the pack.
      - The client agreement references a separate 'Important Information about our Services' document that sets out the full ongoing service description, but this document is not present in the pack, meaning the complete contractual service proposition cannot be verified against the report.
      - The report states the ongoing charge applies 'across all your Aviva Platform investments'; the client agreement states 1% on 'most investment funds' (subject to a minimum of £100) — the qualifier 'most' is not explained and could imply some funds are excluded, which the report does not address.
    The core ongoing service elements described in the report (annual risk review, suitability review, market/legislation updates, ad-hoc adviser access, holistic financial planning, fund monitoring) are corroborated by the client agreement at P2 and P12, which confirm a 1% ongoing annual review management charge and a service including at least annual review meetings, risk review, suitability review, and access to advisers. The charge rate of 1% matches exactly between report and client agreement. The Aviva key features documents at P13 and P14 confirm that adviser charges can be deducted directly from the platform, corroborating that service element. However, three issues remain. First, the Bed & ISA facility described as an annual ongoing service component in the report appears only in the report itself — no client agreement passage, key features document or other evidential document references it as a component of the ongoing service. This is not corroborated by any non-[I] source and therefore constitutes an unsupported assertion. Second, the 'Important Information about our Services' document, which the client agreement expressly identifies as the source of the full ongoing service description, is not in the pack. Its absence means the complete contractual service proposition cannot be verified; the corroboration available is partial. Third, the client agreement at P12 applies the 1% to 'most investment funds' whereas the report states it applies across 'all' Aviva Platform investments — the qualifier is unexplained and creates a minor disclosure inconsistency, though it may simply reflect standard contract language. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability assessment is recorded (path absent per extraction metadata), so no vulnerability-specific adjustment to this finding can be made. The primary concern is the missing corroborating document and the unsupported Bed & ISA service element — together these represent a disclosure shortfall and missing evidence, at moderate severity.

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts five benefits to justify the recommendation: (1) Lower Costs — moving to agency terms reduces the platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%; (2) Risk Alignment — current funds are at risk level 6, too high for the agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4, and the switch corrects this; (3) Professional Oversight — ongoing fund monitoring and portfolio review; (4) Tax-Efficient Contribution — £3,600 gross pension contribution with 20% tax relief; (5) Platform Integration — pension, ISA and GIA on the same Aviva Platform for administrative convenience. The cost justification states the net charge increase of 0.08% (£63 p.a.) is justified by improved risk alignment and professional oversight, and that a critical yield of 2.94% (including adviser fees) is achievable given past fund performance and improved risk alignment.
    File:     The pension switching research report [P5] confirms the agreed risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) and that the investment amount is £79,092. The existing arrangements are on the Aviva Platform with Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% Equity funds [P21]. The existing plan transaction history [P12–P18] shows the existing plan is already on the Aviva Platform — meaning platform integration already exists to a significant degree. The research reports [P2, P5] confirm Aviva Platform as the recommended platform. The client performance report [P16] confirms the existing pension value of £79,092.32 and shows 3-year cumulative performance of 33.91% excluding charges. The suitability report itself [P20] includes a performance comparison table showing the existing strategy (45.28% over 5 years) outperforming the recommended Aviva Multi-asset Plus II fund (30.12% over 5 years) and the ABI benchmark (18.68%), with the report acknowledging the current funds have outperformed the recommended funds but suffered steeper losses. No independent provider illustration or key features document is in the pack showing the new plan's projected values or RIY. The ongoing adviser charge service proposition is described in the report [P22] as including risk reviews, portfolio suitability reviews, market updates, ad-hoc advice access, and holistic financial planning. No separate client agreement for the 1% ongoing charge is in the pack. There is no evidence in the file corroborating the 'Professional Oversight' benefit beyond the report's own description. The existing plan already provides flexible drawdown and a large fund range [P11, P21].
    Discrepancies:
      - The report claims 'Lower Costs' as a benefit justifying the switch, asserting the platform charge falls from 0.32% to 0.21%. However, the total annual recurring charge increases from 0.54% (existing) to 0.62% (new plan) — a net cost increase of 0.08% (£63 p.a.) — so the overall effect is higher cost, not lower. The 'lower cost' benefit applies only to the platform charge layer and is offset by a higher investment management charge; the report does not explain this offsetting effect in the benefits section.
      - The report states £490 implied new plan annual charge (£427 existing + £63 increase per switch charges assessment table), but the annual recurring charges table for the new pension states £512.69. This £22.69 discrepancy is flagged in the extraction's internal inconsistencies and is confirmed by the derived figures: 0.62% on £82,691.94 = £512.69, while 0.08% on £78,750 = £63 implies a different fund base. The two monetary figures for the new plan charge are inconsistent within the report.
      - The 'Platform Integration' benefit implies that bringing pension, ISA and GIA onto the Aviva Platform is a consolidation gain. However, the existing pension is already on the Aviva Platform [P12–P18, P21], so this benefit does not fully apply to the pension switch element — the pension is not being moved to a new platform, it is being restructured within the same platform. The ISA and GIA transfers do represent genuine integration, but the report presents this as a uniform benefit across the recommendation without distinguishing which products genuinely benefit.
      - The report relies on 'Professional Oversight' to justify the cost increase but no independent evidence in the file corroborates the service proposition or confirms a client agreement specifying the 1% ongoing charge. The service description appears only within the suitability report itself [P22], which cannot self-corroborate.
      - The critical yield achievability assertion ('I believe this is achievable given the improved risk alignment and professional oversight') is made against past performance of the recommended fund. The performance table in the report [P20] shows the existing strategy outperforming the recommended fund over all periods shown (45.28% vs 30.12% over 5 years), which the report acknowledges but then qualifies by reference to volatility. No provider illustration or independent projection is in the pack to support the critical yield claim independently.
      - The derived figures show that 0.08% on £78,750 = £63 (the switch charge table basis) and 0.62% on £82,691.94 = £512.69 (the charges table basis), implying the two tables use different fund values as their base. The report does not explain why the fund value used in the switch charges assessment (implied ~£78,750) differs from the fund value implied by the new plan charges table (~£82,692). This is an unexplained internal inconsistency in the charge arithmetic.
    The requirement is whether the benefits relied upon to justify the higher cost are evidenced and consistent. Several issues arise.
    
    1. LOWER COSTS BENEFIT: The report presents a platform charge reduction (0.32% → 0.21%) as a 'Lower Costs' benefit in the benefits section, but this is misleading in isolation: the total annual charge increases from 0.54% to 0.62% because the investment management charge on the new fund (0.41%) exceeds the existing fund charge (0.22%). The benefits section does not make clear that the platform saving is more than offset by a higher fund charge. The 'Like for Like' section does address the net 0.08% increase, but the juxtaposition of a 'Lower Costs' benefit label against a net cost increase without immediate qualification in the same section is a disclosure weakness.
    
    2. MONETARY CHARGE INCONSISTENCY: The switch charges assessment table implies the new pension annual charge is £490 (£427 + £63), but the annual recurring charges table states £512.69. The derived figures confirm £512.69 is the arithmetically correct figure at 0.62% on ~£82,692. The £63 increment appears to be computed on a slightly different base (~£78,750 vs ~£82,692). This is an unexplained internal inconsistency, not merely rounding, and overstates the apparent cost benefit of the comparison by making the increase look smaller (£63 vs £85.69 actual difference).
    
    3. RISK ALIGNMENT BENEFIT: This is the strongest benefit. The research report [P5] confirms the agreed risk level of 4 and the existing fund risk level of 6 is documented in the suitability report [P21] and corroborated by the transaction history showing Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% equity funds. The performance analysis in [P20] provides comparative data. This benefit has genuine support in the file, albeit the supporting performance comparison comes from the report itself [P20] rather than an independent document.
    
    4. PROFESSIONAL OVERSIGHT BENEFIT: The service proposition appears only within the suitability report [P22]. No client agreement or independent fee disclosure document is in the pack. The report cannot self-corroborate. This benefit is asserted to justify the cost increase without independent evidential support in the file — this is the most significant gap.
    
    5. PLATFORM INTEGRATION BENEFIT: The existing pension is already on the Aviva Platform [P12–P18, P21]. Moving pension funds within the same platform is restructuring, not integration. The benefit is real for the ISA and GIA (which are new to the platform), but the report does not distinguish. Consolidation convenience is noted as a real but non-financial benefit per the guard; it is accordingly weighed as stated.
    
    6. CRITICAL YIELD ACHIEVABILITY: The critical yield of 2.94% is stated as achievable based on past performance, but the performance table the report relies on [P20] shows the existing strategy outperforming the recommended fund across all periods. The report addresses this by reference to volatility, which is a plausible qualitative argument, but no independent projection or RIY document is present. The achievability assertion is weakly supported.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: No vulnerability assessment is present in the pack (/vulnerability/assessment absent per extraction). No vulnerability indicators in the retrieved passages affect this specific cost/benefit assessment.
    
    Overall: The 'Lower Costs' label against a net cost increase (partially explained but not clearly in the benefits section), the monetary inconsistency between the two cost tables (£490 implied vs £512.69 stated), and the absence of independent corroboration of the Professional Oversight service proposition collectively constitute material concerns. The monetary inconsistency is particularly notable because it makes the cost increase appear smaller than the charges table implies, and the inconsistency is not acknowledged in the report.

  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The recommendation is more expensive: +0.08% pa (fund charge basis, excluding adviser fee). The report states the proposed plan must outperform the existing plan by approximately 1.08% annually (including the 1% ongoing adviser charge) to match the same net growth. Critical yield projections at three growth rates are: Low (0.00%) £74,000 / RIY 1.6%; Mid (2.94%) £83,300 / RIY 1.7%; High (5.88%) £93,400 / RIY 1.8%. The 'Including Adviser Fee' section states 1.08% required outperformance annually. A monetary charge difference of £63 pa is cited in the switch charges assessment (existing £427, impact +£63). The new plan annual recurring charges table states £512.69 total (0.62%).
    File:     The pension switch report [P7] shows a 'before adviser charge' comparison: Low growth £74,000 / RIY 1.6% / critical yield 0.0% / effect £0 or 0.0%; Mid growth £83,300 / RIY 1.7% / critical yield 2.9% / effect £0 or 0.0%; High growth £93,400 / RIY 1.8% / critical yield 5.9% / effect £0 or 0.0%. The 'after adviser charge' comparison in [P2] shows: Mid growth existing £86,800 / new £86,700 / existing RIY 0.6% / new RIY 0.7%; High growth existing £97,400 / new £97,300 / existing RIY 0.7% / new RIY 0.7%. The switch report [P2] also records an initial adviser charge of £0.00 and ongoing annual adviser charge of 1.00% considered in new solution projections. The derived figures note: £427+£63=£490 implied new plan charge vs £512.69 stated in the annual recurring charges table — a £22.69 discrepancy. The derived figures also show the 0.08% difference implies a fund value of £78,750 while the existing fund value is £79,092.32, a minor basis difference. The switch report projects [P6] existing pension at: Low £77,100, Mid £86,800, High £97,400 — these differ from the suitability report's Critical Yield section projections (Low £74,000, Mid £83,300, High £93,400). The pension illustration [P5] is on file. The 1.08% required outperformance figure does not appear in any retrieved passage.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report's Critical Yield section projections for the existing pension (Low £74,000, Mid £83,300, High £93,400) differ from the pension switch report's projected maturity values for the existing pension (Low £77,100, Mid £86,800, High £97,400) — a difference of £3,000–£4,100 at each rate.
      - The switch charges assessment implies a new plan annual charge of £490 (£427+£63) but the annual recurring charges table states £512.69 — a £22.69 discrepancy noted in the extracted internal inconsistencies.
      - The 1.08% required outperformance figure (including adviser fee) is asserted in the suitability report but does not appear in any retrieved supporting passage — it is unconfirmed by independent evidence.
      - The 0.08% charge difference figure is not matched by any retrieved passage (nearest is 0.1% in [P2] which is the 'effect of switching' percentage, not the charge differential).
      - The suitability report's RIY figures (1.6%, 1.7%, 1.8%) align with the switch report's 'before adviser charge' table [P7] but the switch report's 'after adviser charge' table [P2] shows materially lower RIY figures (0.6%/0.7% existing, 0.7%/0.7% new) — these are on different bases (before vs after adviser charge) which the report does not explicitly reconcile.
      - The critical yield figures cited in the suitability report (0.0%, 2.9%, 5.9%) match the pension switch report's 'before adviser charge' table [P7] but the report presents them alongside the 1.08% required outperformance claim without clearly distinguishing the two bases.
    This requirement concerns whether the report identifies and justifies higher charges producing a required outperformance, and whether the projections and yield figures are consistent with the evidence. Several issues arise. First, the most significant discrepancy is in the projected values used in the suitability report's Critical Yield section versus those in the pension switch report. The suitability report states existing pension projections of £74,000 (Low), £83,300 (Mid), £93,400 (High). The pension switch report [P6] states existing pension projections of £77,100 (Low), £86,800 (Mid), £97,400 (High). These differ by £3,000–£4,100 at each growth rate. Both are in real terms adjusted for inflation (confirmed by [P6] footnotes and [P5]). This is not a basis difference — both use the same growth rates and the same inflation adjustment — and is a genuine data inconsistency. The effect on the new solution projections is also inconsistent: the suitability report's Critical Yield table shows equal projected values for existing and new plan (£74,000/£74,000, £83,300/£83,300, £93,400/£93,400 per [P7]), reflecting the 'before adviser charge' basis, while the switch report's 'after adviser charge' table [P2] shows slightly lower new plan values (£86,700 vs £86,800, £97,300 vs £97,400). The report presents the Critical Yield analysis on the 'before adviser charge' basis but then asserts 1.08% required outperformance 'including adviser fee' in an adjacent section. This dual-basis presentation is not clearly explained for the reader. Second, the 1.08% required outperformance figure is a report-only assertion with no corroborating passage in the file. The figure is arithmetically plausible (0.08% charge difference + 1.00% adviser fee = 1.08%), so it is not impossible, but no independent document confirms it. However, the guard on required outperformance notes that figures under ~0.5% pa are within noise; 1.08% is materially above that threshold and warrants clear support. Third, the monetary charge inconsistency (£490 implied vs £512.69 stated) is noted — the fund value basis for the new plan (implied ~£82,691 at 0.62%) differs from the existing plan value (£79,092.32) because the new plan includes the £3,600 pension contribution, meaning the fund values are not identical. This partially explains the discrepancy but is not articulated in the report. Fourth, the projections in the suitability report's Critical Yield section are materially lower than the switch report projections for the same existing plan. The switch report is dated the same day (16/12/2025) and uses the same growth rates. This difference is unexplained and represents a data inconsistency in a key metric used to assess the value of the switch. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability assessment is on file (noted as absent in extraction), so no additional adjustment is required here beyond the baseline finding. Overall: the required outperformance is above the noise threshold, the 1.08% figure lacks independent corroboration, the existing pension projections differ between the suitability report and the switch report, and the monetary charge difference is not fully reconciled. These are material issues for a like-for-like cost analysis.

CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.3, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). The report states all other risks are detailed in product literature, wholly delegating specific product and investment risks (e.g. currency, emerging markets, drawdown depletion, death benefit tax treatment post-75) to the product literature rather than explaining them in the report itself. The product literature confirms these risks exist but they are not summarised in the report. No explicit loss scenario or reduction-in-yield projection is present in the report or anywhere in the evidence pack. The report does not illustrate a stressed or lower-growth outcome to show how the client's fund or income could be adversely affected. The report's outperformance requirement calculation contains an internal inconsistency noted in the extraction: the switch charges table implies a new plan annual charge of £490 (£427+£63), whereas the annual recurring charges table states £512.69. The report does not explain or reconcile this difference, which affects the accuracy of the '£63 outperformance' disadvantage disclosure.

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report discloses the following material disadvantages and risks: (1) The recommended plan charges are 0.08% more expensive than the current plan on a like-for-like basis (excluding adviser fee), requiring outperformance of £63 in year one. (2) Including the 1% ongoing adviser fee, total annual cost rises to approximately 1.62% versus the current 0.54%, requiring approximately 1.08% annual outperformance. (3) The current Moneybox Cash ISA rate is circa 4.5% but is variable; the recommended ISA funds have averaged 4.72% over 5 years but past performance is not guaranteed. (4) Although current funds have outperformed recommended funds over 5 years, they suffered much steeper losses. (5) GIA gains above the £3,000 CGT annual exemption are taxable and dividend income above £500 is taxable. (6) All other risks are detailed in product literature provided. (7) An Expression of Wish form has been posted as beneficiary nomination is uncertain. The report also describes the mechanics and risks of flexi-access drawdown, including tax on withdrawals at marginal rate, the 25% tax-free lump sum, annual allowance constraints, and cashflow modelling assumptions. No explicit loss scenario (e.g. reduction in yield or stressed projection) is presented beyond the cost differential framing.
    File:     The Aviva Pension Key Features document discloses: value can go down as well as up and the client may get back less than paid in; income drawdown risks including fund depletion if income is too high; tax position risks including overpayment of tax requiring a tax return; death benefit tax treatment (tax-free before 75, taxable at marginal rate after 75); the risk that favourable tax treatment may change; delays in payments/transfers in certain circumstances; disinvestment to cover charges if insufficient cash. The Aviva ISA Key Features discloses: money invested can go down as well as up and may be worth less than invested; restrictions on ISA allowance; flexible withdrawal rights. The Aviva GIA Key Features discloses: value can go down as well as up; tax may be payable on transfer; GIA forms part of the estate on death; risks from emerging markets, smaller companies and currency movements. The Aviva fund factsheet discloses: specific risk flags including foreign exchange risk, emerging markets, smaller companies, fixed interest, high yield bonds, property, alternative investment funds, derivatives. The policy research file confirms the existing plan details. The fact find records no vulnerability flags, no accessible format requirements, and notes the client did not complete an ATRQ at the meeting. No reduction-in-yield illustration or explicit loss scenario (e.g. projected fund value under stress or lower growth rate) is present in the evidence pack.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states all other risks are detailed in product literature, wholly delegating specific product and investment risks (e.g. currency, emerging markets, drawdown depletion, death benefit tax treatment post-75) to the product literature rather than explaining them in the report itself. The product literature confirms these risks exist but they are not summarised in the report.
      - No explicit loss scenario or reduction-in-yield projection is present in the report or anywhere in the evidence pack. The report does not illustrate a stressed or lower-growth outcome to show how the client's fund or income could be adversely affected.
      - The report's outperformance requirement calculation contains an internal inconsistency noted in the extraction: the switch charges table implies a new plan annual charge of £490 (£427+£63), whereas the annual recurring charges table states £512.69. The report does not explain or reconcile this difference, which affects the accuracy of the '£63 outperformance' disadvantage disclosure.
      - The GIA tax disclosure states capital gains above £3,000 are taxable but does not address the client's specific tax position (basic vs higher rate) or quantify the likely tax exposure given the £57,120 GIA investment, leaving the tax consequence generic rather than client-specific.
      - Income drawdown risks (fund depletion from excessive withdrawals, tax on income, death benefit tax after age 75) are present in the product literature but not summarised in the report body; the report refers only to product literature for these.
      - The report does not address the risk of the £100,000 inheritance (noted in the fact find as 'due this month') not materialising before investment is made, which is a client-specific financial risk relevant to the GIA recommendation.
    The report discloses several client-specific disadvantages: the cost differential with and without adviser fee, the variable-rate ISA switch risk, the CGT/dividend tax treatment of the GIA, the past-performance caveat, and the steeper loss profile of current funds. These are genuine and meaningful disclosures. However, a number of material gaps remain.
    
    First, the report wholly delegates specific product and investment risks to product literature via a single boilerplate sentence. The product literature (Aviva Pension KFD, ISA KFD, GIA KFD, fund factsheet) contains material risks including: income drawdown fund depletion risk, post-75 death benefit taxation, currency and emerging markets risks inherent in the recommended multi-asset fund, and the risk that tax treatment may change. These are not summarised in the report. Under COBS 4.2.1R the report itself must be fair, clear and not misleading; delegation to product literature for all such risks falls short, particularly for a client who did not complete an ATRQ on the day and whose engagement level with documentation cannot be confirmed.
    
    Second, no loss scenario is presented anywhere in the pack. The report provides cashflow modelling inputs and growth assumptions but does not illustrate what happens to the client's fund or income if growth is below assumption or markets fall. The 'steeper losses' reference for current funds is comparative, not a forward-looking loss scenario for the recommendation. This is a material gap under the How to Decide criteria.
    
    Third, the monetary outperformance figure (£63) is internally inconsistent: the switch charges table implies £490 as the new plan charge (£427+£63) but the charges table states £512.69, a £22.69 difference. The report does not reconcile this, meaning the stated disadvantage figure may be understated. This is a data inconsistency affecting the accuracy of the disclosure.
    
    Fourth, the GIA tax disclosure is generic. Given the investment amount of £57,120 and the client's tax position (not stated in report), the likely scale of annual CGT or dividend tax is not illustrated.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: the fact find records no accessible format requirement and no vulnerability flags. The client did not complete the ATRQ at the meeting. There is no vulnerability assessment on file (noted as absent in the extraction). The absence of a vulnerability assessment means the adviser cannot confirm whether the client has any comprehension or engagement risk that would require a more prominent or simplified risk summary. This does not itself create a CHK-008 finding but reinforces the concern about delegation of material risk disclosure to product literature.
    
    The guards: risks spread across sections — the report does address disadvantages in several places (Like for Like, Including Adviser Fee, Alternative Solutions, GIA section, Other Considerations). That is noted. However, the complete absence of any loss scenario and the boilerplate delegation of all product-specific investment risks are not explained by the spreading guard. The internal monetary inconsistency is a separate finding. Taken together, the gaps are moderate in severity: disadvantages are partially disclosed but material items (loss scenarios, drawdown depletion, death benefit tax, quantified tax exposure) are absent or delegated.

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report discloses the following product-specific and investment-specific risks: (1) liquidity/access delay of 10-15 working days for disinvestment; (2) pension IHT change from 6 April 2027 for unused DC pensions; (3) death after age 75 — death benefits taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate; (4) cancellation risk — previous provider not obliged to accept return; (5) cancellation market loss risk; (6) excess contribution tax charge if contributions exceed earnings; (7) existing fund risk mismatch (risk level 6 vs agreed level 4) with steeper historical losses; (8) critical yield including adviser fee — new plan must outperform by 1.08% annually; (9) cash inflation erosion risk; (10) market timing risk during switch; (11) no guarantee of outperformance of new arrangement; (12) unit prices and fund values can fall as well as rise, no guarantee of return of capital; (13) critical yield at mid-growth rate of 2.94% including advice fees to match current plan value by age 60. These disclosures appear across multiple sections: 'Other Considerations of Switching', 'Recommended Solution - Pension', 'Cancellation notice', 'Contributions', 'Performance Assessment', 'Like for Like', 'Alternative Solutions', 'Fund recommendation', 'Including Adviser Fee'.
    File:     Product literature on file discloses: [P7] CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc KIID — risk rating 4, derivative risk, fixed interest securities risk, credit risk, smaller companies risk, emerging markets risk, liquidity risk; fund may not be appropriate for investors withdrawing within 5 years. [P3] CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc factsheet — general capital-at-risk warning, full risk list referred to in KIID/KID and prospectus. [P2/P4/P8] Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II S14 factsheet — risk warnings A (general), B (foreign exchange), C (emerging markets), E (fixed interest), F (derivatives); suspend trading risk; derivatives risk; specialist fund risk. [P6] Aviva ISA Key Features — tax treatment of ISA may change; property illiquidity risk; fund deferment/suspension risk; cancellation note. [P9] Aviva Pension Key Features — value can go down as well as up; transfer may result in loss of guaranteed income benefits; importance of considering charges, fund range, valuable benefits lost and tax implications. [P5/P10] Aviva GIA Key Features — charges impact on growth; emerging markets, small company, currency exchange risks; withdrawals reduce value. [P14/P16] Pension projections — lower/medium/higher growth scenarios; value can go up or down; may be worth less than paid in; charges may vary. [P15] Pension review report — existing fund 10-year volatility maps to risk level 6, outside agreed risk level 4. [P17] Fact find — client objectives and risk profile noted. [P18] Cashflow higher inflation scenario — illustrates potential outcomes under higher inflation. [P12] Suitability report (internal) — capital gains tax rates, ISA tax treatment. No search results were returned for Q8.2.4 (loss scenarios/consumer harm illustration section).
    Discrepancies:
      - The product literature for the CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc [P7] identifies specific risks not reflected in the report by name: derivative risk, fixed interest securities risk, credit risk, smaller companies risk, emerging markets risk, and liquidity risk. The report's investment risk disclosures do not address these fund-specific risks by name or category.
      - The Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II S14 factsheet [P4/P8] identifies foreign exchange risk (B), emerging markets risk (C), fixed interest risk (E), derivatives risk (F), and suspend-trading risk as applicable to that fund. The report does not name foreign exchange, fixed interest, derivatives or fund suspension risks in its investment risk disclosures.
      - The Aviva Pension Key Features document [P9] highlights that on a pension transfer the client may lose valuable benefits (e.g. guaranteed income based on final salary) and that the transfer could be complex. The report does not address what specific benefits, if any, are being given up by switching from the existing Aviva pension — it records a risk-level mismatch and a critical yield but does not confirm whether any guaranteed or protected benefits attach to the existing arrangement and are forfeited.
      - The search for Q8.2.4 (loss scenarios / consumer harm illustrations) returned no results. The report states critical yield figures and references performance charts showing steeper losses on existing funds, but no explicit quantified loss scenario (e.g. a specific monetary fall in fund value under an adverse growth assumption) is presented in the manner of a structured loss illustration.
      - The report discloses the pension IHT change from April 2027 as a risk [PensionIHTChange2027] but does not quantify or illustrate the potential IHT consequence for this client (e.g. the size of the taxable estate impact or marginal rate applicable), leaving the disclosure generic rather than client-specific in its effect despite being flagged as isClientSpecific.
      - The report does not mention fund suspension / deferral risk for the GIA or ISA wrappers, which is identified as a material risk in the Aviva ISA Key Features [P6] and Aviva GIA Key Features [P10].
      - The report does not address currency/foreign exchange risk, which is relevant to the recommended funds given their overseas exposure as indicated in the product literature [P8].
    The report contains a substantial body of risk disclosure: market risk, critical yield (with and without adviser fee), risk-level mismatch, IHT change, death-after-75 tax, access delay, cancellation risks, excess contribution tax charge, and cash inflation erosion. The general capital-at-risk warning is present and is product-relevant. The critical yield disclosure at 1.08% (including the adviser fee) and 2.94% (mid-growth rate) is client-specific and quantified, which is good practice for a pension switch.
    
    However, the product literature in the pack identifies a set of fund-specific risks — derivative risk, fixed interest securities risk, credit risk, smaller companies risk, emerging markets risk, liquidity risk (CT Universal MAP KIID [P7]) and foreign exchange, emerging markets, fixed interest, derivatives, and fund suspension risks (Aviva Multi-Asset Plus factsheet [P4/P8]) — that are not named or addressed individually in the report. The guard notes that standard capital-at-risk wording satisfies the general warning but not product-specific risks, and these must be tested separately. The report's risk section does not reference these by category. While product literature is provided to the client, deferring product-specific risk disclosure entirely to that literature is a limitation the guard expressly flags as a finding.
    
    The Aviva Pension Key Features [P9] emphasises that a pension transfer may involve giving up valuable guaranteed benefits. The report identifies the risk-level mismatch and critical yield but does not address whether any protected benefits (guaranteed annuity rates, guaranteed growth rates) exist on the existing Aviva pension and are being forfeited. The extraction notes that surrenderValue and projectedValueAtRetirement for EA1 are absent from the document, suggesting limited disclosure of existing plan terms. If no such benefits exist, the report should say so; silence on this point is a disclosure shortfall for a pension switch.
    
    Fund suspension/deferral risk is explicitly identified in both the Aviva ISA Key Features [P6] and GIA Key Features [P10] as a risk to the client's ability to access money. This is absent from the report.
    
    Foreign exchange risk applies to the recommended funds given overseas holdings indicated in the factsheets but is not mentioned in the report.
    
    On loss scenarios: the report references performance charts showing steeper historical losses on the existing funds and provides critical yield figures, which constitute partial loss scenario information. However, the Q8.2.4 search returned nothing, and no quantified adverse-scenario monetary loss illustration (of the type shown in the pension projections [P14/P16] where the lower growth scenario shows e.g. £73,100 vs £124,000 at higher growth) is clearly embedded as a consumer harm illustration in the report. The pension projections themselves are on file [P14/P16] and do present lower/medium/higher scenarios, but these are supporting documents rather than disclosures within the report itself. This is a moderate concern — loss scenario information exists in the supporting file but its presence in the report is not confirmed.
    
    The IHT disclosure, while present, is generic in its quantification; however, this is a lesser concern given the legislative change is itself general and client-specific modelling may not be feasible at this stage.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: The vulnerability assessment path is noted as absent from the document (/vulnerability/assessment NotPresentInDocument). No vulnerability indicators are identified in the retrieved passages. No vulnerability-related adjustment to this finding is therefore required.
    
    Overall: the report covers the principal financial and switch-related risks well but omits product-specific fund risks (derivative, fixed interest, credit, emerging markets, liquidity, FX, fund suspension) that the product literature on file identifies as applicable. It also does not confirm whether guaranteed benefits are being forfeited. These are disclosure shortfalls on a pension switch rather than a complete absence of risk disclosure, placing the severity at Moderate rather than High.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states the following tax consequences: (1) Pension contributions attract 20% basic rate tax relief — a net contribution of £2,880 becomes £3,600 gross, with the government adding £720; (2) If contributions exceed earnings in the tax year, a tax charge arises, with the excess treated as additional income taxed at the marginal rate; (3) ISA gains, interest and dividends are free of Income Tax and CGT; (4) GIA capital gains above the £3,000 annual exemption are taxed at 18% (basic) or 24% (higher/additional rate); (5) GIA dividend income has a £500 tax-free allowance, above which 8.75% (basic), 33.75% (higher) or 39.35% (additional) applies; (6) Pension death benefits are tax-free if the client dies before 75; on or after 75, payments are subject to the nominee's marginal rate; (7) From 6 April 2027, most unused DC pensions will be included in the taxable estate for IHT; currently IHT liability is £0. The report confirms the client's current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended £3,600 gross contribution.
    File:     The case file (passages [I] category) repeats and supports the above disclosures as part of the suitability report itself. The pension projection illustrations [P14, P23] show lower/medium/higher growth scenarios with projected fund values, tax-free lump sum figures, and annuity estimates at ages 65 and 67, providing loss scenario context consistent with what the report references. The pension research report [P2] confirms death benefit options (lump sum, drawdown) for dependants and nominees. The pension switching research [P1] shows maturity value comparisons including the existing solution. No external (non-[I]) passage contradicts any of the tax disclosures in the report. No passage in the file addresses the client's actual current-year earnings quantum, leaving the assertion that 'current earnings are sufficient' unverified by evidence external to the report. No passage corroborates the claim from outside the report.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the client's current earnings are sufficient to support the £3,600 gross contribution but does not quantify those earnings, and no external evidence in the file confirms the earnings figure — the assertion is self-standing and unsupported by any non-[I] document.
      - The extraction flags an ambiguity: it is unclear whether the contribution is justified on confirmed earnings exceeding £3,600 or on the £3,600 non-earner allowance — the report says 'earnings are sufficient' without quantifying them, which is a different basis from the non-earner allowance and materially affects the tax relief legitimacy.
      - The report does not address the CGT consequence of any disposal that would be triggered by moving assets from the existing GIA (Chartered, £40,000) into the Aviva Platform GIA — no gain/loss figure on the existing GIA assets is stated or evidenced, so the tax cost of the recommended switch is unaddressed for that wrapper.
      - Search Q8.3.4 returned nothing, indicating that at least one expected evidence signal is absent from the file.
    The report's tax disclosures are detailed and cover all material product types recommended: pension relief and excess-contribution risk, ISA tax exemption, GIA CGT and dividend tax, pension death benefits, and the forthcoming IHT change. These are product-specific rather than generic boilerplate, satisfying the main limb of the requirement. The death benefit and IHT disclosures in [P8] and [P4] are [I]-category passages and so are internal consistency only, not independent corroboration — but no external passage contradicts them either.
    
    Two concerns arise. First, the report states the client's earnings are sufficient to support the £3,600 gross contribution but nowhere quantifies those earnings, and no external document in the file evidences them. The extraction flags this as an ambiguity: if the basis is actually the £3,600 non-earner allowance rather than confirmed earnings, the statement 'your current earnings are sufficient' is potentially misleading about the basis on which tax relief is available. The guard notes that a tax consequence stated without the client's tax band established is unverifiable rather than necessarily wrong, but this goes further — the very basis (earnings vs non-earner allowance) is unresolved, and the report makes a positive assertion about sufficiency that no evidence supports.
    
    Second, the recommendation includes switching the existing GIA (Chartered, £40,000) to an Aviva Platform GIA. The report explains CGT rates and the annual exemption but does not address whether the switch itself triggers a disposal and what any CGT cost would be. No gain or base-cost information for the Chartered GIA holdings is present in the file. This is a tax consequence of the specific recommendation that is unaddressed.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability indicators are identified in the evidence that would heighten the concern here beyond the standard disclosure requirement.
    
    The CGT-on-switch omission and the unverified earnings-sufficiency assertion are the substantive concerns. Neither is a wholesale failure to disclose tax consequences (which would be High severity), but both are material omissions for specific recommended transactions — Moderate severity is appropriate.

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report contains a dedicated section headed 'Other Considerations of Switching' in the main body (not appendix only). It states: unit prices and fund values can fall as well as rise; no guarantee of return of original capital; a switch does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement; no guarantee new provider's funds will outperform existing. Tax treatment sections cover CGT rates (£3,000 annual exemption, 18%/24%), dividend tax, interest/PSA rules for the GIA, and ISA tax-free status. Lump sum allowance (£268,275), lump sum and death benefit allowance (£1,073,100) and overseas transfer allowance are disclosed. Capacity for loss is assessed as Medium-High with narrative justification. Cashflow modelling includes a market-crash scenario. The report notes the new plan is 0.08% more expensive (£63 year one outperformance required). Stakeholder pension characteristics disclosed as alternative considered. Cancellation rights (30 days) disclosed including that the previous provider is not obliged to take the plan back. Death benefit tax treatment (pre/post-75) disclosed. Knowledge and experience recorded as very limited for most product types. Risk profile Cautious Balanced (Risk Level 4) explained.
    File:     The pension switch report (P9, P10, P11) provides projected maturity values at three growth rates (low 0.00%, mid 2.94%, high 5.88%) for both existing and new solution, reduction in yield figures, critical yield figures, and effect of switching. At mid growth rate with adviser charges included, the effect of switching is £100 or 0.1% decrease (P11). At mid growth rate without adviser charges the effect is £0 (P10). Critical yield at high growth rate is 5.9% (P9, P11). The switch report records no benefits listed for the existing product (P11). The GIA illustration (P13) shows year-by-year projected values including after-charges figures that are materially lower than before-charges values in year 1 (£53,800 vs £59,600 before charges). The cashflow market-crash scenario (P14) is on file. The pension switch report footnote (P10) explicitly states: 'Any fees applied at the point of switching may reduce the capital value of the pension which will reduce any death benefit payable.'
    Discrepancies:
      - The switch report (P11) records 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' under the Benefits section — the suitability report does not explicitly state what guaranteed benefits or protections (if any) are being given up, beyond a general non-obligation-to-take-back cancellation note; the switch report's own finding that no benefits are lost is not surfaced in the report's body.
      - The pension switch report (P10) states explicitly that fees at the point of switching may reduce capital value and death benefit; this specific consequence is not stated in the suitability report's body (the report discusses death benefit tax treatment and cancellation but does not link switching fees to reduced death benefit payable).
      - The switch report (P11) shows critical yield at high growth of 5.9%, which the suitability report does not mention in the body (the outperformance requirement of £63/0.08% is stated but no critical yield figure appears in the main report text).
      - The GIA illustration (P13) shows a material charge impact (year 1: £59,600 before charges vs £53,800 after all charges on £57,100 invested) — this level of charge drag on the GIA is not illustrated in the report's body as a loss scenario for the GIA specifically.
      - The report states the recommended fund strategy is 0.08% more expensive, implying £63 year-one outperformance; the derived figures note that the implied new plan charge of £490 (£427+£63) is inconsistent with the stated £512.69 in the annual charges table — the report does not address or explain this £22.69 discrepancy, meaning the quantification of the cost disadvantage may be understated.
      - The report's risk section appears to address investment risk generically ('unit prices can fall as well as rise') without product-specific risk content for the GIA and ISA wrappers beyond tax treatment.
    Overall, the report contains a reasonably broad range of risk and disadvantage disclosures: capital-at-risk warnings, no-guarantee-of-outperformance warnings, tax consequences for all three wrappers (pension, ISA, GIA), capacity for loss assessment, cashflow modelling including a stress scenario, and a dedicated 'Other Considerations of Switching' section in the body. Prominence is adequate — the section is in the main body, not solely an appendix.
    
    However, several gaps are identified:
    
    1. The pension switch report (P11) explicitly records 'No benefits were listed for the existing products'. While this is a favourable finding (no safeguarded benefits lost), the suitability report does not surface this conclusion explicitly. This is a minor disclosure gap rather than a material omission.
    
    2. The switch report (P10) flags that switching fees may reduce capital value and death benefit. The suitability report discusses death benefit tax treatment but does not make this link. This is a specific, product-relevant risk that is in the supporting file but absent from the report — a disclosure shortfall.
    
    3. Critical yield (5.9% at high growth) is quantified in the switch report but not mentioned in the suitability report body. The report states the outperformance requirement in monetary terms (£63) but does not give the critical yield rate, which is the standard metric for assessing transfer value.
    
    4. The GIA illustration shows significant charge drag (year 1: £59,600 before charges vs £53,800 after all charges — a £5,800 reduction on £57,100 invested, ~10%). The report does not illustrate a comparable loss/charge scenario for the GIA, leaving the GIA risk presentation weaker than for the pension.
    
    5. The monetary charge inconsistency (£490 implied vs £512.69 stated) means the report's own quantification of the cost disadvantage of switching is potentially understated by ~£23 per year, which is not disclosed.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: The report records very limited knowledge and experience across most product types and describes the client as 'somewhat anxious' about investment decisions (Cautious Balanced profile). Given this, clear and prominent risk disclosure is especially important. The gaps identified above — particularly the absence of a GIA loss scenario and the missing critical yield — are more material in this context because the client may not independently appreciate these risks from product literature alone.
    
    None of the false-positive guards explain these gaps: the differences are not a matter of different bases, different dates or different wrappers making the comparison unsafe — they are risks identified in the file that are absent from the report's body. The severity is Moderate: risk content is present and broadly correct, but specific material risks (GIA charge drag, critical yield, death-benefit/switching-fee link) are absent from the body of the report while present in supporting documents.

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report states that the existing pension (Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd, policy AV2075926) has no safeguarded features including no TFC protection, no GARs, and no MIG. It records the existing plan features as: good platform with comprehensive facilities; full flexi-access drawdown and UFPLS available; over 6,900 funds available; allows servicing adviser. The extraction path /replacementAnalysis[]/featuresOrGuaranteesLost[] is absent from the canonical model, meaning no explicit 'features being given up' section was captured from the report.
    File:     The policy research document [P2] confirms no GAR, no GMP, no guaranteed growth rates for the existing Aviva policy AV2075926. The LOA response [P13] for the existing plan confirms: no annuity options, no guaranteed annuity rates, no guaranteed minimum pension, 25% tax-free cash with 75% taxable flexible drawdown, no terminal bonuses, over 3,000 funds, no MVA/early transfer charges, transfers can be accepted via Origo. The pension switch report [P10] confirms no transfer penalty (£0.00). The existing plan held on the Aviva platform (pre-switch) currently has no platform listed ('None'), whereas the new solution is on the Aviva Platform [P10]. The existing plan is described as 'Pension Portfolio Pre-Retirement' in the transaction history [P14]-[P20], product type Personal Pension Plan [P5], while the new solution is a SIPP (Pension Portfolio - Choice) on the Aviva Platform [P10]. The existing plan's fund range is described as over 6,900 in the report but the LOA response states over 3,000 funds [P13]. The switch report confirms no life cover attached [P4]. No evidence in the file of any protected tax-free cash, enhanced protection, fixed protection, or other HMRC-registered protections being held. The existing plan has retirement age recorded as 60 in the switch report [P5].
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the existing plan has 'Over 6,900 funds available' but the LOA response from the existing provider states 'We have over 3000 funds with more than 110 fund managers' — a material difference in fund range disclosure.
      - The extraction path /replacementAnalysis[]/featuresOrGuaranteesLost[] is absent, meaning the report does not appear to contain an explicit section identifying features being given up; this is a disclosure component that is missing or uncaptured.
      - The existing plan has no platform ('None') per the switch report, whereas the new solution is on the Aviva Platform — the report records this difference in the existing arrangements table but does not explicitly discuss what, if anything, this means for the client in terms of features gained or lost.
    The central question is whether the report adequately discloses what the client is giving up by switching. On safeguarded benefits (GARs, GMP, guaranteed growth rates), both the report and the supporting evidence (policy research [P2], LOA response [P13]) are consistent: none exist. There is no PCLS protection, no enhanced or fixed protection referenced anywhere. The transfer penalty is nil [P5][P10]. These are the most material features to disclose on a pension switch, and the report correctly states none are present.
    
    However, there is one concrete discrepancy between the report and the file: the report records the existing plan as having 'Over 6,900 funds available' [canonical model], but the LOA response from the existing provider [P13] states 'We have over 3000 funds with more than 110 fund managers.' This is a factual inconsistency — the report overstates the existing plan's fund range. While a larger fund range on the existing plan might in principle be a reason to stay, an overstated fund range is not something that would typically prejudice the client against switching. That said, it is still a factual error about a feature of the existing plan.
    
    On the broader disclosure question: the extraction confirms that /replacementAnalysis[]/featuresOrGuaranteesLost[] is absent from the canonical model, with the extraction noting 'NotPresentInDocument' for related paths. The extraction's own confidence assessment and the absence of this section suggest the report does not contain a discrete 'features being given up' section. However, the report does document what the existing plan has in its existing arrangements table, and confirms no safeguarded features — which is the most material disclosure required on a pension switch. The switch report tool [P8][P10][P11] documents the like-for-like comparison and confirms nil effect of switching and no transfer penalty. Given that the existing plan has no material features that are genuinely being given up (no GARs, no GMP, no protected TFC, no life cover, no MVA), the absence of a discrete 'features being given up' narrative is less material than it would be where genuine features existed. The fund range discrepancy (6,900 vs 3,000) is a data quality issue but does not change the conclusion that no material protection is being lost. No vulnerability indicators are present in the file that would heighten the disclosure standard here.
