  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report discloses two layers of cost increase: (1) on a like-for-like basis (excluding adviser fee), the recommended fund strategy is 0.08% more expensive than the current plan, requiring the new plan to outperform by £63 in year one; (2) including the 1% ongoing adviser fee, total cost rises to approximately 1.62% annually versus the current 0.54%, requiring outperformance of approximately 1.08% annually. Justification offered: 'this small increase in charges is justified by the improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight.' For the adviser-fee-inclusive comparison, the report states the plan must grow at 2.94% per year to age 60 to match the existing plan's projected value, and asserts this is achievable 'given the improved risk alignment and professional oversight.' Three scenario projections are given (low 0%, mid 2.94%, high 5.88%) with values at age 60 of £74,000, £83,300 and £93,400 respectively (real terms). A performance assessment notes current funds outperformed recommended funds over 5 years but with steeper losses.
    File:     The pension switch report [P4][P10] provides projections for both existing and new solution at three growth rates. At mid (2.94%), existing = £83,300 and new solution = £83,300 (£0 / 0.0% decrease) when the adviser fee inclusion table is used [P10], and at mid (2.94%) existing = £86,800, new solution = £86,700 (£100 / 0.1% decrease) when the adviser-charge-inclusive table is used [P4]. The switch report [P5] confirms existing valuation £79,092.32, transfer penalty £0, no existing adviser charge. The switch charges assessment in the suitability report [P21] states existing recurring charge 0.54% / £427, impact +0.08% / +£63. The pension switch report does not separately state a £490 new-plan monetary charge; the annual recurring charges table for the new pension states £512.69 (as noted in the extraction's internal inconsistency). The GIA illustration [P3] confirms initial adviser charge of £4,794.37. The research report [P16] shows the recommended fund (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II) has a 5-year cumulative return of 36.21% versus 31.28% for CT Universal MAP Cautious. The pension switch report [P9] notes no benefits are lost when switching. No product literature or research document in the pack independently characterises the 1.08% required outperformance as achievable or unachievable. The justification ('improved risk alignment and professional oversight') is not corroborated by any supporting document as a standalone statement of why those benefits are worth 1.08% per annum.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report's like-for-like monetary comparison implies a new plan annual charge of £490 (£427 existing + £63 increase), but the annual recurring charges table for the new pension states £512.69 — a £22.69 difference that is not explained in the report.
      - The report states the mid-growth critical yield is 2.94% and that the recommended plan must grow at that rate to match the existing plan's projected value. The pension switch report's adviser-fee-inclusive table [P4] shows existing = £86,800 and new solution = £86,700 at mid (2.94%), a £100 / 0.1% decrease — meaning the new solution does not fully match the existing at that growth rate, which is a slightly weaker position than the report implies.
      - The report's like-for-like justification ('this small increase in charges is justified by the improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight') is a benefit assertion; no supporting document quantifies how the improved risk alignment or professional oversight translates into sufficient outperformance to cover the 1.08% annual total-cost gap including adviser fee. The justification is not connected to the 1.08% figure.
      - The report asserts achievability of 2.94% growth 'based on the past performance of your recommended funds' but the fund's 5-year cumulative return data [P16] (36.21% cumulative over 5 years, approximately 6.4% annualised) does not directly evidence 2.94% net-of-charges growth as a minimum threshold analysis — the basis for deriving 2.94% as the critical yield is stated in the switch report but the report does not explain to the client why past performance supports this specific rate.
      - The report does not explain to the client the numerical basis on which 1.08% per annum outperformance is required: it discloses the figure but does not connect it to any illustration of what that means in monetary terms at retirement age, leaving the client with a percentage but no projected monetary consequence of failing to outperform by that margin.
    The report makes two separate cost disclosures — a like-for-like product/fund comparison (0.08% / £63) and an all-in comparison including the 1% adviser fee (1.08% / 2.94% critical yield). Both are present and legible. The switch report evidence [P10] broadly corroborates the like-for-like projections (mid-growth £83,300 for both existing and new solution when charges are applied on equivalent bases). The adviser-fee-inclusive table [P4] shows a small £100 shortfall at mid-growth, consistent with a slightly negative effect of switching when the adviser charge is included — this does not contradict the report materially but does mean the 2.94% critical yield does not exactly neutralise the cost difference; the new solution is £100 less than the existing at that rate. This is a minor but real discrepancy.
    
    The more substantive issue is the quality of the justification for the 1.08% additional annual cost. The report says the increase is 'justified by the improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight.' This repeats the benefit rather than connecting it to the cost. The check guards note that a justification that repeats the benefit without connecting it to the cost is weak but is a justification — it should be graded rather than treated as absent. The justification is present but weak: the report does not illustrate what the client's projected outcome looks like if the 1.08% outperformance is not achieved (i.e. a downside scenario under the higher charge regime), and does not quantify in monetary terms at age 60 what failing to outperform means. The three-scenario projection table addresses this partially (the low-growth scenario at 0% yields £74,000 versus an unstated existing-plan low-growth value), but the report does not present the existing plan's low-growth outcome alongside the new plan's, preventing the client from seeing the monetary consequence of the higher charge at the worst case. The pension switch report [P4][P10] contains both sides but that document is a supporting document, not the suitability report itself.
    
    The £22.69 discrepancy between the implied £490 new-plan charge (£427 + £63) and the stated £512.69 in the charges table is an existing extraction-flagged internal inconsistency. It does not materially undermine the cost justification narrative but is a data quality issue.
    
    No vulnerability indicators are present in the canonical model (/vulnerability/assessment is noted as absent), so no vulnerability overlay adjustment is required.
    
    Overall: the cost disclosure is present and the justification is technically there, but it is weak — it does not quantify what the 1.08% gap means at retirement in a worst-case scenario in the suitability report itself, and the minor discrepancy in the monetary charge figures and the £100 shortfall at mid-growth in the adviser-fee-inclusive switch report projection are additional concerns. The outcome is a Potential Concern at Moderate severity.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report discloses: (1) higher like-for-like charges of 0.08% requiring £63 outperformance in year one; (2) total cost including adviser fee of 1.62% vs 0.54% requiring ~1.08% annual outperformance; (3) ISA switch variable rate risk — current Moneybox rate circa 4.5% variable, recommended funds averaged 4.72% over 5 years but past performance not guaranteed; (4) all other risks delegated to product literature; (5) beneficiary/nomination uncertainty with Expression of Wish form posted; (6) liquidity delay of 10–15 working days for access; (7) IHT change from 6 April 2027 for unused DC pensions; (8) death after 75 tax charge at beneficiary's marginal rate; (9) cancellation risk — previous provider not obliged to take plan back; (10) cancellation market loss if investment falls before notice received.
    File:     The pension switch report [P3][P5][P14][P18] shows projected maturity values at low, mid and high growth rates with effect-of-switching figures (£0 to £100 decrease, 0.0%–0.1%), reduction in yield, and critical yield. The Aviva fund factsheet [P2][P6] lists specific risk warnings A (General — value not guaranteed, can go down), B (Foreign Exchange), C (Emerging Markets), E (Fixed Interest), F (Derivatives). The CT Universal MAP KIID [P4] identifies Derivative Risk, Fixed Interest Securities Risk, Credit Risk, Smaller Companies Risk, Emerging Markets Risk and Liquidity Risk, and notes the fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw within 5 years. The Aviva Key Features [P10] states that on death before 75 benefits are normally paid tax-free, references the lump sum & death benefit allowance, and on death after 75 states benefits taxed at beneficiaries' marginal rate with a 45% charge if paid to a trust. The pension switch report [P3] states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' under Benefits lost on switching. The switch report [P5] shows new solution day one value equals existing pension £79,092.32 with no immediate capital reduction. The GIA research [P7][P12] shows risk level 4 Cautious Balanced fund selected. The personal pension research [P13][P17] shows the existing Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II performance data.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report delegates all product-specific risks beyond those it names to product literature ('All other risks associated with this investment are detailed in the product literature provided to you'), but the CT Universal MAP KIID [P4] identifies a specific suitability limitation — 'this fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years' — that is not referenced anywhere in the suitability report, despite this being a client-specific consideration given the client's retirement context and stated objectives.
      - The Aviva fund factsheet [P2][P6] identifies risk warnings including B (Foreign Exchange Risk), C (Emerging Markets), E (Fixed Interest), F (Derivatives). The report does not reference any of these fund-specific risk categories by name, relying entirely on a generic delegation to product literature. While generic market risk need not be repeated, the combination of emerging markets and derivatives risks in the recommended pension fund is product-specific and goes unmentioned.
      - The pension switch report [P3] shows that at mid growth rate the new solution projected value (£86,700) is £100 less than the existing pension (£86,800), and at high growth rate £97,300 vs £97,400 (also £100 less). The report's 'Like for Like' section discloses the £63 year-one monetary cost difference and the 0.08% charge differential, but does not explicitly show the client a projected maturity value comparison illustrating that the new plan produces a lower projected outcome at all growth rates shown in the switch report.
      - The switch report [P5] includes adviser-charge-inclusive projections showing the new solution underperforms the existing pension by £0 at low and mid growth rates and £0 at high growth rate when adviser charges are included in the comparison — this set of figures (inclusive of adviser charge) appears in the switch report but no equivalent client-facing illustration of projected outcomes including the adviser charge is cited in the suitability report's risk/disadvantage disclosures.
      - The report states the total cost including adviser fee is approximately 1.62% annually versus 0.54% for the current arrangement, but the derived figures note a monetary inconsistency: £427 + £63 = £490 implied new plan charge vs £512.69 stated in the charges table — this internal inconsistency in the monetary quantum of the cost disadvantage is not flagged to the client.
    The report makes a genuine and reasonably detailed attempt at risk and disadvantage disclosure: it covers the higher charge differential, total cost including adviser fee, ISA variable rate risk, IHT 2027 change, death-after-75 tax, liquidity delay, cancellation risks and nomination uncertainty. These are client-specific and materially complete for the core pension switch recommendation. The switch report in the file confirms no benefits are lost on switching [P3] ('No benefits were listed for the existing products'), so no omission arises there. The death-after-75 disclosure in the report aligns with the Aviva Key Features [P10] which confirms benefits are taxed at beneficiaries' marginal rate. The projected maturity value comparison is present in the switch report [P3][P5][P14] and shows minimal switching impact at the product-only level (£0–£100 decrease), which the report characterises as a £63 year-one required outperformance — this is an acceptable summary though not a full scenario illustration in the body of the report itself. The main residual concern is the CT Universal MAP KIID's explicit investor-horizon warning: 'this fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years' [P4]. This is a product-specific suitability caveat from the KIID of one of the recommended GIA/ISA funds. Given the client is recently retired and the report addresses access needs and income objectives, this is a client-relevant limitation that should appear in the report rather than being buried in product literature with only a generic delegation sentence covering it. The fund-specific risk warnings (Foreign Exchange, Emerging Markets, Derivatives) from the Aviva factsheet and CT KIID are more marginal — they are standard multi-asset fund risks and the generic delegation to product literature, while not ideal, is a known practice; the guards caution against requiring verbatim repetition of generic market risk. However the 5-year withdrawal horizon warning is a specific suitability caveat, not generic market risk. The monetary inconsistency in the charges (£490 implied vs £512.69 stated) is an internal inconsistency noted in the extraction metadata and is assessed under CHK-007; it does not constitute an undisclosed risk per se but does mean the monetary quantum of the cost disadvantage disclosed to the client may be understated. On balance, the 5-year withdrawal horizon omission from the CT Universal MAP KIID is a disclosure shortfall for a product-specific limitation, even though the overall disclosure standard is reasonable. No vulnerability-specific factors are identified in the evidence that would heighten this concern.

CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.4, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). Performance — recommended fund 5-year cumulative return: report states 30.12%; factsheet [P19] and research report [P20] state 36.21% (to 31/10/2025). These figures differ by 6.09 percentage points. The report's figure may reflect a different end-date (02/12/2025 vs 31/10/2025) but no source is cited for 30.12% and no document in the pack corroborates it. Existing fund 5-year cumulative performance: report states 45.28% (FE Analytics, to 02/12/2025); client report [P15] states 41.83% (to 23/11/2025) and research report [P17] states 36.21% combined 5-year (to 31/10/2025) — different periods and methodology, but no single document in the pack corroborates 45.28% as at 02/12/2025. Monetary new plan annual charge: switch charges assessment implies £490 (£427 + £63); annual recurring charges table for the new pension states £512.69. These differ by £22.69 and are inconsistent as noted in the extraction's internal inconsistencies. 2 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts a seven-dimension comparison: (1) Charges — existing 0.54% (0.32% platform + 0.22% fund) vs recommended 0.62% (0.21% platform + 0.41% fund), +0.08% worse, monetary difference £63 per year on existing fund of £79,092.32, requiring outperformance of £63 in year one; (2) Risk alignment — existing risk level 6 (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60%/80%) does not match agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4, recommended fund (Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II) is risk level 4, assessed as Better; (3) Fund range — both existing and recommended offer over 6,900 funds, Equivalent; (4) Performance — existing 5-year cumulative 45.28% with steeper losses/higher volatility vs recommended 30.12% with steadier performance, assessed as Worse; (5) Retirement options — existing offers full flexi-access drawdown and UFPLS, recommended offers full flexi-access drawdown (same provider retained), Equivalent; (6) Adviser servicing — existing has no TFAS servicing adviser, recommended adds TFAS with ongoing monitoring, Better; (7) Guarantees — no safeguarded features (TFC/GARs/MIG) on either side, Equivalent. The switch charges assessment shows £427 existing annual charge, +£63 impact, implying £490 new plan charge. The report also notes no transfer penalty and same-day fund value of £79,092.32 on both sides.
    File:     [P2/P3/P4/P7/P14] Pension Switch Report (G) dated 16/12/2025: confirms switch from Aviva Life & Pensions UK Limited Personal Pension Plan (no platform) to Aviva Platform SIPP. Transfer value £79,092.32, transfer penalty £0. Projected values at age 60 under low/mid/high growth show £0 or 0.1% decrease effect of switching (£0–£100 difference).     Discrepancies:
      - Performance — recommended fund 5-year cumulative return: report states 30.12%; factsheet [P19] and research report [P20] state 36.21% (to 31/10/2025).       - Existing fund 5-year cumulative performance: report states 45.28% (FE Analytics, to 02/12/2025); client report [P15] states 41.83% (to 23/11/2025) and research report [P17] states 36.21% combined 5-year (to 31/10/2025) — different periods and methodology, but no single document in the pack corroborates 45.28% as at 02/12/2025.
      - Monetary new plan annual charge: switch charges assessment implies £490 (£427 + £63); annual recurring charges table for the new pension states £512.69. These differ by £22.69 and are inconsistent as noted in the extraction's internal inconsistencies.
      - Fund range claim — over 6,900 funds available on both existing and recommended: no provider document in the pack confirms this figure for either side.
      - UFPLS availability on existing plan: report states 'Full flexi-access drawdown and UFPLS available' for the existing plan; no provider document in the pack confirms UFPLS availability.
      - Fact find policy value: [P24] records the existing plan value as £88,590.88; all other documents and the report use £79,092.32. This discrepancy is not explained in the report.
    The comparison is substantially present and well-structured. The switch report [P2–P4/P7/P14], pension projections [P8/P9/P13], research reports [P11/P17/P20] and factsheet [P19] together corroborate the charge analysis, the transfer value, the absence of transfer penalties, the risk mapping of the recommended fund to level 4, and the projected maturity values showing near-zero switching impact. The most material discrepancy is the 5-year performance figure for the recommended fund. The report states 30.12%; every document in the pack that gives a 5-year figure for the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II shows 36.21% (to 31/10/2025). No document corroborates 30.12%. The guard allows for different end-dates, and 02/12/2025 versus 31/10/2025 is a plausible explanation, but the report cites no source for 30.12% and none is on file. The consequence is material: the performance comparison is the primary justification for accepting higher charges and lower recent absolute returns; if the recommended fund's 5-year return is 36.21% rather than 30.12%, the gap with the existing fund's 45.28% narrows considerably, and the narrative that the recommended fund delivered 'steadier performance' at the cost of lower returns is partly undermined. Similarly, the report's 45.28% for the existing strategy is not directly corroborated by any single document: the client report [P15] gives 41.83% (different period/methodology), and the research report [P17] gives 36.21% for the combined new-fund performance. The monetary charge inconsistency (£490 implied vs £512.69 stated) was identified in the extraction's internal inconsistencies; it does not affect the qualitative comparison but is a disclosure accuracy concern. The fund range and UFPLS claims for the existing plan are unsupported by any document in the pack — these are minor dimensions but both are asserted without evidence. The fact-find value discrepancy (£88,590.88 vs £79,092.32) is unexplained; the switch report uses £79,092.32 consistently, suggesting a different valuation date, but this is not addressed. No safeguarded benefits issue arises: multiple documents confirm no TFC/GARs/MIG. No employer contributions are at risk. Vulnerability: no vulnerability assessment is on file (noted as absent in extraction), so no overlay adjustment can be applied. Overall, the comparison framework is in place and the charge, risk and projection analysis is corroborated, but the performance figures central to the justification for accepting higher charges are not corroborated by file documents and one (30.12%) appears to contradict the factsheet figure of 36.21%.

  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts the following in respect of the pension switch (EA1, Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd, £79,092.32): (1) No exit penalty applies. (2) Platform charge reduces from 0.32% to 0.21% as a benefit of moving to TFAS agency terms. (3) Fund switches to one aligned to risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced), replacing existing funds mapped to risk level 6. (4) Additional benefits include professional fund monitoring, adviser charging facilitation and platform integration. (5) Existing total annual recurring charge: 0.54% (£427). (6) New plan annual recurring charge: 0.62% (implied £490 = £427 + £63; alternatively stated elsewhere as £512.69). (7) Impact of switching: +0.08% / +£63 per annum. (8) The report explains higher recurring cost is justified by risk alignment, platform benefits and platform charge reduction. (9) Initial advice fee: £4,794 (3% of £159,812). (10) Out-of-market risk disclosed. (11) No guaranteed annuity rates, no guaranteed minimum pension, no MVA/ETC on existing plan per LOA.
    File:     [P3] (Pension switch report, G): Shows reduction in yield 0.6% (existing) vs 0.7% (new); critical yield 2.9% (low growth) and 5.9% (high growth); effect of switching at low/high growth = -£100 (0.1% decrease); at mid growth = £0 / 0.0% decrease. The new solution projected value at age 60 equals or comes close to existing at all growth rates, with only a £100 difference at low and high growth rates. [P5] (Pension switch report, G): Confirms fund values at point of switch: existing £79,092.32, new solution day one £79,092.32 — no immediate capital reduction. [P6] and [P11] (Personal Pension Plan review report, G): Confirms client agreed risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced); existing holdings map to volatility of 12.34 / risk level 6 (Balanced Growth) — corroborates the risk misalignment rationale. [P4] (Pension switch report, G): Shows the new solution investment (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II) and a top-10 comparison of solutions by maturity value including a generic stakeholder pension and the existing pension. [P8] (LOA, E): Confirms no annuity options, no guaranteed annuity rates, no guaranteed minimum pension, no MVA/ETC, 25% TFC available, allocation rate 100%. [P3] also shows reduction in yield for new solution is 0.7% vs existing 0.6% — i.e. the new plan has a marginally higher reduction in yield, consistent with the +0.08% charge difference. No passage in the evidence independently corroborates the 0.32% existing platform charge, the 0.21% new platform charge, the 0.54% existing total, or the 0.08%/£63 differential directly from a non-[I] source. The internal arithmetic (£427 + £63 = £490) does not equal the £512.69 stated in the annual recurring charges table for the new pension — a £22.69 discrepancy. This is noted in the extraction as an internal inconsistency. No evidence of vulnerability assessment in the file (extraction confirms /vulnerability/assessment not present in document).
    Discrepancies:
      - The switch charges assessment table implies the new plan annual charge is £490 (£427 existing + £63 impact), but the annual recurring charges table for the new Aviva Platform Personal Pension states £512.69 — a £22.69 difference that is not explained in the report.
      - The reduction in yield figures in [P3] show the new solution at 0.7% vs the existing at 0.6% at both low and high growth rates, confirming the new plan is marginally more expensive in yield terms — consistent with the +0.08% claim in direction but the figure 0.7% - 0.6% = 0.1% reduction in yield does not exactly equal the stated +0.08% charge difference.
      - The report's key stated benefit 'platform charge reduces from 0.32% to 0.21%' is not corroborated by any non-[I] document; no provider or platform documentation in the evidence independently confirms the existing 0.32% or new 0.21% platform charge rates.
      - The existing total charge of 0.54% and the £427 annual amount are not matched in any retrieved passage (nearest in [P3] is 0.6%, an approximation in the reduction-in-yield context, not a charge disclosure).
      - The internal inconsistency between implied new plan charge of £490 and stated £512.69 means the '£63 increase' figure used to characterise the cost of switching is understated relative to the charges table by £22.69, weakening the like-for-like comparison.
      - No vulnerability assessment is present in the file; it cannot be determined whether any vulnerability characteristics affect the suitability of this switch recommendation.
    The switch involves a pension transfer from an existing Aviva plan (EA1, £79,092.32) to a new Aviva Platform Personal Pension. The case for switching rests on three pillars: (1) risk realignment from level 6 to level 4; (2) a platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21%; and (3) consolidation and oversight benefits. Pillar (1) is well supported: [P6] and [P11] independently confirm the client's agreed risk level is 4 and that the existing holdings map to risk level 6, corroborating the misalignment. The pension switch report [P3] and [P4] confirm the new fund is suitable and the effect of switching at mid growth is nil. No safeguarded benefits exist: [P8] confirms no guaranteed annuity rates, no guaranteed minimum pension, no MVA/ETC. This is the most important safeguard check and it passes cleanly. Pillar (2) — the cost benefit — has a material problem. The report presents two monetary figures for the new plan's annual charge that do not reconcile: the switch charges assessment implies £490 (£427 + £63), but the annual recurring charges table states £512.69. The derived figures confirm this inconsistency (£512.69 at 0.62% implies a fund of £82,692 vs £79,092 for the ceding plan). The discrepancy of £22.69 means the stated '£63 annual cost increase' is understated; the true increase based on the charges table figure would be £85.69 (£512.69 - £427), not £63. While the percentage comparison (0.54% vs 0.62%, difference 0.08%) is internally consistent directionally, the monetary comparison used in the like-for-like disclosure is not reconciled. The reduction-in-yield comparison in [P3] (0.6% existing vs 0.7% new) shows a 0.1 percentage point difference, which is slightly wider than the 0.08% stated — this is not directly contradictory (reduction in yield and AMC are not identical concepts) but adds to the picture of imprecision in the cost comparison. Pillar (3) benefits (oversight, charging facilitation, platform integration) are stated in the report but are generic and not independently corroborated; they are qualitative and their value is not quantified. The report acknowledges the new plan is marginally more expensive on an ongoing basis and argues the benefits justify this. The justification is partially supported by the risk alignment evidence but the charge arithmetic inconsistency means the cost-of-switching disclosure cannot be taken as accurate without clarification. The guard 'higher cost is acceptable where the report explains why the benefits outweigh it' applies: the report does provide an explanation. However, the explanation relies on a monetary cost figure (£63) that does not reconcile with the charges table (£512.69 vs £427 = £85.69). This is a data inconsistency in the cost disclosure, not merely a presentation ambiguity. On the vulnerability overlay: the extraction confirms no vulnerability assessment is present in the document. No vulnerability factors are evidenced in the retrieved passages, so no specific concern arises here beyond the general note that the absence is on file.

  [G9.4] Alternatives to the switch considered
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts five alternatives were considered: Option 1 (leave untouched, rejected because current funds at risk level 6 do not match agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4; one fund at risk profile 5, the other at risk profile 7); Option 2 (leave in place and switch funds, implicitly rejected in favour of also moving to TFAS agency for reduced platform charge and ongoing oversight); Option 3 (switch into another existing arrangement, rejected because the Aviva Platform is suitable); Option 4 (switch into employer scheme, implicitly rejected because client recently retired); Option 5 (set up a new pension to move into, with the Aviva Platform SIPP recommended). All five options are listed under 'My Recommendations' and the rejection rationale for each is provided, though Options 2 and 4 rely on inferred rather than explicitly stated rejection reasons.
    File:     Passage P2 (suitability report) lists all five options and records the existing arrangement details. Passage P1 (suitability report) sets out the reasons for the internal changes recommended. Passage P8 (pension switch report, category G) confirms the switch recommendation from Aviva Life & Pensions UK Limited Current Aviva to Aviva Platform Pension Portfolio – Choice with no transfer penalty. Passage P3 (pension switch report, category G) shows a formal benefits comparison with projected values at age 60 under low/mid/high growth, reduction in yield, and critical yield for both existing and new solution; the effect of switching is stated as a decrease of £100 (0.1%) at mid and high growth and £0 at low growth. Passage P4 (pension switch report, category G) provides further projection tables and confirms the immediate fund value at switch point as £79,092.32 for both existing and new solution (no day-one loss). Passage P3 also states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' under the Benefits section (safeguarded benefits). Passage P20 (fact find, category B) records the employer pension question: employer does currently operate a pension scheme, client is not a member and answered 'Yes' to whether eligible to join; the 'when will you become eligible' field is blank. The report states the client recently retired, and Option 4 is rejected on that basis, but the fact find records a live employer scheme with potential eligibility.
    Discrepancies:
      - Option 4 (employer scheme) is rejected in the report on the basis that the client recently retired and has no employer arrangement available; however the fact find (P20) records that the employer does currently operate a pension scheme and the client answered 'Yes' to whether they are or will become eligible to join — this creates a tension between the report's stated rejection reason and the fact find evidence.
      - Option 2's rejection reason is inferred rather than explicitly stated in the report, meaning the report does not contain a direct, client-specific explanation for why an in-situ fund switch was not preferred over a full platform transfer, beyond the general rationale for moving to TFAS agency.
      - The report states the existing funds carry a blended risk level of 6 in the main body and existing arrangements table, but the Alternative Solutions section (and the canonical model) states one fund is at risk profile 5 and the other at risk profile 7 — this is internally inconsistent though potentially reconcilable as a weighted average; the alternative solutions rationale relies on the split (5 and 7) rather than the blended figure.
      - No explicit rejection reason is given for Option 4 in the report text; the rejection is entirely inferred from the client's retired status, which the fact find partially contradicts (employer scheme exists; client may be eligible).
    The report presents a structured five-option alternatives analysis with documented rejection reasons. For Options 1, 3 and 5 the rationale is explicit and evidenced: Option 1 is rejected on risk mismatch (corroborated by P2 confirming the existing funds are Vanguard LifeStrategy 60%/80% at risk level 6, inconsistent with risk profile 4); Option 3 is rejected on platform suitability (Aviva Platform confirmed adequate by P8 and P23); Option 5 is the recommended course. The switch report (P3, P4, P8) provides a formal quantitative comparison including projected maturity values, reduction in yield, critical yield and confirmation of no transfer penalty and no safeguarded benefits — this substantiates the overall switch case. The critical concern is Option 4. The report asserts the client has recently retired and has no employer scheme available, but P20 (fact find, category B) records that the employer does operate a pension scheme and the client answered 'Yes' to eligibility. The fact find does not record when eligibility arises or the current employment status in detail, and the report's narrative elsewhere confirms the client retired shortly before the advice date. There is therefore a tension: if the client is genuinely retired and the employer scheme answer is historical or residual data in the fact find system, the rejection is valid but unsupported. If the employer scheme remained live at advice date, the rejection rationale is materially deficient. This cannot be resolved on the pack alone and constitutes a potential concern. The guard on plan-specific reasoning is relevant to Options 1–3 which are assessed case-wide but are appropriate given there is only one ceding pension. Option 2's inferred rejection is a weaker justification but not a material gap given the explicit rationale for moving to TFAS agency is stated in P1. The fact find inconsistency on Option 4 is the primary finding: the report makes a factual claim (no employer scheme available) that is not corroborated and is partially contradicted by the fact find evidence.

  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) no exit penalty on the existing pension; (2) initial advice fee of £4,794 (3% of total investment values of £79,092 pension + £57,120 GIA + £20,000 ISA + £3,600 contribution); (3) the new pension costs 0.08% more per annum than the existing plan, equating to £63 per year more; (4) critical yield of 0.0% (low growth), 2.9% (mid) and 5.9% (high); (5) effect of switching is £0 or 0.0% decrease at all growth rates in the suitability report's own switch charges assessment; (6) benefits of switching include a reduced platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%, risk realignment from level 6 to level 4, professional fund monitoring, adviser charging facilitation, and platform consolidation; (7) out-of-market risk acknowledged; (8) no benefits lost on the existing product. However the monetary figures contain an internal inconsistency: the switch charges table implies the new plan costs £490/year (£427 + £63), but the annual recurring charges table for the new Aviva pension states £512.69.
    File:     The pension switch report (category G) confirms: (P6) switch from Aviva Life & Pensions UK Limited Current Aviva Personal Pension Plan (no platform) to Aviva Life & Pensions UK Limited Pension Portfolio Choice SIPP on Aviva Platform; (P2) at all three growth rates the effect of switching is £0 or 0.0% decrease, with projected values identical between existing and new solution at low and mid, and identical at high growth; critical yields confirmed as 0.0%, 2.9% and 5.9%; existing pension value at switch point £79,092.32 / new solution day one £79,092.32; (P7) no benefits listed for existing products being lost; initial adviser charge shown as £0.00 in the switch report (not £4,794); ongoing adviser charge 1.00% considered in projections; effect of switching shown as £100 or 0.1% decrease in passages P3 and P7 at mid and high growth rates (contradicting the report's £0/0.0% claim); (P1) existing fund identified as Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II, 100% invested. The switch report does not independently confirm the 0.08% charge differential or the £63 annual difference figure.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report states the effect of switching is '£0 or 0.0% decrease' at all growth rates. The pension switch report (P3, P7) shows the effect of switching as '£100 or 0.1% decrease' at mid and high growth rates, not £0/0.0%.
      - The suitability report implies new plan annual charge of £490 (£427 existing + £63 increase). The annual recurring charges table for the new pension states £512.69, a difference of £22.69 (noted in extraction as an internal inconsistency).
      - The pension switch report (P7) records the initial adviser charge applied to the new solution as £0.00, while the suitability report states the initial advice fee is £4,794. These are on different bases (the switch report excludes external advice fees from its illustration; the suitability report states the total initial advice fee charged to the client), but the switch report's projection does not incorporate the £4,794 initial fee as a reduction to the invested amount, which could affect the comparison.
      - The 0.08% annual charge differential and £63 monetary differential stated in the report are not independently confirmed by any passage in the pension switch report; the switch report shows a reduction in yield of 0.6%/0.7% for the existing plan versus 0.7%/0.7% for the new solution, implying a modest but not precisely quantified differential.
      - The suitability report risk alignment benefit claims existing funds are at risk level 6; the internal inconsistency noted in the extraction states the alternative solutions section describes one fund at risk level 5 and one at risk level 7, which is inconsistent with the single '6' used in the comparison.
    The primary finding is a direct contradiction between the suitability report and the pension switch report on the effect of switching. The suitability report states the effect of switching is '£0 or 0.0% decrease' at all growth rates. However passages P3 and P7 from the switch report show the effect of switching as '£100 or 0.1% decrease' at both mid and high growth rates. This is not a basis difference: both documents purport to state the change in maturity value attributable to the switch. The discrepancy is small in absolute terms but it means the report has either used a different scenario or misstated the switch impact figure. On the core question — do switching costs exceed benefits — the switch report does corroborate that the effect of switching is either nil or a very small reduction (£100 on a £79,000+ fund), and the critical yields (0.0%, 2.9%, 5.9%) are corroborated by P2. The non-financial benefits (risk realignment from level 6 to level 4, platform consolidation, professional oversight) are asserted in the report and the guard permits non-financial benefits to justify a net cost increase where argued explicitly, which the report does. The report does argue these explicitly. The £4,794 initial fee discrepancy with the switch report's £0.00 adviser charge reflects a difference in scope: the switch report illustration excludes the adviser fee from its switching impact calculation (P7 states 'Initial adviser charge of £0.00 which is taken from the new solution' — this appears to be the switch-report tool's field, not the actual charge; the actual charge is deducted separately). However this means the switch report projections do not fully reflect the impact of the £4,794 initial fee on the comparison, which is a disclosure gap. The internal inconsistency between £490 (implied) and £512.69 (stated) for the new plan annual charge is a data quality concern but not decisive on its own. The vulnerability assessment is noted as absent from extraction; no vulnerability-related factors are identified in the passages that would materially alter this assessment. Overall: the comparison is present and broadly supports the recommendation, but the £0/0.0% vs £100/0.1% discrepancy between the suitability report and the switch tool output is a genuine contradiction that requires explanation, and the omission of the £4,794 initial fee from the switch tool's projection means the full cost impact is not captured in the comparison analysis.

  [G9.6] Lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report states that there are no exit penalties or switching costs on the existing arrangement (EA1, Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd, £79,092.32): 'Penalties/Cost of exit/Switching: None'. The canonical model does not contain an extracted value for /existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp, and the extraction metadata records this path as absent — meaning the report is silent on whether safeguarded benefits exist or are being given up, rather than stating there are none.
    File:     [P12] (category E, LOA response from Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd) explicitly records the plan details and states: 'No annuity options', 'No guaranteed annuity rates', 'No guaranteed minimum pension', '25% Tax free cash, 75% Taxable flexible drawdown available', 'No Terminal bonuses', and 'There is no Market Value Adjustment/ Early Transfer Charges applicable to this plan.' [P6] (category G, pension switch report) confirms: 'Transfer penalty: £0.00' and 'Subject to an existing advice charge: No'. [P1] (category G, pension switch report) shows the effect of switching as '£0 or 0.0% decrease' across all growth scenarios, with the existing fund value and new solution day-one value both recorded as £79,092.32. The provider documentation in [P12] therefore evidences that no guaranteed annuity rates, no guaranteed minimum pension, no terminal bonuses and no MVA/early transfer charges apply to the ceding plan.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report does not contain an explicit statement that no safeguarded benefits exist or are being given up — the canonical model has no extracted value at /existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp. However, the provider's LOA response (P12) records no guaranteed annuity rates, no guaranteed minimum pension, and no terminal bonuses, which evidences the absence of safeguarded benefits even though the report itself does not make this assertion explicitly.
      - The report addresses exit penalties (stating 'None') but does not separately address safeguarded or guaranteed benefits as a disclosure item in its own right.
    The guard for this requirement states it only applies where G9.3 identified a benefit actually being given up. The provider documentation at [P12] (an E-category document, the LOA reply from Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd) explicitly records 'No guaranteed annuity rates', 'No guaranteed minimum pension', 'No Terminal bonuses', and 'There is no Market Value Adjustment/Early Transfer Charges applicable to this plan.' This constitutes file-level evidence that no safeguarded benefits exist on the ceding plan. The switch report at [P6] confirms a transfer penalty of £0.00, and [P1] confirms the immediate effect of switching is £0 reduction in fund value. The absence of safeguarded benefits is therefore evidenced by the case file even though the suitability report itself does not contain an explicit narrative statement to that effect. Because the file holds provider confirmation that no such benefits exist, the guard condition is not triggered — there is nothing being given up. The omission from the report's own narrative is a minor disclosure shortfall (the report addresses penalties but does not separately state that no guarantees or safeguarded benefits are being surrendered), but the underlying position is evidenced and the recommendation is not contradicted. No vulnerability factors are identified in the file that would alter this assessment; the extraction notes /vulnerability/assessment as absent from the report, but no vulnerability characteristics are recorded in any retrieved passage that would affect the safeguarded benefits analysis.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report considered six alternatives to the recommended pension switch (internal change within the Aviva Platform to TFAS agency terms with fund switch): Option 1 (leave untouched) — rejected because current funds at risk level 6 do not match agreed Cautious Balanced profile 4; Option 2 (leave in place, switch funds only) — rejected in favour of also moving to TFAS agency to reduce platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%; Option 3 (switch into another existing pension arrangement) — rejected because the Aviva Platform is suitable and no reason to move to a different provider; Option 4 (switch into employer's pension) — rejected because client has recently retired and no employer scheme is available; Option 5 (set up a new pension plan) — rejected because Aviva Platform is excellent and retaining it avoids unnecessary transfer costs; Option 6 (do nothing overall) — implied rejection on same risk-mismatch grounds. The recommendation is to retain the existing Aviva pension on the Aviva Platform but move it to TFAS agency terms (reducing platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%), switch underlying funds from Vanguard LifeStrategy 60%/80% to the Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II aligned to risk level 4, and add a £3,600 gross contribution. The report states the switch costs 0.08% more per annum (£63 in year one) but is justified by risk alignment, lower platform charge, ongoing oversight and platform integration benefits. The report explicitly identifies no lost benefits or guarantees for the ceding plan. The pension switch charges assessment shows the effect of switching as £0 at low and mid growth rates and negligible at high growth rates when the analysis excludes the adviser charge; projections show equivalent or near-equivalent maturity values between existing and new solution.
    File:     The pension switch report [P1][P2][P6][P9][P11] confirms: (a) existing pension is Aviva Life & Pensions UK Limited (AV2075926-001), invested in Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II with 100% allocation; (b) a like-for-like projection shows existing and new solution at identical projected values at the low and mid growth rates (£74,000 / £83,300 at low/mid for one set, £77,100 / £86,800 at mid for the full existing-only set) and a £100 / 0.1% decrease at mid and high growth rates in the comparison table; (c) critical yield for the new solution is 0.0% at low, 2.9% at mid, and 5.9% at high; (d) 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' under the Benefits section [P6]; (e) no transfer penalties apply — day-one fund value equals existing value (£79,092.32 = £79,092.32) [P2]; (f) the pension switching research report [P16] confirms agreed risk level 4 Cautious Balanced and that risk-rated investments at level 4 were selected; (g) the personal pension plan review [P14] shows the existing Vanguard LifeStrategy funds' performance data; (h) policy research [P15][P19][P22] shows no with-profits investment (no MVR), no life cover attached, plan moveable via Origo, flexi-access drawdown available; (i) the pension switching research report ranks solutions by maturity value and the recommended solution (Aviva Platform Personal Pension) was selected from a shortlist [P1]; (j) the fact find [P24] records the policy as 'Not Under Agency' at time of fact find, corroborating the agency-move rationale; (k) one passage [P24] notes the employer 'currently operates a pension scheme' and the client is not a member but may become eligible — this potentially contradicts the report's assertion that Option 4 is not applicable because the client has retired. However, this appears to be a data entry artefact in the fact find (employer scheme section may relate to Yvette, the spouse/partner) rather than Anthony Barnes himself, and the report consistently describes Anthony as recently retired. The research report does not provide a standalone rejection rationale for Option 2 (in-situ fund switch only) beyond the inference that the agency move makes the full recommendation preferable; the rejection reason is inferred rather than explicitly stated in a separate section. No explicit comparator analysis between Option 2 and the recommended route is quantified.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the existing pension holds Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% funds, while the pension switch report [P1] shows the existing pension invested 100% in 'Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II (GBP)' — suggesting the switch report was run against the post-switch fund, or there is a mismatch between what the switch report modelled and what the report describes as the existing holding. The personal pension plan review [P14] shows existing holdings as Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80%, consistent with the suitability report, so the switch report's existing fund table appears to use the wrong fund for the existing pension.
      - The report's rejection of Option 2 (in-situ fund switch only) is inferred and not explicitly quantified: the report does not provide a monetary comparison showing what the client would pay under Option 2 versus the recommended route, making it impossible to verify that the recommended route is materially better than Option 2.
      - The fact find [P24] records that the employer currently operates a pension scheme and the client may become eligible, which is potentially inconsistent with the report's assertion that Option 4 (employer scheme) is not applicable because the client has retired. This may be a data entry issue relating to Yvette rather than Anthony.
      - The pension switch comparison table in [P6] shows a £100 / 0.1% decrease in projected maturity value at mid and high growth rates, while the report's narrative states the switch costs only £63 more per year and is justified — the switch report's own comparison shows a small projected disadvantage at mid and high growth rates, which the report does not address.
      - The report asserts that existing funds carry risk level 6 (overall) and that 'one fund is aligned to risk profile 5 and the other to risk profile 7' — this internal inconsistency (noted in the extraction) means the rejection rationale for Option 1 oscillates between a blended risk level of 6 and individual fund risk levels of 5 and 7, potentially weakening the stated mismatch with risk level 4.
    The report considers six alternatives and provides specific rejection reasons for each. The primary rationale — risk misalignment (existing funds at risk level 6 against agreed profile of 4) and the cost reduction available from the agency move — is corroborated by the pension switching research report [P16] confirming risk level 4 and the suitability report [P5] confirming the platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21%. The benefits section of the pension switch report [P6] explicitly records no benefits lost on switching, and no transfer penalties apply [P2]. These are the highest-severity concerns (safeguarded benefits and transfer penalties) and both are addressed and supported by evidence.
    
    However, three issues require noting. First, the pension switch report [P1] models the existing pension as 100% invested in the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II, yet the personal pension plan review [P14] and the suitability report consistently describe the existing holdings as Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80%. This discrepancy means the like-for-like projections in [P2] and [P6] may not have used the correct existing fund, undermining the reliability of the switching comparison. The switch report's projection comparison showing £0 effect of switching at low growth and only £100 disadvantage at mid/high growth may be based on incorrect existing fund assumptions. Second, the rejection of Option 2 (in-situ fund switch) is inferred rather than explicitly stated and is not quantified. The report lists the agency move as reducing the platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21% (saving 0.11% = approximately £87 per annum on the current fund value), but the recommended fund costs 0.08% more than the existing fund, giving a net saving of approximately 0.03%. Option 2 would achieve the risk-alignment benefit without incurring the higher fund charge, and the report does not explicitly quantify why the full agency move is preferable to Option 2 alone. This is a weak justification for discarding Option 2. Third, the pension switch research report [P13][P16] shows the recommended solution was selected and ranked within the Defaqto system, but the specific ranking comparison (whether other solutions ranked higher by maturity value) is not reproduced in the suitability report, and no explanation is given if a higher-ranked solution was available.
    
    The fact find employer pension entry [P24] records 'Does your employer currently operate a pension scheme: Yes' and the client is not a member but may become eligible. The report treats Option 4 as not applicable because the client has retired. This inconsistency is unlikely to be material — the suitability report consistently describes Anthony as recently retired, and the fact find entry almost certainly relates to Yvette's employment — but it remains unexplained. On vulnerability: no vulnerability assessment is present in the file (noted as absent in the extraction), and the report does not flag any vulnerability characteristics that would affect this analysis. No adjustment to the finding is therefore required on vulnerability grounds.
    
    Overall, the safeguarded benefits and penalty analysis is adequately addressed and evidenced. The main concern is the weak, inferred justification for discarding Option 2 without quantification, and the potential that the switch comparison projections were run against the wrong existing fund — which would undermine the evidential basis for the '£0 effect of switching' claim.

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report acknowledges the recommended pension plan costs 0.08% (£63) more per year than the existing plan on a like-for-like basis. It states this higher cost is justified by: (1) risk alignment — switching funds currently at risk level 6 to a fund designed for risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced); (2) ongoing professional oversight and fund monitoring; (3) adviser charging facilitation; (4) platform integration (pension, ISA, GIA all on Aviva Platform); (5) a lower platform charge of 0.21% vs 0.32% (partially offsetting the higher fund cost). Including the 1% adviser fee, total cost rises to 1.62% vs 0.54% currently, requiring 1.08% annual outperformance. The critical yield at mid-growth (2.94%) is stated as achievable. No exit penalty applies. Initial advice fee is £4,794.
    File:     The pension switch report [P1][P2][P4][P5] confirms: existing plan transfer value £79,092.32, no transfer penalty, retirement age 60. Projected values at all three growth rates show identical maturity values for existing and new solution (e.g. mid-growth: both £83,300), with effect of switching shown as £0 or 0.0% decrease at mid and low growth, £100 or 0.1% decrease at high growth in [P2], and £100 or 0.1% decrease at all growth rates in [P5]. Critical yield at mid-growth is 2.9% per the switch report [P2]. The existing plan's fund is Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II [P3]. The policy research [P10] confirms no life cover attached, can be moved by Origo, no penalties. Pension projection [P9] confirms existing fund OCF/TER 0.22% (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80%). The GIA research [P11] confirms agreed risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced). The performance analysis in the suitability report [P17] (category I) shows the existing Vanguard funds have outperformed the recommended fund over 5 years (45.28% vs 30.12%) but with higher volatility. The switch report [P2][P5] shows that under the same growth assumptions the new solution produces the same or slightly lower projected values than the existing plan, meaning the benefit of switching is not a higher projected fund value but rather risk alignment. There is no external evidence (category E or G) corroborating the claim that ongoing professional oversight or adviser charging facilitation are benefits unavailable on the existing plan.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the benefit of 'lower platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%' as part of the net benefit justification. However, the net-of-charges comparison shows the new solution costs 0.08% more overall (0.62% vs 0.54%), so the lower platform charge is more than offset by the higher fund charge (0.41% vs 0.22%). The report's 'lower costs' claim in the overall net benefit conclusion is therefore misleading when the total recurring charge increases.
      - The switch report [P2] shows the effect of switching at mid-growth as '£0 or 0.0% decrease', yet [P5] shows '£100 or 0.1% decrease' at equivalent growth rates. These two passages appear to present different versions of the same comparison (possibly with and without the new £3,600 contribution), but the basis is not clearly stated in either passage, creating an unexplained difference in the quantified effect of switching.
      - The report's net benefit conclusion states the plan provides 'lower costs' as a primary benefit, but the like-for-like charge analysis in the same report shows costs are higher by 0.08% (£63). No external evidence corroborates claims that professional oversight and adviser charging facilitation are benefits not already available or accessible on the existing plan.
      - The 5-year performance data [P17] shows the existing Vanguard funds returned 45.28% vs 30.12% for the recommended fund — the report acknowledges current funds have outperformed but attributes this to higher risk. The risk misalignment justification is substantiated by the agreed risk level 4 in [P11] and the volatility analysis, but no independent evidence (category E or G) is cited to confirm the existing plan cannot accommodate a lower-risk fund switch in situ, which would achieve risk alignment at lower cost.
      - The report claims 'lower costs' as a net benefit in the OverallNetBenefit conclusion, but the charges comparison confirms the new arrangement is more expensive (0.08%/£63 p.a. more on a like-for-like basis, and 1.08% more including adviser fee). This is an internal inconsistency between the stated conclusion and the stated charge differential.
    The report justifies higher ongoing costs (0.08%/£63 p.a. like-for-like; 1.08% including adviser fee) primarily on two grounds: risk alignment and professional oversight. The risk alignment argument is the stronger of the two: [P11] confirms the agreed risk level is 4 (Cautious Balanced), [P9] confirms the existing funds are Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% Equity carrying a blended risk level of 6, and [P17] (category I, so internal) analyses performance and volatility showing the existing funds carry higher risk than the agreed profile. The switch report [P2][P4] corroborates that no transfer penalty applies and projected values at mid-growth are equal (£83,300 both sides), with critical yield of 2.9% at mid-growth consistent with the report's stated 2.94%. So far as risk alignment is concerned, the external evidence in categories G and H is reasonably supportive. However, a material gap exists: there is no evidence in the file that an in-situ fund switch within the existing Aviva plan to a risk level 4 fund was considered and ruled out. If the client's existing Aviva plan could accommodate a lower-risk fund at comparable or lower cost, the transfer (with its higher ongoing charges) would not be necessary to achieve risk alignment. The 'alternative options' guard in the check's decision logic specifically flags this. The 'lower costs' benefit stated in the report's OverallNetBenefit conclusion is directly contradicted by the report's own charge analysis (0.08% more expensive), which is itself an internal inconsistency — the platform charge saving of 0.11% is more than offset by the higher fund charge. The claim that professional oversight and adviser charging facilitation are new benefits is unsubstantiated by any external evidence; these are features of the new adviser relationship rather than features of the new product per se. The discrepancy between [P2] (£0 / 0.0% effect of switching) and [P5] (£100 / 0.1% decrease) is likely attributable to the £3,600 new contribution being included in one scenario and not the other, but the basis is not stated in either passage, so it cannot be resolved with certainty. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability assessment is present in the pack (noted as absent in the extraction metadata), so no adjustment to this finding is warranted on that basis. Overall, the risk alignment justification has reasonable external corroboration but the absence of evidence that an in-situ fund switch was considered, combined with the misleading 'lower costs' conclusion and the unsubstantiated professional oversight benefit, amounts to a weak and partially contradicted justification for the cost increase.

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) moving to TFAS agency reduces the platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%; (2) the like-for-like recurring annual charge is 0.08% (£63) higher in the new plan, justified by improved risk alignment and professional oversight; (3) including the 1% adviser fee, total cost is ~1.62% vs 0.54% currently, requiring ~1.08% annual outperformance; (4) critical yield based on mid-growth rate of 2.94%; (5) existing funds carry risk level 6, too high for agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4; (6) no safeguarded benefits (GARs, GMP, guaranteed growth rates) exist; (7) no transfer penalties apply; (8) no employer contributions are being lost; (9) the recommended Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II provides more consistent performance with lower volatility; (10) the overall net benefit includes lower costs, appropriate risk alignment, ongoing oversight, and tax-efficient growth; (11) the switch charges table shows existing annual charge £427 at 0.54% and impact of +£63, implying new plan charge of £490; (12) the annual recurring charges table for the new pension states £512.69 total.
    File:     [P2] policy research confirms: no GARs, no GMP, no guaranteed growth rates for AV2075926. [P13] LOA response confirms: no guaranteed annuity rates, no guaranteed minimum pension, no MVA/early transfer charges, allocation rate 100%. [P14] policy research confirms: no exit penalties. [P4] pension switch report confirms: transfer value £79,092.32, transfer penalty £0.00, existing platform charge 0.32%. [P7] pension switch report comparison table shows: at mid-growth rate (2.94%), existing pension projected value at age 60 = £83,300, new solution = £83,300, effect of switching = £0 or 0.0% decrease, critical yield = 2.9%, reduction in yield existing = 1.7%, new solution = 1.7%. [P3] pension switch report recommends switching from Aviva Life & Pensions UK Limited Personal Pension Plan (no platform) to Aviva Platform SIPP. [P11] policy research confirms fund value and transfer value both £79,092.32, no current contributions. [P15] client notes confirm Aviva appears to be only pension and no contributions made. [P1][P6] policy research confirms no with-profits, flexi-access drawdown available. The pension switch report at [P7] shows the effect of switching as £0/0.0% decrease across all growth rates, meaning the switch has no monetary disadvantage in the illustration — but this equality arises because the same charges appear to have been applied to both sides of the comparison (the existing plan's 0.32% platform charge is confirmed in [P4], yet the new solution at 0.62% total would be higher). The switch charges assessment referenced in the report (£427 existing, +£63 impact = £490 implied new plan charge) conflicts with the stated new plan charge of £512.69 in the annual recurring charges table. The internal inconsistency noted in the extraction confirms: implied new plan charge £490 vs stated £512.69.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report's switch charges table implies a new plan annual charge of £490 (£427 + £63), but the annual recurring charges table for the new Aviva Platform Personal Pension states £512.69 — a difference of £22.69. This is an internal inconsistency in the report corroborated by the derived figures.
      - The report claims the recommended plan will benefit the client from 'lower costs' as an overall net benefit, but the like-for-like section simultaneously acknowledges the new plan is 0.08% (£63) more expensive on a recurring basis — these two claims are in tension within the report itself.
      - The report states the existing funds carry risk level 6, but the internal inconsistency noted in extraction indicates the alternative solutions section of the report states one fund is risk level 5 and the other risk level 7, contradicting the single figure of 6 used throughout the main recommendation. Evidence from the file does not resolve which is more accurate.
      - The pension switch report [P7] shows the effect of switching as £0 or 0.0% decrease at all three growth rates, implying no monetary disadvantage. However, the report's own charge analysis shows the new plan is more expensive (0.62% vs 0.54%), which should produce a negative effect of switching unless the illustration applied identical or matched charges to both sides. The basis on which both sides show equal projected values is not explained.
      - The derived figures show the annual charge difference of £63 at 0.08% implies a fund value of £78,750, whereas the actual fund value is £79,092.32 — a minor arithmetic inconsistency in the charge differential calculation.
      - The derived figures show the new plan total ongoing of £512.69 at 0.62% implies a fund value of £82,691.94, which matches neither the existing plan value of £79,092.32 nor any other arrangement value, yet the switch is of the £79,092.32 pension — suggesting the new plan charge illustration may be based on a projected or different fund value rather than the transfer value, but this basis is not disclosed in the report.
    The replacement rationale is substantially supported by the file. Key planks of the rationale — no safeguarded benefits (confirmed by [P2], [P13]), no transfer penalties ([P4], [P13], [P14]), no current contributions ([P11], [P15]), transfer value of £79,092.32 ([P4], [P11]), and a formal pension switching report with a side-by-side comparison ([P3], [P7]) — are all corroborated by categories E and G. The risk alignment rationale (existing risk level 6 vs agreed profile of 4) is stated in the report but the file does not independently corroborate the client's agreed ATR of 4 through a separate ATR assessment document in these passages; [P15] notes 'Only ATR 4', which is consistent with but does not formally corroborate the suitability report's stated risk profile. Two concerns remain. First, there is a genuine internal monetary inconsistency: the switch charges table implies a new plan charge of £490/year (£427 + £63) while the annual recurring charges table states £512.69 — a £22.69 discrepancy confirmed by the derived figures. This means the 'outperform by £63' figure in the like-for-like section is understated relative to the full charges shown elsewhere. The basis difference guard is relevant here: the £490 figure may relate to the existing plan's fund value (£79,092.32) whereas the £512.69 may be based on a projected fund value at some future point, but the report does not state this, so it is an unexplained inconsistency rather than an explained different basis. Second, the pension switch illustration [P7] shows identical projected values and a 0.0% effect of switching at all growth rates, which is surprising given the acknowledged higher charges for the new plan. This equality is not explained and may indicate charges were applied identically on both sides of the comparison, potentially understating the cost disadvantage to the client. The claim that the switch produces 'lower costs' as a net benefit headline is directly contradicted by the like-for-like section's own acknowledgement that recurring charges are higher. The 'lower costs' claim appears to refer only to the platform charge reduction (0.32% to 0.21%) while ignoring that the new fund charge (0.41%) is higher than the existing fund charge (0.22%), resulting in a net increase. This is partially disclosed but the headline claim creates a misleading impression. No vulnerability indicators are identified in the passages that would materially alter this assessment. The overall picture is a replacement rationale that is broadly evidenced but contains a monetary inconsistency in the charge differential and a potentially misleading 'lower costs' headline, both of which are Potential Concerns.

  [G9.10] Material replacement-related information omitted from the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) employerContributionsLost = false; (2) exit penalties = none, stated as 'Penalties/Cost of exit/Switching: None'; (3) the replacement is an internal platform switch from the existing Aviva pension (AV2075926-001, value £79,092.32) to a new Aviva Platform Personal Pension, with total annual charge rising from 0.54% (£427) to 0.62% (£512.69), a difference of +0.08% (£63 per year in year one); (4) the report states the recommended fund strategy is 0.08% more expensive and the plan must outperform by £63 in year one; (5) no features or guarantees lost are mentioned; (6) the extraction notes '/replacementAnalysis[]/featuresOrGuaranteesLost[]' was absent from the model.
    File:     P1 (Fact Find) records: employer pension scheme exists, client is not a member but is or may become eligible to join — no employer contributions are currently being made to the ceding plan. The transaction history (P2–P9, P12–P13, P15–P16) shows regular Aviva platform charges and monthly fund sales (to meet charges) but no employer contribution entries at any point, consistent with no active employer contributions. P14 confirms the ceding plan closing value of £79,092.32 and 34.02% return over 3 years. P11 (LOA) is a bare plan information form with no guarantees or safeguarded benefit declaration. P15 (AV2075926) shows an Aviva charge entry only, no guaranteed annuity rate or safeguarded benefit entry. P18 (Aviva Pension Illustration) shows the new plan uses Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II S14 at 0.21% platform + investment charge, consistent with report. P19 (pension projection) projects fund to £99,900 at age 67 after charges. No document in the pack identifies any guaranteed annuity rate, protected tax-free cash, defined benefit safeguarded benefit, or other valuable feature attached to the ceding plan. No document confirms or denies the absence of such features — the LOA (P11) is blank on this point. The monetary charge inconsistency identified in the extraction persists: the switch charges table implies the new plan charge is £490 (£427 + £63), but the annual recurring charges table states £512.69. The report's own figures therefore produce two different values for the new plan annual charge.
    Discrepancies:
      - The switch charges assessment table implies new plan annual charge of £490 (£427 existing + £63 impact), but the annual recurring charges table states the new plan's total annual charge as £512.69 — a £22.69 difference within the report's own numbers.
      - The 'difference' percentage of 0.08% at £63 implies a fund value of £78,750, while the solution total of £512.69 at 0.62% implies a fund value of £82,692 — the two charge rows are calculated on materially different fund value bases, making the comparison table internally inconsistent.
      - No document in the pack (including the LOA at P11) confirms the absence of safeguarded benefits, guaranteed annuity rates or other valuable features in the ceding plan. The report makes no mention of features or guarantees lost ('/replacementAnalysis[]/featuresOrGuaranteesLost[]' absent from model), and the supporting file contains no provider confirmation that none exist.
      - The fact find (P1) records the client is eligible (or may become eligible) to join an employer pension scheme, yet the report asserts employer contributions lost = false without any narrative addressing this potential eligibility — although the transaction history shows no employer contributions have ever been made to the ceding plan.
    Four issues arise. First, the monetary charge comparison table is internally inconsistent: the switch charges table shows £427 existing + £63 impact = £490 implied new plan charge, but the annual recurring charges table for the new Aviva Platform Personal Pension states £512.69. The derived figures confirm both: £427 at 0.54% and £512.69 at 0.62% each back-calculate to different fund values (£79,074 vs £82,692), meaning the two rows are calculated on different assumed fund values — likely the transfer value before and after the £3,600 gross contribution. The report does not explain this basis difference, and the 'Like for Like' comparison section uses the lower £63/£490 figure without disclosing that the new plan charge is actually higher. This is a disclosure shortfall on the cost comparison. Second, and more materially, no document in the pack positively confirms the absence of safeguarded benefits, guaranteed annuity rates or other valuable features in the ceding plan. The LOA (P11) is blank. The transaction history shows only platform charges and no protected benefit entries, and the Aviva charge document (P15) shows only charges. This is absence of evidence rather than evidence of absence. Under COBS 9A.2.18R and the severity guidance ('Highest: safeguarded benefits given up with no analysis, or no provider documentation confirming none exist'), the lack of any provider document confirming no safeguarded benefits exist in AV2075926-001 is a material gap. The report makes no mention of the features/guarantees question at all. Third, the employer contribution point: the fact find records eligibility to join a workplace scheme (P1), but the transaction history contains no employer contribution entries across the full period 2016–2025. The report's assertion that employer contributions lost = false is consistent with the evidence, but the report contains no narrative acknowledging the eligibility question, which would be good practice though not strictly a failing given the evidence of no active contributions. Fourth, the exit penalty assertion ('None') is stated in the report and is not contradicted by any file document — the transaction history shows no exit penalty charges. This element is therefore consistent. The guard regarding nil exit penalty confirmed by the provider applies: there is no provider document confirming nil penalties, but the transaction history corroborates the report's assertion indirectly. Overall the most significant concern is the absence of provider documentation or adviser assessment confirming the ceding plan holds no safeguarded benefits, combined with the charge comparison inconsistency. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability assessment is recorded in the file (extraction notes '/vulnerability/assessment' absent), so no specific vulnerability factors can be applied to modify this finding.

CHK-010 — Vulnerability, Support Needs and Foreseeable Harm Overlay
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 5 requirement(s). 4 raised a concern (G10.1, G10.3, G10.4, G10.5). The report asserts a registered LPA covering financial affairs and health and welfare but no corroborating file evidence (e.g., a sight note, copy, or fact-find entry confirming its registration) appears in the retrieved passages. The report contains no vulnerability assessment section and the extraction confirms /vulnerability/assessment is NotPresentInDocument; no equivalent assessment document appears in the file either. The client has self-reported 'very little understanding or knowledge' of pension planning and 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares — FG21/1 low-capability indicators — but neither the report nor any file document records any support measures, comprehension adjustments, or follow-up steps taken in response to this. 1 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G10.1] Details of additional support and needs, including third-party support
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report states that the client 'confirm[s] you have a registered Power of Attorney covering your financial affairs and health and welfare.'     Discrepancies:
